Avanza · AZA-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Avanza memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Avanza was written: 51,915 words, against 11,113 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 21,912 words · written 23 October 2026, 03:09 to 03:29 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Avanza (AZA-SE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Investment Analyst, 2026-10-22. Written for the Analyst building the 18-month to four-year operating and financial model. All figures are from the files; SEK unless stated. The Q3 2026 results of 16 October 2026 post-date the FactSet consensus (13 October) and the latest memo (22 September), so where the two disagree the Q3 print is taken as the current state and the consensus as the pre-Q3 state.

A note on the base the model must start from

Three things the Consumer should fix before modelling anything else, because every answer below leans on them.

  1. The company's own income statement, not FactSet's, is the base. Operating income FY2025 SEK 4,495m (FactSet's "revenue" of SEK 5,737m includes gross interest and does not reconcile; the quirks digest says do not use it). Operating profit SEK 3,078m, margin 68%, EPS SEK 16.57, tax 14.5%, 157.8m shares, DPS SEK 12.75 (76% payout).
  2. The revenue engine is four lines on one base. Savings capital (SEK 1,277bn at 30 September 2026, +21% year on year) times a revenue margin that was 45 basis points in FY2025, 47 in Q1 2026, about 45 in Q2 and about 41 in Q3 (operating income SEK 1,283m annualised over roughly SEK 1,240bn of average capital). The 2025 mix was net interest income 33%, brokerage 28%, fund commissions 17%, currency 16%, other 6%. The cost line is guided in one number (9% Swedish growth in 2026 plus SEK 40m for Denmark; 8% average to 2030, 5% by 2030) and is 60% personnel, 15% IT, 7% external services.
  3. The Q3 2026 print changed the question. Operating income SEK 1,283m was 2% below the 18-broker consensus of SEK 1,310m, with every core revenue line below (brokerage -5%, currency -8%, fund -3%, net interest -2%) and only costs (SEK 362m, 3% better, seasonal) offsetting. Savings capital, inflows (SEK 23.1bn, +61%) and fund purchases (a record SEK 18bn, 32% of the Swedish market) were strong; monetisation per unit fell (brokerage yield 10.3 basis points from 11.0, fund margin 22.2 from 22.9, income per customer SEK 2,170, -6% quarter on quarter). The structural franchise case is intact; the earnings-conversion case now needs proof.

External change forces

C5710 questions
C-0162C-0242C-0352C-0455C-0545C-0655C-0752C-0865C-0950C-1058

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external forces have turned from headwind to a mixed tailwind in the way that helps a model least: the improvement is in the slow, structural lines (savings capital, fund flows, deposits internalised, a rate hike in prospect) and the deterioration is in the fast, cyclical lines (trading velocity, currency turnover) and in yield per unit, where Nordnet, niche entrants and passive mix are each taking a little. The company's demand cycle is past its transactional peak and the regime is risk-off. For the Consumer: model savings capital compounding at 10% to 12% a year with a revenue margin drifting from about 45 to about 42 basis points over three years, a rate hike worth 4% to 7% of pre-tax profit from 2027, and a transactional line that normalises 5% to 15% below the H1 2026 run rate before it grows again. The external picture supports a 10% to 12% EPS growth path and does not support the H1 2026 extrapolation that consensus still partly carries.

Horizon: the last six months and the next six, against the last twelve to eighteen.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical, and with what visibility?

C62

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement. End-market demand has two legs that must be modelled separately, because they moved in opposite directions in Q3 2026. The asset-gathering leg (savings flows onto the platform) is structural, strong and visible for two-plus years. The activity leg (how often and how richly customers trade) is cyclical, peaked in H1 2026, turned down in Q3 and has less than a year of visibility.

The asset leg. Net inflows were SEK 18.9bn in Q2 and SEK 23.1bn in Q3 2026 (+61% year on year); mutual-fund purchases set records in both quarters (SEK 17bn, then SEK 18bn, a 32% share of all Swedish fund inflows); recurring monthly inflows run at SEK 3.4bn plus SEK 410m of pension premiums; two-thirds of Q3 inflows came from existing customers. Behind it sit structural facts: Swedish households save 8% to 12% of income, hold 44% to 49% of financial assets in equities and funds, the ISK tax-free threshold doubled to SEK 300,000 in January 2026, the market has grown 7% a year for a decade, and Avanza's customers keep only about one-third of their wealth with it. This leg is visible for the modelling horizon.

The activity leg. Transactions fell 8% quarter on quarter in Q3 and brokerage-generating notes 14%, with trades per user down about 25% from Q2 on DNB Carnegie's reading; Deutsche Bank's September tracker shows Avanza's total trades down 10% year on year against 5% at Nordnet and 3% at flatexDEGIRO. Transaction-related income was 44% of Q1 2026 income. The Wisdom Library's Avanza/Covid "lazy cyclical extrapolation" archetype applies directly: in 2021 to 2022 EPS fell from SEK 13.19 to 10.69 when activity normalised.

The change in trend. Against twelve to eighteen months ago, the asset leg is stronger (inflow share 11.9% rolling, up from 10%) and the activity leg has gone from rising to falling. The macro regime (Fitness for Fighting: risk-off, a discount-rate shock, Sweden's tilt cut from Overweight to Neutral, a Riksbank hike likely on 4 November into one of the most rate-sensitive economies in the developed world) skews the next six months' activity down further, while the two-plus-year asset leg is largely unaffected. One warning for the model: 21% savings-capital growth includes market appreciation; DNB Carnegie puts the controllable twelve-month net-inflow growth at about 6% of opening capital, and the Q3 acceleration has not yet proved that the underlying rate has reached the 10% the SEK 2,000bn 2030 target implies.

For the model. Grow savings capital on market return plus 5% to 8% net inflow; treat brokerage and currency income as a function of turnover per customer and the foreign share, not of savings capital, and do not extrapolate H1 2026 velocity.

C-02

Competitive intensity: to what extent, and in which direction?

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement. Competitive intensity is tightening at the margin, and the tightening shows up as lower revenue per unit rather than as lost customers. That is the more dangerous form for a model, because it erodes the revenue margin assumption silently while the volume lines look healthy.

Evidence of tightening. Nordnet now out-grows Avanza on every measure that matters (customers +12% against +8%; assets under custody +28% against +21%; Q3 operating income +20% against +12%; Q3 operating profit +24% against +13%), has a larger savings-capital base (SEK 1,459bn against 1,277bn), is broadening into Avanza's affluent target (a 50/50 private-banking venture with ABG Sundal Collier, Citi research, pension functionality) and launches in Germany in November 2026. Niche entrants SAVR and Montrose offer lower foreign-exchange fees and faster product cadence at the Private Banking and Pro end; Avanza's Private Banking and Pro customers already convert currency at 12.5 basis points against the standard 25, and their share of brokerage rose to 30% from 26% in Q3, which is the mix effect that took the brokerage yield to 10.3 basis points. Nordnet's commission-free anniversary weeks and Avanza's own fee classes are the retail-end version of the same drift.

Evidence against. No price war: fee schedules are at parity, both platforms run 70%-plus margins, Nordnet's stated posture is not to match price cuts (matching Swedish prices in Denmark would cost it about 1% of group income), incumbent banks are ineffective at capturing savings share, Avanza's home share still rose to 8.7% and its Nasdaq Stockholm transaction share is 20.4%. Churn is 0.7%.

Direction and magnitude. Tightening, moderate, structural rather than episodic, and acting on yield (a few basis points of brokerage margin, a tenth of a basis point a quarter of fund margin) rather than on volume. Nordnet's German launch diverts its attention for 2027 but does not reduce its Swedish product cadence. Score against the last twelve to eighteen months: worse.

C-03

Disruption forces: strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction?

C52

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement. The first-generation disruption threat (commission-free foreign neobrokers) has been disproved in Sweden; the second-generation threat (hidden-fee compression and AI-mediated discovery) is strengthening slowly and acts against Avanza's monetisation rather than its customer base; Avanza is itself a disruptor of the SEK 1,000bn discretionary-management pool through Sigma.

Disproved. eToro, Scalable, Levler and their kind have taken no meaningful Swedish share in three to five years: BankID onboarding, ISK and endowment-insurance tax reporting, and brand trust are the moat. The "false friend" of customer growth masking asset attrition is refuted by the data (capital +21% on customers +8%).

Strengthening. (1) The sector digest's rule: a headline fee is competed to zero (Germany's pension account at zero custody and trading fees) and the money is in fees the customer does not see; Avanza's hidden fees (currency 16% of income, fund rebates 17%) are the ones under slow pressure from cheaper currency accounts, passive mix (index funds 53.8% of fund capital) and the Retail Investment Strategy's value-for-money tests. (2) Jefferies finds neither Avanza nor Nordnet surfaces in searches for Swedish AI investment platforms, so generative search can bypass Avanza's direct funnel without any account closing. (3) A Nordea software leader says incumbent banks can close the user-experience gap within one to two years if they prioritise it. (4) Agentic cash-sweeping is named as an unprecedented deposit-volatility risk and is untested.

Avanza as disruptor. Sigma (SEK 710m one month after broad launch, 0.55% to 0.85% total fee, half the incumbent price) and AI-first development for the Danish platform are real, but management says there are no concrete internal AI savings yet.

Net. Neutral to slightly negative over the next six months; the direction of action is on revenue per unit, which the Consumer should carry as a drift in the revenue margin of roughly 1 basis point a year rather than as a step.

C-04

Political and regulatory change, and how it bears on the company

C55

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement. Regulation is net supportive today and the trend is less supportive than it looks: the largest overhang was removed, a tax tailwind enacted, and in the same eighteen months the supervisor forced a balance-sheet restructuring, a tax deduction was removed, and the post-election political debate has opened three new tax questions.

Supportive. The EU Retail Investment Strategy political agreement (December 2025, member-state approval 12 June 2026) contains no general inducement ban; the Swedish FSA said it will not investigate a national ban during implementation (fund commissions are 17% of income and Swedish fund commissions 60% of that, so this mattered). The ISK and endowment-insurance tax-free threshold rose to SEK 300,000 from January 2026. Avanza, Nordnet and Lysa have petitioned to remove the former-employer signature on paid-up pension transfers, which blocks about 25% of attempted transfers in a SEK 1,062bn non-collectively-agreed market; if granted it is a free asset-gathering lever.

Adverse and new. The FSA's late-2024 deposit-platform rules ended Sparkonto+ (a SEK 9bn single-quarter outflow, since turned into a gain through internalisation); interest on margin lending against endowment insurance lost tax deductibility on 1 January 2026; CEO Unger named at Q3 a political debate over a bank tax, ISK taxation design and endowment-insurance interest deductibility; member states keep discretion to go beyond the RIS on inducements and the FSA still intends to study domestic rules. Denmark adds a second regime with licensing and Pensionsinfo questions before any revenue.

How it bears. The regulatory risks are tail-shaped (a bank tax or a domestic inducement rule would each take 10%-plus of profit) and low probability within the horizon; the tailwinds are in the run rate. For the model: no regulatory step in the base case, a scenario line for a bank levy and for fund-rebate compression.

C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: headwind or tailwind?

❌ D45

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement. A modest, persistent headwind, bounded by a cost cap the company has met for four years. The inputs that matter are people and technology, both inflating; supplier concentration is real but has produced no price or availability shock.

People. Personnel is 60% of costs (SEK 682m of SEK 1,145m in the first nine months of 2026); employees rose to 812 from 709 (+15%) with a 4% salary round; management says 2027 additions will be smaller. Stockholm senior engineers are in high demand and Avanza prefers in-house to consultants. Turnover is low (9%), eNPS 57, so availability is not the problem; the cost of carrying a 15% larger specialist workforce before productivity shows is.

Technology. IT systems and licences are 15% of costs (SEK 176m, nine months) and rising with the Google Cloud migration (SEK 300m through 2030, roughly SEK 50m a year, expensed; on budget so far; the trading core stays on physical infrastructure). Google Cloud is a single-source dependency with activity-based pricing; Bernstein asks how Avanza resists hyperscaler pricing power. Payment commissions rose in Q3 (noted by the company, not quantified). Core infrastructure suppliers (Nasdaq, Euroclear Sweden, Nordic Growth Market, TietoEvry) are concentrated and unchanged.

Fund suppliers. The 1,300-plus external funds pay distribution rebates; the balance of power favours the platform, but passive mix and regulatory value-for-money scrutiny compress the average take (22.2 basis points).

Net. Headwind of perhaps 1 to 2 points of cost growth a year above what a static business would need, inside the 8% to 9% envelope. Not a change force of the first order.

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and is it turning?

C55

Question asked Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement. The company's own cycle has passed the peak of its transactional phase (Q2 2026) and is now in the early normalisation of that phase while the recurring base keeps compounding; net interest income may turn up with a November hike. The growth rate is turning down from the mid-twenties to the low-teens; the level is not turning down.

The run. Operating income by quarter: Q2 2025 SEK 1,062m, Q3 1,142, Q4 1,139, Q1 2026 1,257, Q2 1,334 (+26% year on year), Q3 1,283 (+12%). Operating profit: 709, 818, 733, 879, 928, 920. Annual: income +15% in 2024 and 2025; nine months 2026 +15%, operating profit +16%, EPS +15% to SEK 14.58.

Where each line sits. Brokerage SEK 308m in Q3 was flat year on year and down 12% quarter on quarter; currency SEK 197m up 13% year on year and down 12% quarter on quarter; both were at records in Q2. Fund commissions SEK 244m (+17%) and net interest income SEK 464m (+17%) are on a rising base (fund capital +19%, own savings accounts doubled to SEK 66bn). Deposits fell to 8.9% of savings capital as customers moved to funds and shares, which cut liquidity income in Q3; this is the balancing loop between risk appetite and NII.

Turning. The cyclical part turned down in Q3 for seasonal reasons (summer volatility) and for mix reasons (affluent, lower-priced customers took a larger share of trading); the structural part did not turn. Against twelve to eighteen months ago the company is at a higher recurring base and a lower activity point. The memo's expectation of H2 2026 EPS of about SEK 9.9 looks about SEK 0.4 to 0.6 too high after Q3's SEK 4.90.

C-07

Macro force: on company, sector and industry, and where it bites hardest

C52

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement. The one macro force that acts on Avanza far more than on the universe is the Swedish policy rate, and it has flipped from a two-year headwind to a two-sided lever with positive skew for earnings and negative skew for the share price. It bites hardest at the sector level (platforms are priced as rate losers and as risk-off losers at once) and least at the industry level.

The lever. The company's own sensitivity: a parallel 1 percentage point cut takes about SEK 420m off full-year net interest income (about 11% of consensus FY2026 pre-tax profit); a 1 point hike adds about SEK 1.1bn, before volume, behaviour and competition. The asymmetry comes from a zero floor on transactional deposits and an 80% pass-through on savings accounts. The Riksbank held at 1.75% through Q2 and Q3; Barclays and Deutsche Bank now see a November hike to 2%; DNB Carnegie's forward-curve case (three-month STIBOR to 2.25%) is worth about 4% of 2027 EPS. A 25 basis point hike is therefore worth perhaps SEK 150m to 250m of annual NII after realistic pass-through, 4% to 7% of pre-tax profit, from Q1 2027.

The other side. The Fitness for Fighting regime is a discount-rate shock with the US ten-year near 5.1%, Europe in risk-off, breadth in the bottom quarter, and Sweden's country tilt cut to Neutral because a hike lands on fast-resetting mortgages; Goldman Sachs puts each 100 basis points at 0.6% of GDP, worst in Sweden. Higher rates and risk-off together mean more cash held (good for NII, bad for the leverage ratio), fewer trades, and lower equity markets (bad for savings capital). The sector sits at the 20th percentile of its own multiple range, priced as a rate loser; Avanza at the 12th.

Where it bites. Earnings: slightly positive skew over six months. Multiple: negative, because the market has refused re-ratings and pays for simple, self-funded, deposit-funded cases, which Avanza is, but inside a sector it sells on rates. For the model: a 2.0% policy rate from November 2026 and 2.25% STIBOR in 2027 as the base, with the SEK 420m sensitivity run both ways.

C-08

Industry or sector driver accelerating the company's demand

C65

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement. Yes: the migration of Swedish household savings from banks to digital platforms is accelerating Avanza's asset gathering and is visible in share data, but the same driver is lifting Nordnet faster, so the sector driver is a tailwind to the base and not to relative growth.

Evidence. Avanza's share of Swedish savings capital rose from 7.8% (Q4 2024) to 8.3% (Q4 2025) to 8.7% (September 2026); its rolling share of market inflows is 11.9%, above its stock share, which is the signature of share gain. Fund purchases were 32% of all Swedish fund flows in Q3. Occupational pension capital grew 18% against a 7% market; the unit-linked premium share is 11.1%. The ISK threshold change, the EU Savings and Investment Union agenda and intergenerational transfer (median customer age 39 against a national 49; 2026 cohort median 31) are the structural drivers; the Industry digest's "buyers paying for the client" story (banks buying wealth earnings) is a second-order confirmation that this client pool is the valuable one.

Limit. The driver accelerates volume, not yield, and Nordnet's four-market platform captures the same thematic at 12% to 13% customer growth against Avanza's 8%. The acceleration is relative to the market, not relative to the peer.

C-09

Other material external dynamics

C50

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement. Four further dynamics are material enough for the model or the risk register.

  1. Nordnet's German launch (November 2026) as a relative-multiple event. The sector digest's finding is that the price already pays for Germany (Nordnet at the 44th percentile of its range) and not for Denmark (Avanza at the 12th to 17th); a strong German start would widen Nordnet's premium and could pull Avanza's multiple with it, or re-rate Avanza on the "export" theme in 2027 when Danish milestones arrive.
  2. Currency. The krona has been strong; foreign securities are 31% of brokerage-generating turnover and currency income 16% of revenue. A stronger krona lowers customers' returns on US holdings and the SEK value of foreign trading; the risks report's dual-shock (30% krona appreciation) costs SEK 179m of revenue. Morgan Stanley sees EUR/SEK at 10.40 by late 2026. Balance-sheet translation is nil.
  3. Global retail-trading cycle and the AI trade. The FfF regime names a crack in AI financing; foreign trading is 70% US; a US retail bust hits brokerage and currency together.
  4. Sweden's post-election tax debate (bank tax, ISK, endowment insurance), covered at C-04, and the Q3 pause on new mortgages for SEK 3m to 10m customers, which says the balance-sheet constraint is already shaping Private Banking growth.
C-10

Thematic drivers one by one, then the whole external picture

C58

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Taking the sector's thematic drivers in turn.

  • Hidden fees scaling with assets over interest on client cash (the sector digest's Big story): a tailwind to Avanza's recurring base (fund commissions +17%, savings capital +21%) and a headwind to its yield (fund margin 22.2 basis points, brokerage 10.3). Net positive on the level, negative on the per-unit trend.
  • Rates: turned from a two-year headwind to a mild tailwind with a November hike; the share price is still priced for the headwind.
  • Exporting the model (Denmark unpriced, Germany priced): a tailwind to the narrative from 2027, a cost before then; the one external lever that could re-rate the stock inside twelve months, per the sector digest.
  • Guide low and beat routinely: weakened as a group story in 2026 because the market trades 2027 expansion bills; Avanza's Q3 miss confirms it is not the carrier of this theme.
  • Wealth democratisation and digital share shift: strong, structural, visible; the base of the model.
  • Risk-off regime and discount rate: a headwind to activity and to the multiple over six months.

At value-chain level. Customers supportive and not fragile (churn 0.7%); competitors tightening on yield; regulators supportive now with a supervisor that has acted against the model once; suppliers a modest cost headwind; substitutes weak.

At industry and sector level. Financials neutral; digital platforms neutral with relative strength C from B; the whole cohort at the bottom fifth of its range after a run of H1 beats that did not re-rate.

The overall external change picture. Better than twelve to eighteen months ago on the two forces that de-rated the stock (rates paused and now rising; the inducement ban removed) and on the structural asset leg; worse on competitive yield pressure and on the cyclical activity leg, which has turned; and set inside a market regime that is not paying for the improvement. The external tailwinds are structural and slow; the external headwinds are cyclical and fast. For an 18-month model that means strong volume lines, a drifting revenue margin, and earnings sensitivity concentrated in two external variables (the policy rate and turnover per customer).

Internal change forces

C5413 questions
C-1158C-1265C-1345C-1462C-1555C-1650C-1728C-1845C-1948C-2055C-8638C-8750C-2155

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The internal picture is of a well-run, prudently governed franchise executing a cautious set of adjacencies with a team that is new but complete, funded by a cost line that grows at almost the revenue rate. The one internal change that mattered to earnings (the deposit internalisation) is done; the next ones are 2027 to 2030 events. The internal forces therefore support the predictability of the cost line (high) and of the recurring revenue base (high), and they add nothing to near-term earnings growth beyond what the external lines deliver. The internal risk that matters is not failure of any initiative but the combination of a larger fixed cost base and a drifting revenue margin, which Q3 2026 showed for the first time. Net: a stable B-grade internal change picture, with the "price rises" and "shareholder returns" items the clear weak points.

Horizon: the last six months and the next six, against the last twelve to eighteen.

C-11

Chief executive, key leaders and key actors: how much change, and what it signals

C58

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement. The leadership change is complete rather than under way: a fully reconstituted executive team is now in place after seven senior seat changes in 24 months, and the last six months added depth (Denmark, Fonder) rather than churn. The signal is a deliberate rebuild under a disciplined administrator, with execution capacity still untested on the hardest task (a second platform in a second country).

The facts. Gustaf Unger CEO since March 2024 (externally hired; KTH, ETH PhD, McKinsey, SEB, Nordea, a Nordnet board seat 2022 to 2024). CFO Jonas Svärling from 2 January 2026 (21 years at SEB) after Anna Casselblad left in September 2025 following 13 years. COO Jesper Bonnivier from 15 March 2026, Maria Wärn CEO of Avanza Fonder from 1 May 2026, Christian Sillesen Country Manager Denmark from 12 October 2026 (Lunar, Saxo). Group management is 11 members (nine at end-2025). Board unchanged, all nine re-elected April 2026; Sven Hagströmer chairman since 1997. No activist; founders (Hagströmer family 10.13%, Creades 10.08%) unchanged.

What it signals. Positive: urgency (a former analytics head credits the new team with ending circular discussions), cost and treasury discipline (four years of beaten cost guides; AT1 at STIBOR plus 2.85% against Nordnet's plus 5.0%), and a team built for Strategy 2030 rather than for the last cycle. Negative: three forced exits in six months of 2025 during a re-platforming; the CEO's profile is delegative and conflict-averse on a former peer's account, with no engineering background, and his stated preference is to be in the forefront but not first with new technology. The key-person dependency is now the CTO (Fredrik Broman) on cloud and the Danish build. For the next six months the leadership change is a stabiliser, not a force.

C-12

Strategy and strategic priorities: how much change, how well articulated and resourced

C65

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement. Strategy 2030 has moved from articulation (the October 2024 Capital Markets Day) to execution across all five priorities in the last six months, and it is well articulated and adequately resourced inside a stated cost envelope; the weakness is that resource allocation is running ahead of measurable earnings contribution, and the one quantified efficiency promise is deferred to 2030.

The priorities and their state. (1) Deeper Swedish wallet share (customers keep about one-third of savings with Avanza; two-thirds of Q3 inflows from existing customers): at early output. (2) Private Banking leadership: Sigma launched 31 August 2026 (SEK 710m after one month), mortgage tiers above SEK 30m, Kepler Cheuvreux research hub; but new mortgages for SEK 3m to 10m customers paused in Q3 and the rate raised 10 basis points. (3) Occupational pensions leadership (11.1% unit-linked premium share; Benifex and Lifeplan partnerships; Pension Portal; transfer-law lobbying): early output. (4) Cloud acceleration (SEK 300m to 2030, on budget): input. (5) At least one European market: Denmark moved from announcement (April 2026) to a branch application, Copenhagen prototype tests, local social media and a country manager: input-only until H2 2027.

Articulation and resourcing. Targets are explicit (SEK 2,000bn of savings capital at 15% a year; 8% average cost growth to 2030, 5% in 2030; return on equity at least 35%; 70% payout); the Danish budget is phased (SEK 120m to 150m pre-launch, 20% capitalised, SEK 40m in 2026, SEK 80m running plus up to SEK 60m marketing for three years, profitability about five years after launch). Headcount rose 15% to fund it. What is missing: any quantified productivity benefit from cloud or AI, Sigma's economics, and a Danish pricing schedule. Management explicitly reinvests efficiency gains rather than banking them ("more initiatives than capacity").

Against twelve to eighteen months ago: more real (a branch, a leader, a product) and more expensive, with the same targets.

C-13

Guidance raised, mid-term targets set or raised?

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement. No. Avanza does not guide on earnings; its cost guidance for 2026 (9% Swedish growth) was reiterated for the fourth consecutive quarter; the 2030 targets were set in October 2024 and have not been lifted; the one change at Q3 was a SEK 10m reduction in 2026 Danish cost (SEK 40m from 50m) with the total budget unchanged. The signal is of a company that guides only what it controls and does not move it; for the Consumer this means the guidance line is a cost ceiling to model against, not an earnings anchor, and no upgrade impulse from guidance is available in the next six months. The 15% savings-capital target was missed in 2025 (13%) and is being met in 2026 only with market help (21%).

C-14

Standards improving in execution, operations, governance or cost discipline?

C62

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Standards are high and improving on cost control and automation, stable on governance, and showed a first crack on monetisation and on operational capacity in Q3 2026.

Improving. Cost ceilings beaten in 2022 (-2.7%), 2023 (-0.7%), 2024 (9.2% against 9.5%) and 2025 (10.4% against 11.0%); first-half 2026 cost inflation 6.6% gave DNB Carnegie comfort on the full-year 9%; Q3 costs 3% below consensus. Cost-to-savings capital 14.1 basis points (2025), about 14 in H1 2026, against Nordnet's 15 to 16. Automation: free pre-filled K4 tax forms cut service calls by more than 40%; securities transfers, fund transfers and corporate margin applications automated; app deployments in minutes; the Tieto Hub back-office migration done; cloud milestones on time. Danish spend came in under budget through internal work. Return on equity 44% in Q3.

Not improving. Costs still rose 12% year on year in Q3 and 15% in Q2 (headcount +15%), the Q3 beat was seasonal, and management keeps the 8% framework rather than tightening it. Brokerage yield, fund margin and income per customer all fell. A former business developer says the Swedish infrastructure was not built for the present scale and high-traffic events caused outages; the CFO has said internal systems need significant upgrades. Governance scaffolding (three lines of defence, an IT committee, ICAAP-justified payouts) is unchanged.

Net. The cost discipline is the single most reliable input the model has; the execution standard on platform capacity and the monetisation standard are the ones to watch.

C-15

Reducing the perimeter: divestments, simplification, exits?

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement. The perimeter was simplified in three steps over the last eighteen months and is now widening again; the simplification is complete and in the run rate, and no further reduction is in prospect.

Done. Sparkonto+ (external partner-bank deposits) wound down fully on 13 May 2026, forced by the FSA; of SEK 42bn that left external accounts since January 2025, 60% (about SEK 25bn) was retained on Avanza's own balance sheet, doubling own savings accounts to SEK 66bn and converting a roughly 5 basis point distribution fee into a roughly 50 basis point spread; this is the largest single earnings change of the period and it is now fully in the quarterly base (Q2 NII SEK 486m, Q3 SEK 464m). The Stabelo mortgage stake was sold to Swedbank in Q2 2025 while distribution continues off balance sheet. Placera's editorial team was disbanded in 2024 and content bought in. Sigmastocks was legally absorbed in Q3 2026.

Widening. Denmark, a second platform, Sigma as a new product category and a 15% larger workforce all add rather than remove perimeter. The perimeter is therefore at its narrowest now and will be wider by H2 2027, which the predictability question (C-79) must carry.

C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

C50

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement. Avanza is entering one new geography (Denmark, H2 2027) and one new product market (digital discretionary management, Sigma, August 2026). Both are sensible as bounded options; neither is sensible as a near-term earnings driver; and the geographic one carries a structural disadvantage the company has chosen to accept.

Denmark. The prize: a market Avanza sizes at up to SEK 9trn (Nordnet SEK 6.2trn; the SEK 2.8trn gap is unexplained), median household wealth twice Sweden's, fund participation 13% against 64%, Nordnet's revenue margin there 50 basis points against Avanza's 44 in Sweden, and prices that stay high. The problem: Nordnet has 652,400 Danish customers, 9% to 10% population penetration, 20 years of brand, a Livrente insurance-based pension wrapper that captures the largest pool (SEK 2trn, 72,000 pension customers), Pensionsinfo integration and the price-setter's seat; Avanza will launch with trading and Aktiesparekonto parity, no insurance licence, and no disclosed fee schedule. The method: a separate, market-agnostic, AI-coded platform to protect the Swedish core, at SEK 120m to 150m pre-launch (80% expensed) and SEK 300m-plus cumulative before break-even around 2032; Jefferies counts SEK 340m to 380m over three years against Nordnet's SEK 200m to 230m for a market six times larger. Sensible: bounded (about 2% to 3% of EPS a year), organic, sequenced, led by a local hire, and unpriced by the market; the sector digest's view is that Denmark is the sharper half of the export story because the Danish risk is cost, not price. Not sensible: dual-stack maintenance for ever, and a late entry into an incumbent's pension moat.

Sigma. A SEK 1,000bn addressable pool at half the incumbent price; SEK 710m in a month (some transferred from existing Avanza products on DNB Carnegie's reading); margin-accretive if it scales; SEK 5bn to 10bn by 2028 would add 3% to 5% to fee income; nothing disclosed on acquisition cost, retention or payback. Option value, not forecast.

C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent?

❌ D28

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement. There are no real price rises, and the model's promise to customers is the opposite. Unit prices are drifting down through mix and competition, and the only price levers in use are on the funding side (deposit rates) and at the margin of the lending book.

The evidence of erosion. Gross brokerage income per unit of turnover: 11.4 basis points (Q2 2025), 10.6 (Q1 2026), 11.0 (Q2), 10.3 (Q3), as larger trades in the fixed-fee class and Private Banking and Pro customers (30% of trading) take a larger share; average fund margin 23.3 basis points a year ago, 22.7 in Q2, 22.2 in Q3 under index-fund mix (53.8% of fund capital); currency conversion at 12.5 basis points for Private Banking and Pro against the standard 25; Sigma priced at half the market; a 17 basis point Swedish index fund; commission-free trading up to SEK 50,000 for new customers.

The levers that exist. Deposit pricing (an 80% pass-through on savings accounts and 50% on investment accounts, used immediately after each Riksbank move; the Q3 interest-bearing share of deposits 60%); a 10 basis point rise in the mortgage rate for SEK 3m to 10m customers alongside the application pause; the 25 basis point foreign-exchange spread held for standard customers. Management has itself said its brokerage pricing may not align with its simplicity goals.

Latent. Pricing power is exercised through mix (foreign trading at 31% of turnover lifts the blended rate) and retention, not list price. For the model: no price contribution; a revenue-margin drift of about minus 1 basis point a year in the base, minus 2 in the adverse case.

C-18

Mergers and acquisitions: stepping up, and how sensible?

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement. Not stepping up. The only acquisition in the period is Sigmastocks (SEK 21.1m total, final SEK 6.3m paid July 2026, legally merged, its algorithm amortising at just under SEK 2m a quarter to August 2031), which was strategically sensible (it became Sigma within a year), disciplined in size and integrated. Denmark is explicitly organic. There is no indication of acquisitive intent, and the balance-sheet constraint (the leverage ratio) argues against it. This is neither a positive nor a negative force for the model; it removes one source of EPS noise.

C-19

Balance-sheet use and shareholder returns: increasing, or mooted?

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement. Balance-sheet use has increased materially, by regulation and by design, and shareholder returns have not stepped up; management is not mooting a step-up, and the binding leverage ratio is the reason.

Balance sheet. Deposits from the public SEK 111.9bn (September 2026) after internalising Sparkonto+; loans to the public SEK 38.2bn (mortgages SEK 19.5bn-plus, +38% year on year; margin lending SEK 13.4bn-plus); SEK 1.3bn of AT1 issued (SEK 800m at STIBOR plus 3.25% in 2025, SEK 500m at plus 2.85% in March 2026) to hold the leverage ratio, which is 4.8% against a 3.5% requirement with SEK 46bn of deposit headroom. The Q3 pause on new mortgages for the SEK 3m to 10m segment says the constraint is already rationing growth in the lending book, which the Consumer should model as a ceiling on NII growth from lending rather than an open line.

Returns. Dividend SEK 12.75 for 2025 (76% payout, above the 70% policy); consensus SEK 14.52 for 2026; a 10% buyback authorisation unused at 30 September; no special. Nordnet, by contrast, runs a 4.0% to 4.5% leverage target with buybacks. Management's stated preference is to adjust the payout above 70% to avoid overcapitalisation rather than to repurchase. No step-up in the next six months is likely; the lever that could change this is a sustained risk-on shrinkage of deposits (every SEK 8bn to 10bn of customer buying restores 30 to 50 basis points of leverage ratio).

C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces, present or plausible

C55

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement. Five smaller positives are present, all on the recurring side and none individually large.

  1. Sigma's early traction (SEK 710m) and the pension business (capital +18%, share 11.1%, Autopension default, Benifex and Lifeplan channels) are both compounding lines outside consensus.
  2. Internal engineering capacity is proving cheaper than guided (Denmark SEK 40m against 50m in 2026; cloud under budget); AI-first development on the Pension Portal and the Danish platform is real even if unquantified.
  3. The transfer-law petition, if granted, unlocks sticky pension assets at no acquisition cost.
  4. The cultural reset (new principles; eNPS 57; 9% staff turnover; second among technology students) supports delivery through the build.
  5. Product cadence remains high (pre- and post-market US trading, free real-time US quotes, Placera Forum, the analysis hub), which is the retention asset. Plausible but unproven: a sustained STIBOR rise, which would be the largest single positive (C-07).
C-86

Negative corporate change developments, present or plausible

❌ D38

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement. Four negative developments are present and two are plausible; together they are the reason the Q3 print changed the earnings-conversion question.

  1. Monetisation decline (present): brokerage yield 10.3 basis points, fund margin 22.2, income per customer SEK 2,170 (-6% quarter on quarter), currency income 8% below consensus. Management calls it temporary; the mix behind it (affluent customers, passive funds) is strategic and therefore not temporary.
  2. Cost base built ahead of revenue (present): headcount +15%, costs +12% year on year, a second platform, cloud expensed, efficiency gains reinvested; the 8% framework holds costs growing near the revenue rate, so operating leverage is a later promise.
  3. Balance-sheet rationing of Private Banking growth (present): the mortgage pause and the 10 basis point rate rise.
  4. Relative activity weakness (present): trades down 10% year on year against 5% at Nordnet; customer and asset growth behind Nordnet.
  5. Platform capacity and technical debt (plausible): outages under load, no disclosed uptime data, a Google Cloud single source.
  6. Discovery disintermediation (plausible): absence from AI search results. None is a cockroach in the framework's sense; together they fit the "Pret sandwich" pattern the memo already flagged, and the Q3 print was the first output in which the sandwich showed.
C-87

Have controlling actors indicated scope for change in strategy or approach?

C50

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement. No. The Hagströmer family and Creades (20.21% combined, each 10.1% of votes) have given no indication of any change; the chairman has held the seat for 29 years and described the executive turnover as a normal opportunity for the CEO to build a team; all nine directors were re-elected; there is no activist, no shareholder agreement, no voting cap. The founder block's influence runs through the nomination committee and is aligned with management's prudent posture. For the model this is stability: no change of strategy, no capital-structure event, no takeover premium, and no external discipline on the Danish bet either.

C-21

Taken together: how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes?

C55

Question asked Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

The positive internal changes are broad (a rebuilt team, a codified cost cap, a simplified and then widening perimeter, three product and channel adjacencies, a first foreign market, a heavier balance sheet) and resourced inside a stated envelope, and the largest of them, the deposit internalisation, has already reached full output and is in the base. What remains is adjacency rather than re-basing: Denmark, Sigma and pensions are each worth low single digits of EPS in the horizon and are input-only or at early output, and the next quantified step-change (cost growth to 5% by 2030) is three years away. Against the negatives present (yield drift, a cost base that grew ahead of revenue, a rationed lending book), the net internal force over the next six to eighteen months is modestly positive on substance and neutral on near-term earnings. For the Consumer: model costs at the guided 9% then 8% (SEK 1,580m in 2026, about SEK 1,800m in 2027 including Denmark), no margin expansion before 2028, Denmark as a cost line of SEK 40m, about SEK 120m and about SEK 140m in 2026 to 2028, and Sigma and pensions as upside scenarios rather than base-case lines.

Industry and Value Chain

C6216 questions
C-2475C-2565C-2672C-2768C-2870C-2962C-3165C-8460C-3268C-3370C-3448C-3558C-3670C-3755C-3852C-3955

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry structure is one of the better ones in European financials for a model: a two-firm digital tier taking share from banks at fee parity, margin-first behaviour, high switching friction, a direct route to market, and weak substitutes. The structure protects what Avanza already has (customers, capital, the cost ratio) and protects less well what it needs to grow (yield per unit, relative growth, the multiple), because the second duopolist is compounding faster on a wider footprint and the fee pool per unit is drifting down through mix, niche pricing and regulation. The historical evidence says this sector re-rates on a visible shift toward asset-scaled fees and de-rates on rate dependence and on extrapolated activity; Avanza is better placed than its peers on the first and has just shown a mild case of the second. For the Consumer: the industry supports high-confidence volume lines, a medium-confidence revenue margin with a downward drift, and no help from the industry on the multiple until the Danish milestones arrive.

C-24

Rank by market share or size, and distance from number one or two

B75

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement. Avanza is the clear number one in its home activity and the number two in its region, and the distance from the regional number one is widening.

At home. 8.7% of Swedish savings capital (SEK 1,277bn of a SEK 13,900bn market) against Nordnet's 3.8% to 5% (the sources differ by period and venue); 20.4% of all Nasdaq Stockholm transactions, the largest single operator; 2,391,100 customers, 20.5% of the population and 38% of Swedes aged 20 to 39; a rolling share of market inflows of 11.9% and 32% of Q3 fund-market inflows; 16 consecutive Swedish Quality Index wins. The gap to the number two at home is roughly two to one on savings capital and widening slowly (share up about half a point a year). In occupational pensions Avanza ranks about fourth with an 11.1% unit-linked premium share, its stated aim being leadership by 2030.

Regionally. Nordnet has SEK 1,459bn of savings capital and about 2.57m customers across four countries against Avanza's SEK 1,277bn and 2.39m in one; Nordnet grew assets 28% to 29% against 21% to 22% and customers 12% to 13% against 8%. In Denmark, Avanza will enter in H2 2027 against an incumbent with 9% population penetration. Among European platforms, Fineco (EUR 1.3bn revenue) is larger in absolute revenue.

What it means for the model. Home leadership supports the cost ratio and the brand; the regional gap matters for the multiple, not for the Swedish earnings path, which does not require beating Nordnet.

C-25

Market concentration: top five above 30%, and how steep?

C65

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement. The relevant market has two layers. The total Swedish savings market is bank-dominated and moderately concentrated: the four universal banks (Swedbank, SEB, Handelsbanken, Nordea) plus Avanza hold well over 30% by construction (the two digital platforms alone hold about 13%). The digital investing tier, where Avanza competes for flows, is a duopoly: Avanza and Nordnet hold effectively the whole tier, with SAVR, Montrose and Levler unsized and sub-scale, so the share distribution is extremely steep (the top two hold many times the share of the rest). Concentration is a support: the banks compete on balance sheet and service rather than on platform pricing, and the two platforms compete on product at fee parity. The one structural caveat is that the concentration is in distribution; the fee pool itself (fund rebates, foreign-exchange spreads) is set in a wider European context where regulation and passive mix are compressing it.

C-26

Share stability and new entrants' share over three to five years

C72

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement. Shares are stable in direction and slowly moving in Avanza's favour at home; new entrants have taken no meaningful share in three to five years.

Avanza's share of savings capital rose from 7.8% (Q4 2024) to 8.3% (Q4 2025) to 8.4% (June 2026) to 8.7% (September 2026); its share of inflows runs above its share of stock (11.9% against 8.7%), which is the mechanical signature of continuing gain. Churn is 0.7% rolling twelve months, historically under 2% through cycles, so the installed base does not move. Entrants: commission-free brokers (Levler) have faced sustainability problems; eToro's Nordic push dates only from August 2025; SAVR and Montrose are credible on product but, on a former SAVR executive's own account, the step from tens of thousands to hundreds of thousands of customers is hard because Swedes are loyal to Avanza and Nordnet; incumbent banks lose flows despite stated digital ambitions. The one entrant that matters is Nordnet, which is not new, and whose gains are in the Nordics rather than in Sweden (its Swedish operating profit fell 24% in Q1 2026 while Avanza's rose). The watch item is not share of customers but share of the most profitable customers (Private Banking and active traders), where SAVR and Montrose aim.

C-27

Does share consolidate toward the largest and the most advantaged, and is Avanza among them?

C68

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement. Yes on both counts at home, and the second count is the one that matters: share consolidates toward the lowest-cost, highest-satisfaction digital operator, not toward the largest balance sheet, and Avanza is that operator in Sweden. Regionally, scale on one platform is compounding faster for Nordnet.

The mechanism is mechanical rather than heroic: each new customer adds to brand, data and order flow at near-zero marginal cost; the cost-to-savings ratio falls as capital grows (14.5 to 14.1 basis points, about 14 in 2026); the platforms take a disproportionate share of net flows because they are cheaper and better rated (satisfaction 77.6 against an industry 70.0). The banks have the balance sheets and lose the flows; this is why "largest" and "most advantaged" diverge. Nordnet's four-market platform lowers its unit cost with each period and its revenue margin outside Sweden is higher (50 basis points in Denmark), so regionally the consolidating pull favours it; Avanza's answer (a second platform for one market) adds cost without regional scale. For the Swedish earnings model the pull is a tailwind worth about half a point of share a year; for the multiple it is contested.

C-28

Competitors' attitude: margin first or volume first; benign or loss-tolerant; do prices fall when volume falls?

C70

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. Margin-first, benign and stable at the two scaled competitors; loss-tolerant only at sub-scale entrants; and prices did not fall when volumes fell in the last trough.

Both platforms run 70%-plus operating margins and neither has used price as a weapon; fee schedules are at parity (commission-free up to SEK 50,000 for new customers; comparable volume tiers). Nordnet says it feels no need to match Avanza in Denmark and that aligning Danish prices with Swedish would cost only 1% of group income; when it cut the Danish minimum commission in late 2024 the cost was SEK 50m a year, a measured move. Incumbent banks respond with service (Swedbank's 14-second call answering; Nordea's mortgage speed), not platform pricing. Loss-tolerant behaviour exists at SAVR (returns fund kickbacks) and Levler (zero fees) and is sub-scale. In the 2022 to 2023 volume trough brokerage rates held; the 2026 yield decline (11.4 to 10.3 basis points) is mix within Avanza's own fee classes at high volume, not a price response to weakness. The one deliberate price concession in the system is Avanza's own: cheaper currency accounts and fee classes for its most active customers, which is a defence of retention at the top end rather than an industry price move.

C-29

Stability of that attitude over eighteen months to three years; O'Leary instability; shark attack risk

C62

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude is stable and predictable; the drift over eighteen months to three years is toward giving the most active and affluent customers more for less, which is a softening at the edges rather than a change of regime; the one place a shark attack could come is Denmark, and the evidence says it is unlikely.

Over the period: Avanza introduced flexible fee classes (Q4 2025), half-price currency conversion for Private Banking and Pro, mortgage tiers for the very wealthy, and Sigma at half the discretionary market price; Nordnet launched currency accounts on ISK wrappers, eight new venues, commission-free weeks and a private-banking venture; SAVR and Montrose price foreign exchange below both. None of this is reactive or situation-dependent; each is a product move aimed at retention of the top cohort. There is no O'Leary actor in the Swedish tier; the only unpredictable actor is the supervisor. In Denmark, Nordnet has the means (76% Danish operating margin), the motive (9% penetration to defend) and has shown the opportunity (the 2024 minimum-commission cut); DNB Carnegie argues it has no need to, because its primary front is displacing expensive banks rather than a new digital peer, and the stated posture is welcoming. The risk the Consumer should carry is not a Swedish price war but Danish foreign-exchange fee compression if Avanza launches below 25 basis points, which could cost both platforms up to SEK 200m on DNB Carnegie's estimate.

C-31

How supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C65

Question asked Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. Supportive: a concentrated digital tier with rational, margin-first competitors, structural flow migration from banks, high switching friction (tax wrappers, pensions, BankID), and a growing pool. Fragile on three edges, all already visible in the data rather than hypothetical: the second duopolist compounds faster on a wider footprint; the fee pool per unit is drifting down through mix and regulation; and the supervisor has acted against the platform model once (deposit rules) and may study domestic inducement rules. The support therefore protects volume and the installed base well and protects yield and relative growth less well. For the model this translates into high confidence on savings-capital growth and cost ratio, medium confidence on the revenue margin, and the multiple exposed to the peer's growth.

C-84

Historical precedents and empirical evidence on how this sector outperforms and underperforms, and Avanza's fit

C60

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. The evidence in the files converges on five patterns, and Avanza fits the outperformance patterns on asset gathering and quality and the underperformance patterns on cyclical extrapolation and rate dependence.

  1. Shares follow the fee lines that scale with assets, not the quarter's rates or volumes. The sector digest's rule: interest on client cash and trading move the quarter; over one to two years the shares follow fees that compound with assets, and a visible mix shift toward them is the re-rating. Precedent: the cohort pulled back 6.7% over three months straight through a run of H1 2026 beats, because the market trades the next year's expansion bills. Avanza fits: fund commissions and NII (50% of income) compound with capital; the market has not yet paid for the shift (12th to 19th percentile).
  2. Hidden fees reprice as long as assets compound; headline fees go to zero. Germany's pension account (zero custody and trading at flatexDEGIRO, Scalable, Smartbroker) is the live example. Avanza's income is heavy in hidden fees (currency 16%, rebates 17%), which is good for durability and exposed to the specific pressures on those two lines.
  3. Rate dependence is the sector's recurring false friend. AJ Bell (cash interest 100% of EBIT, 47% of revenue), Fineco (NII 47%), Nordnet (NII 43% to 55%) and Avanza (33%, 42% in 2024) all de-rated as rates fell; the Avanza risks report's own ACH found the FALSE FRIEND profile (rate lags masking structural decay) most consistent with the evidence in June 2026, and Q3's NII miss on lower deposits is a mild instance. Avanza's lower NII share and asymmetric sensitivity make it the least exposed of the four.
  4. Lazy cyclical extrapolation after an activity peak (the Wisdom Library's Avanza/Covid archetype): EPS fell 19% in 2022 after the 2020 to 2021 boom, and the multiple fell from a five-year peak of 37x. The H1 2026 activity peak and Q3 normalisation is a smaller version of the same shape.
  5. An unpriced geographic entry re-rates; a priced one does not. Nordnet (Germany, 44th percentile) against Avanza (Denmark, 12th to 17th); the sector digest prefers the unpriced entry and dates the proof to Avanza's 2027 quarterly reports. Fineco's 2023 UK exit after 23,000 clients in six years is the honest base rate for greenfield platform entries.

Where companies in this sector have outperformed operationally it has been through cost caps delivered against double-digit revenue (Avanza 2024 to 2025; flatexDEGIRO's 21% personnel cut in 2026), manufacturing margin captured in-house (Fineco Asset Management at 78 basis points against 46 for third-party product), and sticky wrappers (Nordnet's Livrente). Avanza has the first, is attempting the third in pensions, and has only a small version of the second (23 own funds). Where they have underperformed it has been price wars in open markets (flatexDEGIRO's acquisition cost doubling to EUR 171), capitalised technology unwinding, and regulatory shocks to cash retention (Rathbones). Avanza's expensing policy and clean accounting put it on the right side of the second; the first is a Danish risk; the third is a Swedish tail.

C-32

Customer demand: how far it supports the thesis, and how fragile

C68

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement. Customer demand is the thesis's strongest foundation on retention and asset flows and its most fragile element on activity and cash allocation; the two now move in opposite directions, and Q3 2026 showed that strong asset demand can coexist with weaker revenue conversion.

Support. Rolling churn 0.7%; 490,000 daily active users; 52,800 net new customers in Q3; two-thirds of inflows from existing customers (the wallet-share strategy working); record fund demand; customers hold only one-third of their wealth with Avanza, of which management reckons half the remainder is addressable. Fragility. Customers are becoming less valuable per krona: they buy index funds (margin 22.2 basis points), the affluent trade at lower rates, broad retail trades less when volatility falls, and the cash they hold (the NII base) falls when they re-risk. The willingness to switch providers in Sweden is the highest in Europe (38%), which has not hurt Avanza because it is the destination, but it means loyalty is a barrier, not a wall. The customer base is young (median 39), which is the long-run asset and the near-run reason for lower balances per customer. For the model. Demand supports savings capital and customers; it does not by itself support income per customer, which fell 6% in Q3 to SEK 2,170 annualised and should be modelled flat to slightly down in the base case.

C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and how fragile

C70

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement. The route to market is direct, digital and owned, which is the cleanest in the comparison set and a structural advantage; the intermediated edges are small and the two that matter (pension transfers and digital discovery) are where hindrance could arise.

Avanza onboards through BankID with no intermediary holding the relationship; 44% of Nordnet's new customers come from organic search and direct app traffic and the pattern is similar for Avanza, whose acquisition cost is about SEK 590 against a lifetime value many multiples of that. Intermediaries exist in three places: occupational pensions (Benifex and Lifeplan partnerships for employer distribution, a help; employer-signature rules on transfers, a hindrance blocking about 25% of attempted transfers), mortgages (Stabelo and Landshypotek distributed off balance sheet, a help to capital), and research and content (Kepler Cheuvreux, Placera content bought in). The emerging hindrance is discovery: if generative search becomes the gateway, Avanza's direct funnel is not where AI tools send Swedish users today (Jefferies). Fragility is low over the horizon and rising beyond it.

C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour: does it help or hurt, and how fragile is Avanza's position?

❌ D48

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement. Nordnet's behaviour hurts Avanza's relative growth and its multiple while leaving the Swedish franchise intact; the banks' behaviour helps by default; the niche entrants' behaviour hurts at the margin of yield. Avanza's position against them is strong on the installed base and fragile on growth and on revenue per unit.

Nordnet: faster customer and asset growth, a Q3 that beat Avanza's on growth and matched it on margin (71%), a broadening affluent proposition, Germany from November 2026, and pricing power outside Sweden that Avanza will have to attack from a standing start. Banks: Swedbank, SEB, Handelsbanken and Nordea defend legacy pools and gain in mortgages on speed, but lose savings share; a Nordea technologist's claim that banks can close the gap in one to two years is a medium-term watch, not a current fact. SAVR and Montrose: lower foreign-exchange fees and faster cadence at the affluent end; the Q3 yield compression is the first output consistent with their pressure, although management attributes it to sentiment and mix. Avanza's fragility is therefore in exactly the lines Q3 missed (brokerage and currency yields) and not in customers or capital.

C-35

New entrants by type, and how much of a threat each is

C58

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement. Three types, three different threat levels, none a near-term share threat.

  • Adjacent-market entrants (eToro with Nasdaq data since August 2025, Revolut's investing and wealth aggregation, Klarna's savings and current accounts, German flat-fee platforms): a long-term threat to customer attention and to the primary-relationship position, not to Swedish savings share within the horizon; BankID, ISK reporting and trust keep them sub-scale; the risk digest rates eToro's Nordic expansion medium plausibility for pricing pressure.
  • Standard entrants (Levler and commission-free brokers): negligible; hidden fees and poor service limit them.
  • Orthogonal and innovative entrants (SAVR returning kickbacks, Montrose for active and Private Banking clients, and the untested class of AI agents sweeping cash or routing orders): the only type with evidence of effect, visible as pricing pressure on foreign exchange and on the Pro and Private Banking tiers rather than as customer loss; one expert warns Montrose could peel away lucrative clients, another calls it low plausibility given Avanza's roughly 50% share of retail equity trading. For the model: no entrant line; the effect is already inside the revenue-margin drift.
C-36

Substitutes and alternatives: how much of a threat, and how is it changing?

C70

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement. Substitutes are weak and the substitution runs toward Avanza. The alternatives to a digital savings platform are bank deposits, bank-distributed funds and bank advisers; the flow data (platform share of inflows above share of stock; fund inflows at 32% of the market) says customers are substituting away from the banks. The one substitute that hurt Avanza was its own partner-bank savings product, now gone. Two alternatives are strengthening slowly: automated discretionary management and AI-mediated advice, both of which Avanza offers (Sigma) and both of which compress the fee pool if they become the norm; and, over the long run, algorithmic cash sweeping that would make deposits less sticky at every platform. Within the horizon the threat is low; the direction is toward lower-fee forms of the same service, which Avanza is positioned to provide but at lower margin.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: help or hurt, and how fragile?

C55

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement. On the current status they help: no inducement ban, a doubled ISK threshold, a supervisor that declined to pursue a national ban, a reduced risk-based Pillar 2 requirement (5.71% to 5.02%). On the trend they are the least predictable actor in the chain: the FSA's deposit rules forced a SEK 42bn product closure within the last two years, the tax deduction on endowment-backed margin loans was removed, the FSA still intends to study domestic inducement rules, and the post-election debate has raised a bank tax and ISK redesign. The leverage ratio (non-risk-based, 3.5% including guidance) is the supervisor's binding lever on the balance sheet and it is already rationing mortgage growth. The position is supportive with a fragile trend; the probability-weighted effect over eighteen months is small; the tail is large (10%-plus of profit on either a bank levy or a rebate rule). Avanza's own posture (transparent, low-fee, no advice inducements) is on the right side of the regulatory direction, which is the mitigant.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how, and how fragile does it leave the company?

C52

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement. Disruption is acting on every actor, mostly in Avanza's favour at the customer end and mostly against its yield at the monetisation end.

  • Demand: customers want multi-currency, social features, automated portfolios and lower fees; Avanza has answered with Sigma, Placera Forum, the analysis hub and extended US trading, not with crypto; passive adoption is the disruption that costs it margin.
  • Competition: AI-assisted development lowers the cost of building a platform (Avanza's own Danish build), which lowers entry cost for everyone; Nordnet is agentic-first in 43% of its product teams; AI search can redirect discovery.
  • Regulators: the RIS value-for-money tests and a possible T+1 settlement cycle (October 2027) push toward cheaper funds and more automation; net positive for a low-cost platform, negative for the rebate line.
  • Suppliers: cloud replaces owned infrastructure (vendor power rises); fund manufacturers lose shelf leverage to platforms (Avanza's power rises).
  • Other actors: agentic cash sweeping could destabilise deposits at every platform; untested. Fragility: low on the customer relationship within three years, medium on monetisation per unit, and the position improves if Avanza's own AI investment produces measurable productivity, which it has not yet.
C-39

Suppliers: a support or a source of risk?

C55

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement. A support on economics and a concentrated source of operational risk. The suppliers that matter are a small set of critical infrastructures (Nasdaq, Euroclear Sweden, Nordic Growth Market, TietoEvry), one hyperscaler (Google Cloud, selected March 2025, SEK 300m through 2030, activity-based pricing), 1,300-plus external fund managers (who pay Avanza, and over whom it has volume leverage), and external managers of nine of its 23 own funds (Amundi and others). None has failed; the Tieto Hub migration completed; the first Google Cloud production service went live on time. The risks are outage (login failures under load are documented; a prolonged cloud or settlement outage would freeze trading), vendor pricing power as the cloud share rises, and a rebate pool that regulation and passive mix shrink. Payment commissions rose in Q3, an early supply-side cost signal the company did not quantify. The Consumer should carry supplier cost inside the 9% cost line and carry outage as an unquantified tail.
