How has headcount growth (Avanza 709 to 812 employees in a year) translated into revenue per employee and savings capital per employee over time at Avanza and Nordnet? What do the management commentary and the 2030 framework (8% average annual cost growth, 5% by 2030) imply for the cost-to-savings-capital ratio, and what ratio have Nordnet and Fineco reached at larger scale? How are payment commissions, exchange and clearing fees and data costs trending per transaction? Focus: Given a cost base guided to grow 8% to 9% a year regardless of revenue, how much of each 10% change in income actually reaches operating profit at Avanza on the way up and on the way down, and is there any realistic scope, with precedent, for the cost line to be cut or held flat in a year in which trading income normalises, or does the operating leverage only ever work in one direction at a time?

Request: T-P2

Peers: Nordnet (SAVE-SE), the direct peer, the price-setter in Denmark with a revenue margin of about 50 basis points there against Avanza's about 44 in Sweden, which cut its Danish minimum commission in late 2024 and reported its brokerage commission rate falling 11% in one quarter in early 2026; SAVR and Montrose (private Swedish entrants), which price foreign exchange below the two incumbents and target active and Private Banking customers; Lysa (private), the Swedish low-fee robo-adviser; flatexDEGIRO (FTK-DE), the precedent for a flat-rate pricing model and for a zero-fee German pension account; FinecoBank (FBK-IT), the precedent for holding deposit remuneration near zero through a rate cycle and for a 78 basis point in-house fund margin; AJ Bell (AJB-GB) and Hargreaves Lansdown (private), the UK precedents for custody fee cuts under regulatory fair-value pressure and for retained interest on client cash. Operating conditions: A Riksbank hiking cycle (a rise from 1.75% from November 2026) and a cutting cycle (2024 to 2025, from 4.0% to 1.75%); high and low equity-market volatility (the first half of 2026 against the third quarter); risk-on periods in which customers move cash into funds and shares and risk-off periods in which they hold cash; a rising share of Private Banking and Pro customers in trading (30% in the third quarter of 2026 from 26%); a rising share of index funds in fund capital (53.8%); competitive entry by niche platforms at the affluent end; regulatory scrutiny of fund inducements under the EU Retail Investment Strategy and any Swedish domestic rules; the Danish market entry against an incumbent price-setter. Inputs: What has happened to Avanza's gross brokerage income per unit of turnover quarter by quarter since 2019 (the company reported 11.4 basis points in the second quarter of 2025, 10.6 in the first quarter of 2026, 11.0 in the second and 10.3 in the third), and how much of each move is explained by the foreign share of turnover, by the fee class mix, by the Private Banking and Pro share, and by list-price changes? What is the history of Avanza's fee schedules (brokerage classes, the 25 basis point standard currency conversion fee, the 12.5 basis point manual currency account for Private Banking and Pro, commission-free trading up to SEK 50,000 for new customers, the 17 basis point Swedish index fund) and when have prices been raised, cut or restructured? What is the evidence on Avanza's deposit pricing: the pass-through of policy-rate changes to savings accounts and to investment accounts in each cut since May 2024 (brokers have cited 50% to 80%), the speed of each change, and the share of deposits that is interest-bearing (60% in mid-2026)? What is the evidence that the 25 basis point currency conversion fee is being eroded by cheaper currency accounts at Avanza, Nordnet, SAVR and Montrose, and what share of foreign trading now runs through them? How have fund distribution margins (Avanza reported 22.2 basis points in the third quarter of 2026 from 23.3 a year earlier) moved with the index share and with regulatory pressure in Sweden, the Nordics and the UK, and what do fund manufacturers say about rebate negotiations with platforms? What did Nordnet actually do to Danish pricing when Saxo Bank undercut it, and what did it do when it cut the minimum commission, with the revenue effect? What do customers and the sell-side say about Avanza's pricing power at the affluent end after the mortgage application pause and the 10 basis point mortgage rate increase for customers with SEK 3m to 10m in the third quarter of 2026? How is Avanza Sigma priced (0.55% to 0.85% total fee) against the incumbent discretionary offerings it targets, and what has early adoption shown about price sensitivity? Focus: Whether the compression of Avanza's revenue per unit in 2026 (brokerage yield, fund margin and income per customer) is cyclical and reversible with volatility and sentiment, as management says, or structural and continuing because of the mix shift to affluent customers and index funds and the entry of cheaper niche platforms, and what the realistic path of the revenue margin on savings capital (about 45 basis points in 2025) is over the next four years under each operating condition.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Avanza's income statement is a set of loops between customer risk appetite, deposits, net interest income, the leverage ratio, fund mix and fee margin, and the third quarter of 2026 showed several of them running at once (risk-on reduced deposits and liquidity income while lifting fund volumes at a lower margin), so the second set's Analyst needs the loops mapped and their precedents dated before building the model. Peers: Nordnet (SAVE-SE), the direct peer with the same deposit, leverage-ratio and mix loops across four markets and a social network (Shareville) that runs an engagement loop Avanza lacks; AJ Bell (AJB-GB), the precedent for a reinvestment flywheel in which scale economies are cut into lower fees that attract flows; FinecoBank (FBK-IT), the precedent for an in-house manufacturing loop and for the "false friend" in which custody and deposit inflows grow while fee income does not; Hargreaves Lansdown (private), the precedent for a loop that turned balancing when fee cuts followed inflow slowdown. Inputs: The wallet-share loop: do existing customers who add products (pensions, mortgages, Sigma, margin lending) bring more net inflow and churn less, and what is the evidence (two-thirds of third-quarter 2026 net inflows came from existing customers)? The risk-appetite and deposit loop: when customers move cash into funds and shares, deposits and liquidity income fall and the leverage ratio improves, and the reverse in a sell-off; how large and how fast has this run in 2024 to 2026 and in 2020 to 2022, and does it stabilise or amplify earnings? The passive-mix loop: lower fund fees attract more fund inflow at a lower margin; is there a floor, and what have Nordnet and Avanza's own index funds shown? The leverage-ratio loop: deposit growth consumes regulatory headroom, which forces AT1 issuance or deposit-rate cuts, which push cash into funds; how has it operated since the Sparkonto+ wind-down? The scale and cost loop: does each year of savings-capital growth mechanically lower the cost-to-savings ratio, or does reinvestment absorb it (Avanza 14.5 to 14.1 basis points; Nordnet 15 to 16)? The brand and word-of-mouth loop: what share of new customers arrives without paid marketing at Avanza and Nordnet, and does it weaken as penetration passes 20% of the population? The Danish loop: can an entrant start a word-of-mouth loop against an incumbent with 9% penetration, with precedents? The Private Banking and lending loop: do mortgages and margin lending anchor affluent assets, and what happened to affluent inflows when Avanza paused mortgage applications in the third quarter of 2026? The accounting loop at Nordnet: does software capitalisation flatter margins today and store amortisation for later, and how does that compare with Avanza's expensing? Focus: Which of these loops are reinforcing for Avanza's earnings over the next eighteen months to four years and which are balancing, with the evidence that each is running now, and in particular whether the combination of risk-on flows, passive mix and affluent-customer mix forms a balancing loop that caps income per customer even as savings capital compounds.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The company gives no earnings guidance, so the earnings-per-share bridge rests on a handful of inputs the sell-side models very differently (FY2027 EPS estimates run from SEK 19.3 to SEK 22.9), and the second set's Analyst needs each input's history, management's statement on it and the sell-side's assumption side by side. Peers: Nordnet (SAVE-SE), the direct peer with published rate sensitivities, deposit betas, a buyback programme and a leverage-ratio target of 4.0% to 4.5%; FinecoBank (FBK-IT), the precedent for net interest income stabilising after rate cuts through volume and for a 70% to 80% payout; AJ Bell (AJB-GB), the precedent for a published base-rate sensitivity and for buybacks alongside a progressive dividend; flatexDEGIRO (FTK-DE), the precedent for capitalised development costs unwinding into amortisation. Inputs: Savings-capital growth decomposed into market return and net inflow by year since 2019 and by month since 2024, with Avanza's share of Swedish market inflows and the company's 2030 target of 15% a year and SEK 2,000bn. The revenue margin on savings capital by line (net interest income, brokerage, fund commissions, currency, other) by quarter since 2019, and what management and each broker assume for 2026 to 2028. Net interest income: the company's published sensitivity (about SEK 420m per point of cuts and about SEK 1.1bn per point of hikes in 2026), the treasury portfolio's size (about SEK 85bn), composition and duration, deposit volumes (about SEK 112bn in September 2026) and their interest-bearing share, the pass-through assumed by Morgan Stanley, DNB Carnegie, Deutsche Bank and Jefferies, and the Riksbank's path as the brokers and the forward curve had it in October 2026. Operating costs: the 9% Swedish guide for 2026, the 8% average to 2030, the Danish cost by year (SEK 40m in 2026 on the company's October 2026 statement), the cloud budget, and each broker's cost line for 2026 to 2028. The tax rate: why it is about 14.5% (yield tax on the insurance company's income), what would move it, and whether any broker models a bank levy. Below the line: the AT1 coupons on SEK 1.3bn of notes at three-month STIBOR plus 2.85% and plus 3.25% and their treatment in EPS; warrant dilution (a 1.6% cap across live programmes; 466,673 shares issued in September 2025) and the share count brokers use; the dividend policy (70% of profit, 76% paid for 2025) and whether any broker models use of the 10% buyback authorisation. The leverage ratio (4.8% in September 2026 against a 3.5% requirement; about SEK 46bn of deposit headroom on the company's estimate) as a constraint on deposit growth, lending growth and payout, and management's statements on how it would manage a breach. Transactional velocity: turnover per customer, trades per customer and the foreign share of turnover by month since 2019, their relationship to market volatility, and what each broker assumes for 2027. Income per customer (SEK 2,170 annualised in the third quarter of 2026) by segment where disclosed. Focus: For each input, how far apart the company's own statements and the sell-side's assumptions are for 2026 to 2028, which inputs the FY2027 estimate dispersion is concentrated in, and what evidence from Avanza's own history and from the peers says about the most likely value of each over the next eighteen months to four years.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 13,910 words · written 23 October 2026, 14:55 to 15:14 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Avanza (AZA-SE): second set of questions, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Investment Analyst, 2026-10-23. Written for the Analyst assessing the predictability of the forecasts (third set) and their reflection in guidance, consensus and narrative (fourth set). All money in SEK; the company's own income statement is the base (operating income FY2025 SEK 4,495m, operating profit SEK 3,078m, EPS SEK 16.57), not FactSet's standardised lines. "Consensus" means the FactSet median of 13 October 2026, which pre-dates the Q3 print of 16 October; where a broker has published since, I say so.

How to read this set

The first set established the shape of the case: a top-decile franchise whose volume lines (customers, savings capital, inflows, deposits) are highly predictable and whose monetisation per unit (brokerage yield, fund margin, currency margin, income per customer) turned down in Q3 2026 while costs are guided to grow 8% to 9% a year regardless. This set turns that shape into numbers. The one organising idea the Consumer should hold throughout is this: Avanza's operating profit is the difference between two growth rates that are set by different forces. Revenue growth is set by savings-capital compounding (structural, 10% to 12% a year) minus per-unit yield erosion (structural, about 1 basis point of revenue margin a year) plus or minus the activity cycle and the policy rate (cyclical, worth plus or minus 10% of revenue in a year). Cost growth is set by management at 8% to 9% in Sweden plus a dated Danish bill, and it does not respond to revenue within a year. Every answer below is a statement about how those two growth rates will stand against each other in each of the next five fiscal years, and the base case in P-10 is simply the arithmetic of that comparison.

Where I disagree with the first set I say so in the answer concerned. The two material disagreements are: (1) the revenue margin on savings capital should be carried at about 42 basis points for 2027 drifting to about 38 by 2030, not 43 to 44 drifting to 42, because the Q3 2026 level (41.3 basis points annualised) is the new base once the H1 2026 velocity is removed and only part of it is seasonal; and (2) the base-case EPS path is therefore about SEK 19.1, 20.2, 21.7 for FY2026 to FY2028, slightly below the first set's SEK 19.0, 20.3, 22.0 to 22.5 in the outer year, with 2027 the squeeze year in which the Danish bill and the cost escalator meet a normalising revenue line.

Financial forecasts

C519 questions
P-0146P-0260P-0348P-0432P-0554P-0650P-0855P-0950P-1054

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Taken together, the nine answers describe a company whose forecast is unusually easy to structure and unusually hard to pin down in any single year. It is easy to structure because the model has four moving parts that are each disclosed monthly or quarterly (savings capital, the revenue margin by line, a cost line guided in one number, and a policy rate with a published sensitivity), because below the operating line nothing surprises, and because the balance sheet is a passive consequence of customer behaviour that is now loosening rather than binding. It is hard to pin down because 44% of revenue is transactional and sits on a fixed cost base that grows 8% to 13% a year whatever happens, so that each 10% of revenue is 15% of EPS and the record shows 10% swings in revenue are ordinary. The base case that falls out of the structure is a deceleration, not a deterioration: revenue growth halving to about 8%, the operating margin plateauing at 68% to 69% because the scale loop on costs and the yield drift on revenue cancel, EPS compounding at about 9% a year to about SEK 25 by 2030, and the dividend growing with it at a 75% payout. Against the 13 October consensus the base sits 2% to 5% lower on EPS for FY2026 to FY2028, and the difference is entirely in two assumptions that the Q3 2026 print brought into question: the revenue margin on savings capital (consensus still carries a level that needs the H1 2026 velocity) and the Danish cost phasing (consensus carries it light). The next 18 months are the weakest stretch of the horizon for earnings growth (about 6% in 2027) and the strongest for asset growth, and the pricing power, the feedback loops and the track record all point the same way for that period: lean the revenue line down, trust the cost line, take one rate hike and not two, and expect the operating margin rather than the revenue line to be where the 2027 disappointment shows. For the investment case the financial forecasts are therefore modestly negative over 18 months (growth decelerating faster than consensus allows, with downside skew from the activity cycle and no cost flexibility) and neutral to modestly positive over four years (a 9% compounder with a 4% to 4.5% yield, a loosening balance sheet, an unpriced Danish option and a 2030 cost cap that restores operating leverage just beyond the horizon). The whole case rests on the market's willingness to pay for the structural half of the business while the cyclical half normalises, which is the question the third and fourth sets must answer.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how it shapes the main line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

❌ D46

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement. The operating leverage is as high as the first set found (roughly 90% of any revenue change reaches operating profit within the year), but over the next 18 months it works against earnings rather than for them, and over the four years it is roughly neutral in the base case until 2030. The reason is simple and the Consumer should carry it as the central fact of the model: the cost line is an escalator fixed in advance (9% in 2026, about 8% in 2027, stepping down to 5% by 2030, plus a Danish bill of about SEK 130m to 200m a year from 2027), so the operating margin moves by about 0.3 points for every 1 point by which revenue growth exceeds or falls short of cost growth. In 2023 to 2025 revenue grew 15% a year against costs at 11%, so the margin rose from 56% (2022) to 68.5% (2025): that was operating leverage working for the company. In the base case for 2027, revenue grows about 8% against costs at about 13% (8% Swedish plus the Danish step-up from SEK 40m to about SEK 130m), so the margin falls about 1.3 points to about 68%; in 2028 and 2029 the two growth rates are close and the margin is flat; only in 2030, when the Swedish cost growth reaches 5% and the Danish marketing cap lapses, does the margin rise again (to about 69.5%). The leverage therefore stops being an earnings driver and becomes purely an error-bar setter: a 10% revenue shortfall in any year costs about 15% of EPS, and a 10% beat adds about 15%.

The mechanism, quantified. The cost base is SEK 1,413m (2025), about SEK 1,575m (2026) and about SEK 1,780m (2027 with Denmark). Personnel is 60% to 62% (SEK 682m in nine months 2026; 812 employees, +15% in a year), IT systems and licences 14% to 15% (rising with the cloud migration, SEK 300m through 2030 and expensed), external services 7% (flat as a share for two years, and sticky because it includes compliance and audit), depreciation about 7%, marketing 2% to 3%, and the only genuinely variable items are transaction costs and payment commissions (transaction costs fell 11.5% to SEK 138m in 2023 when volumes fell; payment commissions rose in Q3 2026). On that structure about 90% of the cost base is fixed within a year. The incremental operating margin on each year's revenue change in the base case is therefore the mirror of the cost escalator: 76% in 2026 (revenue +SEK 670m, costs +SEK 162m), 53% in 2027 (revenue +SEK 435m, costs +SEK 205m, because Denmark adds about SEK 90m on top of the 8% Swedish growth), 69% in 2028, 70% in 2029 and 82% in 2030. An incremental margin below the 68% average dilutes the margin; above it expands it. That is the whole story of the operating margin over the five years: 69.5% (2026, flattered by the H1 2026 activity peak and a seasonally light Q3), 68.2% (2027), 68.3% (2028), 68.4% (2029), 69.4% (2030).

Against the last four to six years. The record contains both directions. In 2019 to 2021 income rose close to 180% while costs rose 30% and the margin reached 74%; in 2022 income fell 10%, costs rose 19% to SEK 1,031m and operating profit fell 21%, the margin dropping to 65%; in 2023 to 2025 income rose 15% a year, costs 11% a year, and the margin recovered to 68.5%. Nordnet in 2022 held cost growth to 6% while income fell 7%, so its profit fell only slightly: the peer shows that a platform can throttle costs in a down year, and Avanza's own record shows that it did not. The next four years differ from the last four in two respects. First, the revenue tailwinds of 2023 to 2025 (a rate cycle that took NII from SEK 321m in 2020 to SEK 1.6bn in 2023, the deposit internalisation of 2025 to 2026 that converted a 5 basis point distribution fee into a 50 basis point spread, and the H1 2026 activity peak) are in the base and not repeatable; the forward revenue growth is the structural 7.5% to 8.5%, not 15%. Second, the cost line carries an expansion bill (Denmark, cloud) that the 2023 to 2025 line did not. The combination means that the margin expansion of the last three years (12 points) is followed by a margin plateau, and the Consumer should not model any operating leverage benefit before 2030 in the base case. The sector digest's rule applies exactly: a cost cap is operating leverage only where there is no expansion bill, and Avanza has one from 2027.

How it shapes each line.

  • Revenue: unaffected by the leverage itself; the leverage acts on the gap between revenue and the escalator.
  • Operating costs: SEK 1,575m (2026), about 1,780m (2027), about 1,910m (2028), about 2,045m (2029), about 2,130m (2030); cost-to-savings capital falls from 13.9 basis points (2025) to about 11.7 (2030) only because capital compounds at 10% to 12% while costs grow 6% to 8%; this is the one place where scale shows.
  • Operating profit: about SEK 3,590m (2026, +17%), 3,820m (2027, +6%), 4,115m (2028, +8%), 4,430m (2029, +8%), 4,830m (2030, +9%). The 2027 deceleration to 6% is the leverage working in reverse and is the single most important number for the third set, because it is the year the market will test.
  • EPS: follows operating profit less a 14.5% tax rate and about SEK 70m of AT1 coupons, so the same shape.

The range the leverage creates. Around the 2027 base (revenue SEK 5,600m, costs SEK 1,780m), a plus or minus 10% revenue outcome (SEK 5,040m to 6,160m) gives operating profit of SEK 3,260m to 4,380m, or minus 15% to plus 15% against the base, and EPS of about SEK 17.2 to 23.1. The company's own sensitivities compose that range: 2 points of trading volume about SEK 0.25 a share, 5 points of cross-border mix about SEK 0.35, a point of policy rate SEK 420m down (about SEK 2.3 a share) or up to SEK 1.1bn up (about SEK 5.9 a share before pass-through). A 2022-type year applied to 2027 (revenue down 8% on 2026 rather than up 8%) gives operating profit of about SEK 2,970m, down 17% year on year, and EPS of about SEK 15.7: the downside is larger than the upside in practice because costs would still rise 13% in that year (Denmark is committed; management has never cut), while on the upside the same fixed costs let a velocity recovery drop through almost entirely.

Why the asymmetry matters more now than in 2022. In 2022 the cost base was SEK 1,031m against income of SEK 2,973m (a 35% cost ratio); in 2027 it is SEK 1,780m against about SEK 5,600m (32%). The cost ratio is lower, which reduces the leverage multiplier slightly (from about 1.5x to about 1.45x), but the absolute fixed cost at risk is 70% larger and 44% of revenue is transactional, so the krona amount of profit exposed to a trading normalisation is larger than it has ever been. The leverage is a two-edged sword that has just been sharpened on the cost side.

For the model. Carry costs as an escalator independent of revenue (Part 1 of the forecast inputs encodes it through the margin), expect the habitual 1% to 2% beat on the cost guide (worth SEK 15m to 35m a year, under 1% of profit), and run the plus or minus 10% revenue case as the standard range rather than as a tail. The Consumer should treat the 2027 operating margin (base 68.2%) as the line most likely to disappoint against consensus (69.5%), because consensus costs look SEK 50m to 100m light on the Danish phasing and consensus revenue assumes a higher revenue margin than Q3 showed.

P-02

Financial leverage: how it shapes EPS and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years

C60

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Conventional financial leverage contributes nothing to EPS and will not, and the regulatory leverage ratio, which is the real constraint, is loosening rather than tightening over the horizon, so it stops rationing growth from about 2027 and opens a modest capital-return option from 2028 that management has not yet claimed. The balance sheet grows with customers (deposits) and with affluent lending, not with leverage; return on equity drifts from 40% to 44% today toward 37% to 39% by 2030 as equity compounds faster than earnings at a 75% payout; and the AT1 coupons are a 2% permanent drag on EPS that the first set correctly identified. The one way financial leverage could shape EPS materially is through a buyback, and the arithmetic says the leverage ratio will allow one by 2028 if deposits stay below 9% of savings capital; whether management uses it is a question of character, and the first set's reading (a distributor, not a repurchaser) is the right base case.

What the leverage actually is. No borrowings other than SEK 1.3bn of perpetual AT1 (SEK 800m at three-month STIBOR plus 3.25% from 2025; SEK 500m at plus 2.85% from March 2026) and small leases; FactSet's net cash of SEK 1,489m at June 2026 is a holding-company cash figure and FactSet's "total debt" of SEK 118.8bn (FY2026 consensus) rising to SEK 138.7bn (FY2028) is customer deposits, not debt. Net financial items are therefore nil in the operating sense and the model should carry only the AT1 coupon (about SEK 60m in 2026, SEK 70m a year from 2027 at STIBOR 2.25%), which is charged to equity below the line and reduces EPS attributable to ordinary shareholders by about SEK 0.40 a share; DNB Carnegie deducts it, which is part of why its FY2026 EPS (SEK 18.90) sits below others. The constraint that matters is the non-risk-based leverage ratio: 4.8% at September 2026 (4.2% in March 2026, 4.4% at end-2025) against a 3.5% requirement including Pillar 2 guidance, on an exposure measure of about SEK 125bn to 133bn, with risk-weighted exposure only SEK 20.9bn and a total capital ratio of 29%. The company puts the deposit headroom at about SEK 46bn.

How it moves over the horizon: the arithmetic. Tier 1 capital of about SEK 6.0bn to 6.4bn (equity about SEK 9.5bn at end-2026 on consensus, less deductions, plus SEK 1.3bn AT1) grows by retained earnings of about SEK 750m to 1,000m a year at a 75% payout, which adds about 55 to 70 basis points of ratio a year on a SEK 130bn exposure. Exposure grows with deposits and lending: deposits of SEK 111.9bn growing with customers at 7% to 8% a year (about SEK 8bn to 9bn a year if deposits per customer are flat) and lending of SEK 38.2bn growing 12% to 15% (about SEK 5bn a year) consume about 35 to 45 basis points a year. Net, the ratio rises by about 15 to 30 basis points a year in a flat-risk-appetite world, toward 5.2% to 5.5% by end-2028, which is above Nordnet's 4.0% to 4.5% target range. Two forces can change that path. A sustained risk-on period drains deposits (every SEK 8bn to 10bn of customer buying restores 30 to 50 basis points) and accelerates the loosening; a crash with a dash to cash (exposure toward SEK 168bn in the risks report's scenario) could consume the headroom within a quarter or two and would require either deposit-rate cuts, a third AT1 issue, or a payout cut. The important point for the model is that the base path loosens, which means the Q3 2026 mortgage pause for the SEK 3m to 10m tier is a 2026 to 2027 event and not a permanent ceiling: by 2028 the constraint on lending-driven NII growth is appetite for affluent credit risk, not capital.

The balance-sheet lines to carry. Deposits SEK 112bn (September 2026) to about SEK 120bn (end-2027) and SEK 130bn to 135bn (end-2028), with the interest-bearing share drifting up from 61% (it was 60% in Q2, and it rises as savings-account customers grow faster than transactional cash). Lending SEK 38bn to about SEK 44bn (2027) and SEK 50bn (2028), split roughly half mortgages (SEK 19.5bn, +38% to 44% year on year, and the pause will slow that to 15% to 20%) and half margin lending (demand-driven, counter-cyclical with cash). Treasury portfolio about SEK 81bn to 85bn (84% covered bonds, duration under 0.25 years, repricing with three-month STIBOR). Shareholders' equity about SEK 9.5bn (2026), 10.5bn (2027), 11.5bn (2028), consistent with consensus book value per share of SEK 55.9, 63.3, 70.9. Credit losses nil (none since 2011; loan-to-value ratios under 50%). AT1 SEK 1.3bn, with a possible third issue of SEK 300m to 500m only in the crash case.

What it does to EPS. Three effects, all small. (1) The AT1 coupon: SEK 60m to 70m a year, about 2% of net income, permanent. (2) Dilution from warrants: about 0.4% a year, capped at 1.6% across live programmes, so the share count moves from 157.8m (end-2025) to about 160.6m by 2030; no buyback offsets it in the base case. (3) The payout: 76% for 2025 against a 70% policy; management's stated preference is to lift the payout above 70% rather than buy back, so I carry 75% (DPS about SEK 14.3 for 2026, 15.2 for 2027, 16.3 for 2028), slightly below consensus (SEK 14.52, 15.41, 16.93), which seems to run the payout toward 74% to 76% on a higher EPS. The return on equity consequence: equity grows about 8% to 10% a year while net income grows 6% to 9%, so ROE falls gently from 40% (2025) and about 42% (2026) toward 38% to 39% (2028) and 37% to 38% (2030), still above the 35% minimum target and the best in the peer set.

The option the loosening creates, and why it is worth carrying as a scenario rather than a base. If the ratio reaches 5.2% to 5.5% by 2028, the company could hold a 4.5% ratio and return about SEK 1bn of surplus (about 1.6% of the market capitalisation) through the unused 10% buyback authorisation, which would add about 1.5% to EPS once. The reasons not to put it in the base: management has shown no appetite (the authorisation has been renewed and never used; the stated tool is the payout ratio); Denmark and the cloud absorb management's attention through 2028; and the same risk-on world that frees the capital also drains deposits and NII, so the buyback would be an offset to a weaker NII line rather than additive. Nordnet's practice (SEK 1bn of buybacks over eighteen months at a 4.0% to 4.5% target; a 5.1% ratio now) is the template that the market will use to ask the question of Avanza at the April 2027 and April 2028 AGMs.

Against the last four to six years. Financial leverage was never a driver (no debt in 2019 to 2024), and the regulatory constraint only became binding in 2025 to 2026 when SEK 40bn of Sparkonto+ deposits came on balance sheet and the ratio fell to 4.2%; the two AT1 issues and the mortgage pause were the response. The next four years reverse that: the one-off internalisation is complete, the ratio rebuilds, and the balance sheet returns to being a passive consequence of customer behaviour. The shift from "constrained" to "loosening" is a modest positive that neither the first set nor the sell-side has emphasised.

P-03

Scope for cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the line items, and when

❌ D48

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement. Very little reaches the income statement as a reduction in cost over the horizon, and what reaches it does so as a deceleration in cost growth from 2028, not as a cut. Management's stated and demonstrated practice is to reinvest efficiency ("more initiatives than capacity"; Nordnet's AI gains are reinvested the same way), the two largest technology programmes (cloud at about SEK 50m a year, and the Danish platform at SEK 130m to 200m a year) add cost rather than remove it, and the one quantified efficiency promise (Swedish cost growth at 5% by 2030) is dated at the far end of the horizon. The amount that reaches the lines is therefore: (1) the habitual guide beat of 1% to 2% of the cost line each year (SEK 15m to 35m, under 1% of profit), already in the base; (2) from 2028, the step-down of Swedish cost growth from about 8% to about 7%, 6.5% and 5%, which against an 8% straight line is worth about SEK 15m in 2028, SEK 45m in 2029 and SEK 100m in 2030, or 0.3, 1.0 and 2.0 points of operating margin cumulatively by 2030; and (3) nothing from AI, cloud or the Danish build before 2029, because none of it is quantified by the company and the peer evidence (Nordnet's 14% fall in development cost per pull request, reinvested) says the gains go into cadence, not into the cost line. The efficiency that does show in the numbers is the scale ratio: cost-to-savings capital falls from 13.9 basis points (2025) to about 11.7 (2030) because capital compounds faster than costs, and that is the mechanism by which Avanza's operating margin can hold at 68% to 69% while its revenue margin falls about 6 basis points over five years.

Line by line, what is cuttable and what will be cut.

  • Personnel (SEK 910m annualised, 60%): headcount +15% in a year to 812 with a 4% salary round; management says 2027 additions will be smaller, which is the deceleration already in the 8% guide. In a downturn a hiring freeze holds the line at about 4% growth; a 5% to 10% reduction is feasible (flatexDEGIRO cut personnel expenses 14.9% in H1 2026 by moving roles to lower-cost countries) but has no precedent at Avanza, whose 2022 response was to keep growing costs 19%. Will not be cut in the base; the deceleration is the only efficiency that reaches the line.
  • IT and licences (about SEK 235m, 15%): rising, not falling. Management says cloud hosting will cost more than on-premise; the migration (SEK 300m to 2030, SEK 50m a year, expensed) buys developer speed and scalability, not savings. Licence costs scale with activity, so they flex a little in a quiet year. After 2030 the cloud budget lapses, which is worth about 2.5% of the 2030 cost base in 2031: outside the horizon.
  • External services (about SEK 100m, 7%): the most cuttable line (consultants) and the source of the 2024 beat; a temporary uptick in Q4 2025 for the migration; the Danish under-budget (SEK 40m against 50m in 2026) came from using internal staff instead of consultants. Expect it to stay at 7% and to be the swing line in each year's beat.
  • Denmark (SEK 40m in 2026; about SEK 130m in 2027; about SEK 150m in 2028; about SEK 175m in 2029; about SEK 170m in 2030 as the three-year marketing cap of SEK 60m a year lapses): the opposite of elimination. It is entirely discretionary before launch and committed after; a deferral would save 2% to 3% of pre-tax profit, and management has given no sign of one. The first set carried SEK 40m, about 120m and about 140m for 2026 to 2028; I carry slightly more in 2027 and 2028 (about SEK 130m and 150m) because the remaining pre-launch budget (SEK 80m to 110m, 80% expensed) falls mostly in H1 2027 and the running cost (SEK 80m "and rising") and marketing (up to SEK 60m) start in H2 2027, so 2027 is about SEK 70m pre-launch plus SEK 40m running plus SEK 30m marketing. Jefferies' three-year total of SEK 340m to 380m (against Nordnet's SEK 200m to 230m for Germany) is consistent with my phasing.
  • Marketing (2% to 3%) and transaction costs: small; marketing is word of mouth (acquisition cost about SEK 590 against Nordnet's SEK 850); transaction costs are the only line that falls with volume (minus 11.5% in 2023) and they offset perhaps 3% to 5% of a brokerage decline.

Realistic cuttable amount if it were ever needed. 10% to 15% of the cost base (SEK 180m to 270m) within twelve months without damaging the franchise: Denmark deferral (SEK 100m-plus), halving external services (SEK 50m), a hiring freeze against the plan (SEK 50m to 80m), and licence and marketing trims. That would offset a 4% to 6% revenue decline. It is a contingency, not a plan: the company's response to 2022 was to hold cost growth, not reverse it, and the strategic commitments (a second platform, a second country, a cloud migration with a committed vendor) make a reversal in 2027 to 2028 less likely than in 2022, not more. The model should therefore carry costs growing 6% to 8% even in a downturn year; the third set's cone should not include a cost-cut rescue.

Peer precedents on the ceiling. FinecoBank's cost-to-income of 27% and Nordnet's 14 basis point cost margin (last twelve months, after capitalising SEK 178m of personnel cost) show that the operating-cost ceiling is above Avanza's current level; Hargreaves Lansdown's GBP 175m five-year remediation shows what deferred back-end investment costs when it comes due, which is the argument for Avanza's current spending; flatexDEGIRO's personnel reset shows a cut is possible at the price of a slowing franchise. The pattern across the set is that platforms outperform on cost through caps delivered against double-digit revenue, not through cuts, and Avanza is the exemplar of that pattern: four years of cost guides met or beaten (2022 minus 2.7%, 2023 minus 0.7%, 2024 9.2% against 9.5%, 2025 10.4% against 11%).

When it reaches the lines. 2026 to 2027: nothing beyond the 1% to 2% beat; the cost ratio to revenue rises in 2027 (from 30.5% to about 31.8%). 2028 to 2029: the Swedish growth step-down begins to show (cost ratio flat at about 31.7%); the Danish line is still rising. 2030: Swedish growth at 5%, Danish marketing lapsing, cloud budget in its last year; cost ratio falls to about 30.6% and the operating margin rises about 1 point. 2031 onward (outside the horizon): the cloud budget ends, Denmark reaches scale or is reassessed, and the cost growth could fall toward 4% to 5% against 7% to 8% revenue growth, which is where the 72% to 75% margin the first set mentioned would come from. For the next 18 months the Consumer should expect the efficiency narrative to be entirely about delivery against the guide (the 2027 cost guide on 22 January 2027 is the dated test; the sector digest names it as the re-rating test for the whole cohort), and not about any reduction.

Disagreement with the first set. None of substance; I phase Denmark slightly heavier in 2027 and 2028 (SEK 130m and 150m against about 120m and 140m), and I would put the 2030 margin at about 69.5% rather than at the 72% to 75% the first set associated with the 5% cost cap, because that cap is reached only in 2030 itself and the Danish running cost is still rising then.

P-04

Pricing power: how price contributes to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years

❌ D32

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Price contributes negatively to revenue in every year of the horizon, by about 1.0 to 1.5 points of revenue growth a year (SEK 55m to 85m), and the model should carry it as a steady drift in the revenue margin rather than as any step. The erosion is structural (it comes from the strategic mix the company is deliberately building: affluent customers who pay 12.5 basis points for currency and trade in cheaper fee classes, index funds at 22 basis points falling, Sigma at half the incumbent price) and partly cyclical (the Q3 2026 brokerage yield of 10.3 basis points is depressed by a quiet quarter in which the affluent share of trading rose to 30%). The only positive price levers are on the funding side (deposit pricing, used at 75% to 80% pass-through on the way down and lagged on the way up, which is where the November 2026 hike adds value) and at the edge of the lending book (the 10 basis point mortgage rise for the SEK 3m to 10m tier, which is capacity management, not pricing power). Volume must therefore do all the work: savings capital must compound at 11% to 12% a year for revenue to grow 8%. That is exactly the relationship the first set's C-17 and C-45 described, and the second-set evidence sharpens it: the question is not whether price contributes, but how much of the Q3 compression is the new base, and my answer is about two-thirds of it.

The decomposition of the Q3 2026 compression, line by line, and what persists.

  • Brokerage yield (gross brokerage over brokerage-generating turnover): 11.4 basis points (Q2 2025), 10.6 (Q1 2026), 11.0 (Q2), 10.3 (Q3). The Q2-to-Q3 fall of 0.7 basis points is explained by the Private Banking and Pro share of trading rising from 26% to 30% and by larger average trades in the fixed-fee class. Of the 0.7, about 0.3 is cyclical (when retail re-engages the affluent share falls back toward 26% to 27%) and about 0.4 is structural (the affluent segment, under 2% of customers and over 45% of savings capital, is where the strategy and the growth are; its trading share trends up, and Nordnet's commission-free weeks and 15 basis point foreign-exchange campaign and SAVR's and Montrose's pricing act at the same end). Base case: 10.5 basis points in 2027 (DNB Carnegie's 10.6 is the same reading), drifting to 10.0 by 2030. Not back to 11.
  • Currency margin on foreign turnover: 19 basis points (Q2 2026) to 18 (Q3) against a 25 basis point list price for standard customers and 12.5 for Private Banking and Pro manual currency accounts (launched February 2025, adoption slower than feared in 2025, now visibly spreading). This is the purest hidden-fee compression in the model: the list price holds (which the sector digest says is the durable kind), and the blended rate falls as the share routed through the cheaper account rises. Base case: 17.5 basis points in 2027, 16 by 2030. The 31% foreign share of turnover (stable through 2026, up structurally over years) partly offsets this at the revenue level.
  • Fund margin: 25.0 basis points (2025 average on Deutsche Bank's basis), 23.3 (Q3 2025 on the company's basis), 22.9 (Q2 2026), 22.2 (Q3 2026), with the index share of fund capital at 55.5% (49.7% a year earlier). This is entirely structural and continuing at about 0.6 to 0.8 basis points a year while the index share rises 5 to 6 points a year; it would slow only when the index share plateaus (Morgan Stanley's 2023 observation that an 8% rise in fund assets produced 1% fund-fee growth is the historical analogue). The offset is own funds (41% of fund capital at Avanza; Nordnet's own funds take 50% of its incremental inflows at 31% of stock), which hold the blended margin above where third-party index distribution alone would put it. Base case: 21.5 basis points in 2027, 20 by 2030; fund commissions still grow 11% to 14% a year because fund capital grows 15% to 18%.
  • Deposit pricing: average deposit rate 0.81% in Q3 2026 (0.79% in Q2) on a 61% interest-bearing share; pass-through of cuts was 100% on the first 25 basis points (May 2024) and 75% to 80% thereafter, exceeding brokers' expectations, and a February 2025 savings-rate cut of 25 basis points saved SEK 40m a year. This is real, demonstrated pricing power, exercised against the company's own customers on the liability side, and it is why the NII sensitivity is asymmetric (SEK 420m per point down, SEK 1.1bn per point up): on a hike the company will lag the pass-through, so a 25 basis point hike is worth about SEK 160m a year net (SEK 275m gross less about SEK 110m of deposit cost at a 65% pass-through on the interest-bearing 61%). Base case: the November 2026 hike to 2.0% is passed through at about 60% to 70% with a lag of one to two quarters.
  • Lending pricing: the mortgage rate for the SEK 3m to 10m tier rose 10 basis points from 1 November 2026 alongside the application pause, and the book still grew 44% year on year, so affluent price sensitivity is low; but the move is capacity rationing under the leverage ratio and will reverse when the ratio loosens (P-02), so no revenue contribution should be modelled.
  • Sigma: 0.55% to 0.85% total fee against incumbent discretionary pricing of roughly twice that; SEK 710m in a month, partly transferred from existing Avanza holdings. It is price-led share-taking in a SEK 1,000bn pool, margin-accretive per krona of new money and margin-dilutive per krona transferred. Base case: SEK 3bn to 5bn by 2028, worth SEK 20m to 35m of revenue, in "other income"; option value beyond.

The aggregate: the revenue margin on savings capital. 44.2 basis points (FY2025: SEK 4,495m on average capital of about SEK 1,017bn), about 43.2 (FY2026: about SEK 5,165m on about SEK 1,195bn), about 41.8 (2027), 40.6 (2028), 39.4 (2029), 38.2 (2030) in the base case. Of the roughly 1.2 basis points a year of decline, about 0.8 is price and mix (the four lines above) and about 0.4 is the mechanical effect of market appreciation inflating the denominator (capital grows with markets; brokerage and NII do not scale with market levels one for one). The first set's 43 to 44 for 2027 drifting 1 basis point a year is where I disagree: that path required the H1 2026 velocity to persist, and Q3 showed 41.3 with only part of the shortfall seasonal. The historical range of the margin is 34 basis points (2019, zero rates, low activity) to 48 (2021 and 2023), so a path to 38 by 2030 at a 2.0% to 2.25% policy rate and normal activity is well inside the range and should not be read as a collapse. It should be read as what a business that shares scale with its customers looks like when rates and activity are neither helping nor hurting.

Operating conditions and how price behaves in each. In a hiking cycle (from November 2026) price helps through the deposit lag: plus SEK 160m a year per 25 basis points, with a second hike to 2.25% (the forward curve by late 2027, 2.4% on some readings) worth the same again; this is the one condition in which Avanza has upside price power and it is the main upside to my base. In a cutting cycle (2024 to 2025 precedent) price defends NII at 75% to 80% pass-through but cannot defend it below a 0% deposit floor, which is why the downside per point is SEK 420m. In high volatility (H1 2026) the retail share of trading rises, the brokerage yield recovers 0.3 to 0.5 basis points and foreign turnover rises; in low volatility (Q3 2026) the reverse. In risk-on periods deposits fall as a share of savings capital (8.9% in Q3 from 9.6%) and the higher-margin cash is replaced by lower-margin fund capital: a negative price-mix effect of about SEK 25m a quarter per point of deposit share. Under affluent-entrant competition (SAVR, Montrose, Nordnet's private-banking venture with ABG Sundal Collier) the pressure is on the two hidden fees (currency, brokerage class) and shows as drift, not as a price cut; the risk of a list-price cut to the 25 basis point standard currency fee is the one step-change in the model (DNB Carnegie has flagged it as the response if the entrants gain momentum) and would cost SEK 100m to 150m a year; low probability within 18 months. Under RIS implementation (final text January 2027, application July 2029, no inducement ban, a value-for-money test) the pressure on the 17% of income from fund rebates (60% of it Swedish) is slow and regulatory, and it falls after the horizon except as a sentiment overhang. In Denmark Avanza is the entrant against a price-setter earning 49 to 50 basis points; its launch schedule is undisclosed; the likeliest outcome is parity with Nordnet on Danish brokerage and a lower foreign-exchange fee, with Nordnet's response measured (it cut its Danish minimum commission in November 2024 at a cost of SEK 50m a year and says matching Swedish prices would cost about 1% of group income); Danish price has no effect on Avanza's revenue before 2028 and a small effect after.

What the sell-side and the company say. Management calls the Q3 compression "temporary market sentiment"; DNB Carnegie (the closest reader) says the slowdown is seasonal but that heightened Private Banking competition is a concern and that yields are unlikely to return to historical peaks, so growth must come from assets; Deutsche Bank (the bull) says volume will overwhelm yield; Jefferies (the bear) reads structurally lower income per customer; Morgan Stanley argued in 2023 that a 45 basis point revenue yield is unsustainable over the long term. My reading sits between DNB Carnegie and Jefferies: about two-thirds of the Q3 compression is the new base.

For the model. No price contribution to revenue; a revenue-margin drift of about 1.2 basis points a year in the base, of which about 0.8 is price and mix; brokerage yield 10.5 basis points (2027) to 10.0 (2030), fund margin 21.5 to 20, currency margin 17.5 to 16 on a 31% to 32% foreign share, deposit pass-through 60% to 70% on hikes and 75% to 80% on cuts. In the adverse case (a 25 basis point standard currency fee cut, or a 2 basis point a year fund-margin fall under RIS pressure), the drift is 2 basis points a year and 2028 EPS is about 5% lower.

P-05

Reinforcing and balancing feedback loops: how they shape the main line items over the next 18 months to four years

C54

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. The loops at work sort cleanly by which line they act on, and that sorting is the most useful thing the Consumer can take from this answer: every reinforcing loop acts on volume and every balancing loop acts on yield or on capital. The reinforcing loops (wallet share, brand and word of mouth, scale and cost, in-house funds, affluent lending as an anchor) are all running now and together produce savings-capital growth of 10% to 12% a year with high confidence. The balancing loops (risk appetite and deposits, passive mix, affluent mix, the leverage ratio, the penetration ceiling, and the loop between prices and flows that is the moat's own engine) are also all running now, and together they cap income growth at roughly volume growth minus 3 to 4 points. The net of the two sets is the base case's revenue growth of 7.5% to 8.5% a year: the reinforcing loops are stronger in the stock of assets and the balancing loops are stronger in the income per unit of asset, and Q3 2026 was the first quarter in which the market could see both at once (capital +21%, income +12%). The answer to the question the research was commissioned to settle (does the combination of risk-on flows, passive mix and affluent mix form a balancing loop that caps income per customer even as capital compounds?) is yes, and the cap is about SEK 2,100 to 2,300 of annualised income per customer at a 2.0% policy rate, which is where the figure now sits (SEK 2,170 in Q3 from SEK 2,302 in Q2).

The reinforcing loops, with the evidence that each is running and how it enters the model.

  1. Wallet share. Customers hold about one-third of their wealth with Avanza; two-thirds of Q3 2026 net inflows (SEK 23.1bn, +61%) came from existing customers; recurring monthly inflows of SEK 3.4bn plus SEK 410m of pension premiums; churn 0.7%. Each product added (pensions, mortgages, Sigma, margin lending) deepens the relationship and lowers churn further. In the model: net inflow of 5% to 7% of opening capital a year (SEK 70bn to 90bn in 2027), of which two-thirds from the base; this is the most durable line in the forecast.
  2. Brand and word of mouth. Marketing is 2% to 3% of costs; acquisition cost about SEK 590 (SEK 96 to 102 in 2021); 52,800 net new customers in Q3; 38% of Swedes aged 20 to 39 are customers; 16 consecutive Swedish Quality Index wins. The loop weakens with penetration (20.5% of the population; DNB Carnegie expects customer growth to become harder), which is why customer growth is 8% against Nordnet's 12% and why the strategy has shifted to wallet share. In the model: customers +7% to 8% a year, decelerating toward 6% by 2030.
  3. Scale and cost. Cost-to-savings capital 14.5 (2024), 14.1 (2025), about 13.2 (2026), falling to about 11.7 by 2030 in the base. The loop is mechanical only if cost growth stays below capital growth; it did not run in 2022 (capital fell, costs rose 19%) and DNB Carnegie's 2022 forecast that the ratio would stay around 17 basis points was wrong because capital recovered. In the model: this is the operating margin's defence against the revenue-margin drift; it holds the margin at 68% to 69%.
  4. In-house manufacturing. Own funds are 41% of fund capital; Nordnet's own funds take 50% of its incremental inflows; FinecoBank's in-house margin is 78 basis points against 46 for third-party product. The loop (cheap own index funds attract flow, which supports the blended margin against the passive shift) partly offsets balancing loop 2 below. In the model: fund margin falls 0.6 to 0.8 basis points a year rather than 1.0 to 1.5.
  5. Affluent lending as an anchor. Private Banking and Pro are under 2% of customers and over 45% of savings capital; mortgages grew 44% year on year and internally financed lending 11% in Q3 alone despite the pause; lending is a genuine synergy for asset attraction (Jefferies). In the model: lending +12% to 15% a year, NII from lending +SEK 80m to 100m a year; and the pause did not visibly dent affluent inflows in Q3, which is the first evidence that the anchor holds without new credit.

The balancing loops, with the same.

  1. Risk appetite and deposits. When customers buy funds and shares, deposits fall (8.9% of savings capital in Q3 from 9.6%; cash deposits down 4% year on year on Deutsche Bank's tracker), liquidity income falls (Q3 NII 2% below consensus), the leverage ratio improves, and brokerage and fund income rise; in a sell-off the reverse. In 2020 to 2022 the loop was propelled by rate velocity (NII from SEK 321m to SEK 1.6bn); in 2024 to 2026 it is volume-driven and gentler. At a 2.0% policy rate the loop is a genuine stabiliser (a dash to cash adds NII at a 2% yield while trading falls); at a 0.5% rate it would not be, which is why the 2022 downturn had no cushion. In the model: deposits flat to slightly down as a share of capital in the base (risk-neutral); the two-way sensitivity is about SEK 25m a quarter per point of deposit share.
  2. Passive mix. Index funds 55.5% of fund capital (49.7% a year earlier); fund margin 22.2 basis points and falling; record fund inflows of SEK 18bn in Q3 (32% of the Swedish fund market) at the lowest margin yet. Lower fees attract more flow at lower margin; there is a floor only where own funds hold the blend. In the model: described at P-04.
  3. Affluent mix. Private Banking and Pro took 30% of trading in Q3 from 26%; brokerage yield fell to 10.3; currency margin to 18. The strategy (Private Banking leadership, Sigma, mortgage tiers above SEK 30m) deliberately feeds this loop because the affluent bring assets; the price of the assets is a lower yield on trading. In the model: brokerage yield 10.5 basis points in 2027 and drifting.
  4. Leverage ratio. Deposit growth consumed headroom in 2025 to 2026 (ratio 4.2% in March 2026), forcing SEK 1.3bn of AT1 and the mortgage pause; retained earnings and risk-on deposit outflows now rebuild it (4.8% in September). P-02 describes the path; the loop turns from binding to loosening over 2027, so its balancing effect on NII growth fades.
  5. Penetration. At 20% of the population, customer growth slows and acquisition cost rises; the response (wallet share, pensions, Private Banking) feeds loops 1 and 5 above and balancing loop 3. In the model: customer growth 8% decelerating to 6%.
  6. Sharing scale with customers (the moat's own engine). Zero platform fees, a 17 basis point index fund, free K4 forms, Sigma at half price, commission-free trading up to SEK 50,000: the company converts scale into lower prices to drive flow share (11.9% of market inflows against 8.7% of stock). This is simultaneously the strongest reinforcing loop on volume and the strongest balancing loop on yield, and it is the reason the two cannot be separated in the forecast. AJ Bell (dealing fees halved in April 2024, customers +24%) is the precedent of the loop working; Hargreaves Lansdown (a 45 basis point fee held too long, share lost, a forced cut to 35 basis points in March 2026 applied to the back book) is the precedent of what happens when a platform refuses to run it.

The Danish loop. Unproven and cost-first. Nordnet's Danish revenue after five years from its 2001 launch was SEK 82m; Lunar shows that superior design without lending access does not monetise; Nordnet has 9% to 10% population penetration, a pension wrapper and the price-setter's seat. Avanza's word-of-mouth loop cannot start before there is a product (H2 2027), and the marketing budget (SEK 60m a year) is the substitute until it does. In the model: a cost line only, through 2030; the first evidence of a loop would be Danish net inflows above SEK 500m a month by mid-2028.

The accounting loop at Nordnet. Nordnet capitalises SEK 178m of personnel cost a year (capitalised projects SEK 382m at end-2024) and amortises over three to seven years; Avanza expenses almost everything (Sigmastocks' algorithm, under SEK 2m a quarter, is the only material amortising intangible). Nordnet's reported margin (71% to 76% depending on basis) is therefore flattered today and carries amortisation forward; Avanza's 68% to 72% is clean. For the model this means two things: Avanza's margin comparison with Nordnet should be adjusted by 2 to 3 points in Avanza's favour, and Avanza's cost line will not carry a delayed amortisation ramp as Nordnet's and flatexDEGIRO's (EUR 22.4m capitalised in H1 2026; EUR 11.1m amortised in 2025) will.

How the loops shape each line over the horizon.

  • Savings capital: reinforcing loops dominate; SEK 1,277bn (September 2026) to about 1,270bn (end-2026, flat markets in a risk-off quarter), 1,410bn (2027), 1,560bn (2028), 1,730bn (2029), 1,910bn (2030) at 5% market return plus 6% net inflow; the SEK 2,000bn 2030 target is missed by about 5% in the base, as the first set anticipated, and met only with a 7%-plus market or an acceleration of wallet share.
  • Revenue: the balancing loops take 3 to 4 points off volume growth; 7.5% to 8.5% a year.
  • Costs: the scale loop holds the ratio to capital falling; the Danish loop adds a fixed bill.
  • Operating margin: flat at 68% to 69%, the net of the scale loop (positive) and the yield loops (negative).
  • Deposits and NII: the risk-appetite loop makes NII counter-cyclical to brokerage, which stabilises revenue within a range of about plus or minus 3% a year at a 2% policy rate; it does not stabilise it against a rate cut.
  • Income per customer: capped at SEK 2,100 to 2,300 annualised in the base; the Consumer should treat a fall below SEK 2,000 as the signal that the balancing loops have won, and a rise above SEK 2,400 as the signal that the activity cycle has returned.

Reinforcing or balancing on balance? For the stock of assets, strongly reinforcing and visible for the whole horizon. For earnings, balancing over the next 18 months (the yield loops and the Danish loop are both at their strongest, and the scale loop is temporarily overwhelmed by the cost escalator) and modestly reinforcing from 2029 (when the scale loop and the lapsing of the Danish marketing cap outrun the yield drift). The first set's C-47 reached the same conclusion from the competitive-advantage side; the second-set evidence adds the mechanism and the dating.

P-06

Track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts should carry, and in which direction to lean

C50

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement. The forecasts should carry high weight on the cost line and the volume lines, medium weight on the recurring revenue lines, and low weight on the transactional lines and on any single year's EPS; and they should be leaned down for the next 18 months and roughly neutral beyond. The reasoning is the company's own record, read by what it controls. What management controls (costs, capital, products, retention) it has delivered on every time in the last four years, and in a way that is slightly better than guided; what it does not control (trading velocity, the policy rate, market levels) has moved earnings by plus or minus 15% to 20% in a year, and the last four quarters show the symmetric pattern that the first set's C-69 identified (beats of 4% to 5% in active quarters, misses of 2% to 5% in quiet ones). The next two to three quarters begin in quiet, risk-off conditions after an activity peak, with consensus not yet cut, so the lean is down; the forecast error in the base case should be set at plus or minus 5% for FY2026 EPS, plus or minus 10% for FY2027 and plus or minus 15% for FY2028.

The record, sorted by what it says about each line.

  • Costs (weight: very high; lean: slightly below guidance). Cost guides met or beaten in 2022 (minus 2.7% against the floor), 2023 (minus 0.7%), 2024 (9.2% against 9.5%), 2025 (10.4% against 11%); the 2026 Danish cost cut from SEK 50m to 40m with the 9% Swedish guide held for four quarters; first-half 2026 cost growth of 6.6% and a Q3 3% below consensus (phasing, not structural, per DNB Carnegie). The beats come from external services and from internal substitution for consultants; they are small (SEK 10m to 30m) and reliable. The model carries SEK 1,575m for 2026 (about 0.3% under the SEK 1,580m guide) and 1% under the framework thereafter.
  • Volume lines (weight: high; lean: neutral). Customers +8% a year for several years; savings capital share rising about half a point a year (7.8% to 8.7% in 21 months); flow share above stock share (11.9% against 8.7%); churn 0.7% to 0.9%. The one miss is the 15% savings-capital target (13% in 2025; 21% in 2026 with market help); the model's 10% to 12% is below the target and in line with the controllable rate (net inflows about 6% of opening capital on DNB Carnegie's reading).
  • Recurring revenue (NII, fund commissions; weight: medium; lean: NII up, funds neutral). NII has moved with the policy rate exactly as the published sensitivity says (it was SEK 450m per point in 2025 and SEK 420m in 2026, and the 2024 to 2025 cuts were absorbed at 75% to 80% pass-through as guided); the company has never surprised on the mechanism, only on deposit volumes (Q3 2026 minus 2% on lower cash). Fund commissions have grown 17% in the last year on 19% fund-capital growth, a stable relationship. The lean on NII is up if the Riksbank hikes on 4 November (Barclays and Deutsche Bank expect it; the forward curve has 2.25% to 2.4% by late 2027) and the base case includes one hike; a second is upside.
  • Transactional revenue (brokerage, currency; weight: low; lean: down for 12 months). These lines produced the 2022 miss (EPS minus 19%), the Q2 2026 beat (+4%) and the Q3 2026 miss (brokerage minus 5%, currency minus 8% against consensus). Trades were down 10% year on year in September (worse than Nordnet's minus 5% and flatexDEGIRO's minus 3%); daily trades per customer were down 15% year on year; DNB Carnegie expects reduced velocity after October's turbulence; the Fitness for Fighting regime is risk-off. The base case has 2027 brokerage at SEK 1,350m (+2% on 2026) and currency at SEK 810m (minus 1%), both below consensus (SEK 1,394m and 839m).
  • EPS against consensus (weight: medium; lean: down). Last eight quarters: +2%, miss (GDPR), +2%, +4%, minus 2%, +5%, in line, in line, +4%, minus 2%. Average a small beat, but conditional on the activity cycle: in falling or stable volatility after a strong run the company misses by 2% to 5%. The pre-Q3 consensus FY2026 revenue of SEK 5,225m needs a Q4 of about SEK 1,350m against Q3's SEK 1,283m; my Q4 is SEK 1,290m. Post-Q3 revisions so far are trims (DNB Carnegie to SEK 18.90 from 19.04 for FY2026, FY2028 cut to 20.79), and the FactSet series shows FY2027 EPS still up 1% over one month and 13% over twelve, so the estimate cycle has not turned: the dashboard's A rating for sell-side momentum is a lagging reading.

Management's own forecasts and their quality. Avanza does not guide on earnings or revenue; the 2030 targets (SEK 2,000bn, 15% a year; 8% average cost growth; 35% ROE; 70% payout) were set in October 2024 and never moved; the one target with a record, savings capital, was missed in its first year. The CEO's stated practice is to guide only when highly certain, which is why the cost guide is reliable and why no revenue cushion exists in guidance. The communication after Q3 ("another strong quarter"; yield compression "temporary") is more reassuring than the numbers, which the third set should treat as a mild negative for the predictability of management's framing rather than of the numbers themselves.

Peers as a check. Nordnet landed costs on guidance four times running and grew faster, with a 10% pre-tax beat in Q2 2026 and a 2% miss in Q3 2026 (growing fast but landing just short, in Deutsche Bank's words); flatexDEGIRO beat by 14% in Q2 and missed on the top line in Q3; FinecoBank beat by 5% in Q2. The pattern of trading-driven symmetry is sector-wide and the sector's Q3 was softer than its H1, which supports the down-lean for the next two prints.

Which direction, by horizon.

  • Next 18 months (Q4 2026 to Q1 2028): lean down on revenue (2% to 4% below the 13 October consensus for FY2026 and FY2027), slightly down on costs (the beat), up on NII if the hike arrives, and net down 2% to 4% on EPS (my SEK 19.1 and 20.2 against SEK 19.40 and 20.87). The probability that FY2027 consensus EPS is cut by 3% or more before the Q4 2026 print is, in my judgement, about 60%.
  • 18 months to four years (FY2028 to FY2030): lean neutral to slightly down on revenue (the revenue-margin drift), neutral on costs (the guide will be met), neutral on the policy rate (two-sided), and down on the Danish cost phasing (consensus seems to carry it light). My FY2028 EPS of SEK 21.7 is 5% below the consensus SEK 22.88 and 4% above DNB Carnegie's SEK 20.93.

Disagreement with the first set. The first set leaned FY2026 to FY2027 EPS 1% to 3% below consensus; I lean 2% to 4% below, and I add that the lean should be heavier on revenue (3% to 4%) than on EPS because the cost beat gives back about 1%.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items and share count: how they shape EPS, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years

C55

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. Below the operating line Avanza is close to a pass-through: operating profit equals pre-tax profit, tax takes 14.5%, the AT1 coupon takes about 2%, warrants add 0.4% to the share count a year, and 75% of what remains is paid out. Over the horizon those items subtract a steady 2.5% to 3% from the growth that operating profit would otherwise deliver to EPS (0.4% dilution, plus the AT1 coupon fixed in kronor while profit grows, plus no buyback), against a last four to six years in which the below-the-line items were, if anything, a small tailwind (tax fell from 16% in 2021 to 13.5% in 2023 and 14.5% in 2025; no AT1 before 2025; dilution similar). There are no one-offs of consequence in either direction in the base case. Three items deserve a scenario line rather than a base-case entry: a rise in the effective tax rate toward 16% to 17% if a bank tax or a yield-tax change arrives (minus 2% to 3% of EPS), a crossing of the Swedish risk-tax threshold on liabilities around 2029 to 2031 (minus about 2% of pre-tax profit a year once crossed), and a buyback from 2028 if the leverage ratio builds as P-02 expects (plus 1.5% to EPS once). Free cash flow in the FactSet sense is not a meaningful measure for this company (it is customer deposit and lending flow: SEK minus 797m in 2025 against net income of SEK 2,609m), and the cross-check in the forecast inputs is therefore net income, which converts to distributable cash at close to 100% after capital expenditure of SEK 60m to 90m a year.

Tax: why 14.5%, and what moves it. The group pays corporate tax (20.6%) on the bank's and the fund company's profit, but most of Avanza Pension's income bears yield tax levied on policyholder assets and charged to customer accounts rather than corporate tax on the insurer's profit, so the blended rate was 13.8% (2019), 15.2% (2020), 16.0% (2021), 14.1% (2022), 13.5% (2023), 14.0% (2024) and 14.5% (2025). The rate rises when the bank's share of profit rises (brokerage- and NII-heavy years) and falls when the insurance wrapper's share rises; a path in which NII and lending grow faster than the endowment-insurance book nudges it up by perhaps half a point by 2030. I carry 14.5% throughout, with 15% as the sensible outer-year alternative. What would move it materially: (1) a change in the yield-tax treatment or in ISK and endowment-insurance taxation (the post-election debate the CEO named at Q3), which could shift income from the insurer to the bank or alter the wrapper's attraction; (2) a bank tax levied on profit rather than liabilities; (3) the removal, from 1 January 2026, of tax deductibility for interest on margin loans against endowment insurance, which acts on customers' demand for margin lending rather than on Avanza's own rate. The sensitivity: each point of tax rate is 1.2% of EPS.

The risk tax on credit institutions: a dated item nobody models. Sweden's risk tax applies to credit institutions whose liabilities exceed a threshold set at SEK 150bn when it was introduced in 2022 and indexed annually since; the rate is 0.06% of liabilities. Avanza's 2023 annual report states the bank is below the threshold, and the EPS-drivers research found that no broker models it. The relevant liabilities are overwhelmingly customer deposits: SEK 111.9bn at September 2026, which FactSet's "total debt" consensus has at SEK 118.8bn (2026), 128.4bn (2027) and 138.7bn (2028). At 7% to 8% a year the bank's liabilities reach about SEK 150bn around 2029 to 2030 and the indexed threshold (which will by then be higher, perhaps SEK 170bn to 180bn) around 2031 to 2032 in the base; a dash to cash of SEK 30bn to 40bn in a crash would bring the crossing forward by two to three years. Once crossed the tax applies to the whole liability base, so the first year costs about SEK 95m to 110m (0.06% of SEK 160bn to 180bn), about 2% of pre-tax profit, and it grows with deposits thereafter. This is outside my base case but inside the four-year cone, it is the one way the "bank tax" debate reaches Avanza without any new legislation, and the company has a lever against it (deposit pricing to slow balance-sheet growth) that it has said it would use for leverage-ratio reasons anyway. The Consumer should carry it as a 2030-plus scenario line and as a reason the management will not want deposits to grow faster than customers.

Interest and the AT1 coupon. No interest-bearing debt. The AT1 coupons on SEK 1.3bn (three-month STIBOR plus 3.25% on SEK 800m, plus 2.85% on SEK 500m) are about SEK 60m in 2026 (STIBOR averaging about 1.9%) and about SEK 70m a year from 2027 at STIBOR 2.25%, rising SEK 3m per 25 basis points of STIBOR; they are charged to equity under IFRS and reduce earnings attributable to ordinary shareholders, so they belong in EPS; they are not tax-deductible. I carry them as the "net financial items" line in the forecast inputs so that the model's EPS is on the shareholder basis. A third AT1 issue is a crash-case event only (P-02).

One-offs. None recurring. The record: a SEK 15m GDPR fine and SEK 29m of customer compensation for an interest-rate error in 2024; the Stabelo stake sale (SEK 59m proceeds) in 2025; Sigmastocks (SEK 21.1m total consideration, final SEK 6.3m paid July 2026) with an algorithm amortising at under SEK 2m a quarter to August 2031; Q3 2026 other income of SEK 69m (41% above consensus) from Avanza Markets, which I normalise to about SEK 60m a quarter. Reported and FactSet-adjusted EPS differ by 0.2%, development is expensed, and there are no minorities, associates or pension deficits. The Danish start-up is 20% capitalised (about SEK 25m to 30m of the SEK 120m to 150m), which creates a small amortisation charge from launch; immaterial.

Share count and distributions. 157.8m shares at end-2025; warrants capped at 1.6% dilution across live programmes, about 0.4% a year (466,673 shares issued at SEK 201.90 in September 2025; the 2023/2026 programme settled partly in new shares in 2026). I carry +0.6m shares a year: a weighted average of about 158.2m (2026), 158.8m (2027), 159.4m (2028), 160.0m (2029) and 160.6m (2030). DNB Carnegie holds a flat 158.3m; the difference is 0.4% a year of EPS, which is the point. No buyback in the base (the 10% authorisation unused; management's tool is the payout ratio); dividend at 75% of EPS (SEK 14.3, 15.2, 16.3, 17.5, 19.0 for FY2026 to FY2030), which is within the 70% to 82% range of the last three years and below consensus by 1% to 4% because my EPS is lower. Equity therefore compounds at 8% to 10% a year, and the dividend yield at SEK 360 is 4.0% rising to 4.5% on the FY2027 dividend.

Net income and the conversion to cash. Net income: SEK 2,609m (2025), about 3,020m (2026, +16%), 3,210m (2027, +6%), 3,460m (2028, +8%), 3,730m (2029, +8%), 4,070m (2030, +9%). Capital expenditure SEK 74m (2025) and SEK 60m to 90m a year on consensus; no working capital; leases small. Cash available for distribution is net income less retained capital needed for the leverage ratio, which at a 75% payout leaves SEK 750m to 1,000m a year of retention that adds 55 to 70 basis points to the ratio; the conversion of earnings into distributable cash is therefore a capital-policy choice rather than an operating constraint, and the only thing that would break it is a deposit surge. FactSet's free-cash-flow line should not be used for anything.

Against the last four to six years. 2019 to 2025: EPS grew faster than operating profit in 2023 (tax 13.5%) and in line otherwise; dilution 0.4% a year; payout 43% (2020) to 82%; no AT1 until 2025. 2026 to 2030: EPS grows about 2.5% to 3% a year slower than it would with a flat share count, no AT1 and a buyback at Nordnet's scale. The below-the-line layer is therefore a small, steady and predictable drag, and the one item that is new (the AT1) is already in the run rate from Q2 2026. The Consumer can carry this layer with high confidence; its contribution to the forecast error is under 2% of EPS in any year unless the tax regime changes.

P-09

The net likely impact on the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years

C50

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. The net effect of everything above is a change of regime in the growth rates and no change in the quality of the franchise: revenue growth roughly halves (from 15% a year in 2023 to 2026 to 7.5% to 8.5% in 2027 to 2030), the operating margin stops rising (from a 12-point climb in 2022 to 2025 to a plateau at 68% to 69%), and EPS growth falls from mid-teens to 6% to 9% a year, with 2027 the trough year at about 6%. Returns on equity stay near 40%, earnings remain cash, the balance sheet loosens, and the dividend grows with EPS. Against consensus the net impact is a base case 2% to 5% below on EPS for FY2026 to FY2028, concentrated in the revenue margin and in the Danish cost phasing, and with the operating margin (consensus 69.5% flat) the line most likely to disappoint. The next 18 months are therefore the period in which the company's earnings growth is at its lowest since 2022 while its asset growth is at its highest since 2021, and the model and the third set must hold both facts at once.

The line items, last four to six years against the next four, in one view.
