Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 16,093 words · written 24 October 2026, 13:21 to 13:42 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Avanza (AZA-SE): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and their reflection in guidance, consensus and narrative

Investment Analyst, 2026-10-24. Written for the Analyst who writes the investment memo for Richard. All money in SEK; the company's own income statement is the base (operating income FY2025 SEK 4,495m, operating profit SEK 3,078m, EPS SEK 16.57), not FactSet's standardised lines. "Consensus" is the FactSet median of 13 October 2026 (before the Q3 print of 16 October) unless a later date is given; the sell-side revision counts are of 22 October; the share price is SEK 360.1 (21 October, dashboard) where the dashboard is the source and SEK 380.2 (13 October) where the financials block or the Financial Analyst's model is the source. "Our base case" means the second set's base case (EPS SEK 19.1, 20.2, 21.7, 23.3 and 25.3 for FY2026 to FY2030; operating profit SEK 3,590m, 3,820m, 4,115m, 4,430m and 4,830m), which I adopt with the qualifications given in each answer. Where I disagree with the first or second set I say so in the answer concerned; the disagreements of substance are listed at D-01.

Before the answers: the Financial Analyst's model gap, explained and corrected

The one-page summary flags every forecast year. The model's EPS is SEK 24.45, 25.87, 27.80, 29.84 and 32.46 for FY2026 to FY2030 against the second set's cross-check of SEK 19.10, 20.20, 21.70, 23.30 and 25.30; net income SEK 3,866m against 3,020m in FY2026, and so on. The ratio is 1.28 in every year, which points to one cause, not five.

The cause. The model applies Part 1's revenue growth to its own revenue history, which is FactSet's standardised revenue of SEK 5,737m for FY2025: gross interest income before interest expense and before commission expense. The quirks digest and the financials block both say this line does not reconcile with the company's reported operating income (SEK 4,495m; a 21.6% gap, flagged SERIOUS) and must not be used. The second set's operating profit margins (69.5%, 68.2%, 68.3%, 68.4%, 69.4%) were computed on the company's SEK 4,495m base, so the model multiplied a margin stated on one revenue basis by a revenue stated on another. SEK 5,737m divided by SEK 4,495m is 1.276; the model's FY2026 operating profit of SEK 4,581m is 27.6% above the second set's SEK 3,590m; EPS is 28% above (the extra 0.4 points is the model taxing the AT1 coupon that the cross-check treats as not deductible, which the second set already noted as immaterial). The phasing note ("the periods were moved by +15.9 points to match the year") is the same error seen from the quarters: the model's reported H1 2026 margins on its revenue basis are 54.4% (Q1) and 54.2% (Q2), so to reach 69.5% for the year it had to lift H2 to about 85%. Nothing in the second set's economic judgement is wrong; one input is stated on the wrong basis for this model.

The correction (written to inputs-changed.md). I restate the operating profit margin on the model's revenue basis so that the model's operating profit, pre-tax profit, net income and EPS equal the second set's: 54.5%, 53.5%, 53.5%, 53.6% and 54.4% for FY2026 to FY2030 (against the model's own FY2025 history of 53.7%), and 56.7%, 51.9%, 53.6%, 53.0%, 54.9% and 51.6% for the six periods of Part 3 (against 54.4% and 54.2% actual for Q1 and Q2 2026 on the same basis). Revenue growth is left as the second set set it (14.9%, 8.4%, 7.6%, 7.5%, 7.5%), because those rates are the Analyst's view of the company's operating income and because any alternative (a negative growth rate to force the model's revenue onto the company basis) would be nonsense. Three consequences for the next summary: the model's revenue line stays on the FactSet basis and must not be compared with consensus revenue (SEK 5,225m, 5,726m, 6,216m); the comparison with consensus should be read on net income and EPS only, as the summary already does, and will show our base 1.5%, 3% and 5% below consensus for FY2026 to FY2028; the model's "two-year-forward multiple today" will move from 14.2x to about 18x at SEK 380.2 (about 17x at SEK 360.1), in line with FactSet's 16.7x on consensus; and the return at the guideline fair multiple will fall sharply, which I treat at D-01 as a congruence problem in its own right. The summary says free cash flow is not shown for this company, so there is no second gap to explain.

The third set: assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

C527 questions
D-0158D-0260D-0347D-0444D-0570D-0660D-0748

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The seven answers describe a forecast that is easy to structure, easy to monitor and hard to pin down in any single year, and they agree on why: the difference between the next four years and the last four is explained by five drivers (the absence of a rate and activity tailwind, a 1.2 basis point a year yield drift, a Danish bill, a cost escalator stepping down, and a 2.5 to 3 point a year below-the-line drag), while the variance around the forecast is explained by two inputs the company does not control and discloses monthly (trading velocity and the policy rate through deposits). The earlier sets are congruent on all of this; the one judgement that remains open is how much of the Q3 2026 yield compression is structural, and it moves FY2027 EPS by about plus or minus 5% around the base of SEK 20.2, with the answer due on 22 January and 16 April 2027. The expected change rests on demonstrated strengths (asset gathering, retention, cost control, deposit pricing) and asks for one thing the company has not shown, earnings growth without a cycle; the precedents say it is achievable and the base's demands are lighter than Hargreaves Lansdown's or Avanza's own 2023 to 2025. The cone for FY2027 EPS is about SEK 16 to 23 with a probability-weighted centre about 5% below consensus and tails of roughly equal probability but unequal size, because costs are fixed and management does not cut; the share price is now about half sector-driven and half company-driven, inside a multiple band the sector has set at the bottom quarter of its range. Two corrections improve the picture against the earlier sets: the risk tax is not inside the four-year cone (threshold SEK 197bn against liabilities of SEK 114bn) and the deposit pass-through on a hike is likely lower than the base assumes (a small NII upside). Two matters must be put right before the memo uses the model: the operating margin input must be restated on the model's revenue basis (done in inputs-changed.md; EPS falls 22% to the second set's figures), and the model's return at a 27.70x guideline multiple must be replaced by the memo Analyst's own judgement of a fair multiple, for which the first set's 17x to 20x is the supported range. Net: the predictability of the financial forecasts over 18 months is medium: high on the cost line, the volume lines and the direction of earnings growth (low to high single digits in 2027), medium on the recurring revenue lines, low on the transactional lines and on any single quarter, with a downside lean into the next two prints and a neutral lean thereafter. For an investment case this is neither the simple, low-risk setup the dashboard's Case Riskiness B suggests nor a fragile one; it is a quality franchise in the weakest eighteen months of its five-year earnings path, with every variable that matters visible in advance.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets?

C58

Question asked How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

Judgement. The two earlier sets are highly congruent on structure and direction, and the structure they share is the right one: Avanza's operating profit over the next four years is the difference between a revenue growth rate set by savings-capital compounding less per-unit yield erosion plus or minus the activity cycle and the policy rate, and a cost growth rate set by management in advance (9%, then about 8% stepping to 5%, plus a Danish bill). Every answer in both sets either supports that frame or is a consequence of it. The congruence weakens in three places that the Consumer must hold explicitly: one unresolved pivot of judgement (how much of the Q3 2026 yield compression is structural), one mechanical incongruence between the second set and the Financial Analyst's model (the revenue-basis error above, now corrected), and one incongruence of valuation judgement inside Viewforth's own materials (a guideline fair multiple of 27.70x against the first set's fair range of 17x to 20x). The new evidence in the eight AlphaSense reports corrects two facts in the earlier sets, both in the case's favour, and contradicts the earlier sets on nothing of substance.

The spine the two sets share, and that every answer below rests on. (1) Volume lines (customers +7% to 8%, savings capital +10% to 12%, net inflows about 6% of opening capital, churn 0.7%) are the most predictable inputs in the model. (2) Monetisation per unit fell in Q3 2026 (brokerage yield 10.3 basis points, fund margin 22.2, currency margin 18, income per customer SEK 2,170) and the Q3 print was the first quarter in which capital growth (+21%) and income growth (+12%) visibly decoupled. (3) The cost line is the most reliable guided number in the sector (four years of guides met or beaten) and grows regardless of revenue. (4) Operating leverage therefore works against earnings in 2027 (costs +13% with Denmark against revenue +8%) and is neutral to 2029. (5) Below the operating line nothing surprises (tax 14.5%, AT1 coupon about SEK 70m, dilution 0.4% a year, payout 75%). (6) The policy rate has turned from a two-year headwind to a two-sided lever with positive earnings skew (SEK 420m per point down, SEK 1.1bn per point up before pass-through), and the base carries one hike on 4 November 2026. (7) Denmark is a cost line only to 2030 and an unpriced option. (8) The multiple (16.2x, 12th percentile of ten years) is near a fair trough while estimates sit near a twelve-month high and have not been cut.

Where the sets differ, and my adjudication.

  1. The revenue margin path. The first set carried 43 to 44 basis points for 2027 drifting 1 a year; the second set 41.8 drifting to 38.2 by 2030, on the argument that two-thirds of the Q3 compression is the new base. I side with the second set, for a reason neither set gave: the Q3 level of 41.3 basis points was reached with deposits at 8.9% of savings capital and a brokerage yield of 10.3, both near the low end of their ranges, so the cyclical part of the compression can revert (deposits rebuild in risk-off; the retail share of trading recovers when volatility returns), while the structural parts (index share of fund capital rising 5 to 6 points a year to 55.5%; the Private Banking and Pro share of trading trending up to 30%; the 12.5 basis point manual currency account spreading) cannot. Post-Q3 evidence supports the split: DNB Carnegie now carries a flat 10.6 basis point brokerage yield for 2027 and 2028 and says yields are "unlikely to return to historical peaks", and Nordnet's own brokerage yield fell to 13.1 from 13.9 basis points in the same quarter for the same mix reason, which says the affluent-mix drift is sector-wide and not an Avanza pricing failure. This is the pivot of the 18-month forecast: if the compression is mostly cyclical (management's "temporary market sentiment"; DNB Carnegie's 17% EPS upside if H1 2026 activity returns), FY2027 EPS is SEK 21.5 to 22.5; if it is mostly structural (Jefferies), SEK 19 to 19.5; the base of SEK 20.2 sits between, and the next two quarterly reports (22 January and 16 April 2027) decide it.
  2. The leverage ratio: binding (first set, C-19 and C-76) or loosening (second set, P-02). Both are right about different dates. The ratio binds the lending book now (the Q3 mortgage pause for the SEK 3m to 10m tier, two AT1 issues, no buyback) and loosens from mid-2027 if deposits stay at or below 9% of savings capital; Nordnet's ratio fell to 4.7% from 5.1% in a single quarter on deposit growth, which shows how quickly a risk-off quarter reverses the loosening. For the forecast the difference is immaterial (perhaps SEK 20m to 40m of 2027 lending NII); for the narrative it is the gate to a first buyback, which is a 2028 scenario, not a base-case line.
  3. The value of a 25 basis point hike. The first set gave SEK 150m to 250m of annual NII after pass-through; the second set used SEK 160m at a 60% to 70% pass-through on the interest-bearing 61% of deposits. The new deposit-history report (AlphaSense, own question 1) says Avanza's cumulative deposit beta reached only about 36% by Q3 2023 after a 400 basis point hiking cycle, Swedish bank betas about 50%, and that platforms will pass through "at least 30%" of a November hike. A 60% to 70% pass-through on the first 25 basis points is therefore conservative; the realistic value is SEK 180m to 210m, so the base's NII carries a small upside skew of SEK 30m to 50m a year (about 1% of pre-tax profit). I do not change an input for it; I record it as a skew.
  4. Danish phasing. The second set's SEK 130m, 150m, 175m and 170m for 2027 to 2030 against the first set's about 120m and 140m: I accept the second set; the remaining pre-launch budget (SEK 80m to 110m, 80% expensed) falls mostly in H1 2027 and the running and marketing costs start in H2 2027.
  5. Near-term EPS. The first set's "H2 2026 EPS of about SEK 9.9 looks SEK 0.4 to 0.6 too high" and the second set's Q4 EPS of about SEK 4.55 (H2 SEK 9.45) are the same statement; FY2026 base SEK 19.0 (first) and 19.1 (second) are the same number.

New facts in the AlphaSense reports that correct the earlier sets. (a) The risk tax on credit institutions (second set, P-08). The second set carried the threshold at "SEK 150bn when introduced in 2022, indexed since, perhaps SEK 170bn to 180bn by 2031", put Avanza Bank's crossing at 2029 to 2032, and sized the first year's cost at SEK 95m to 110m. The tax report (own question 5) gives the facts: the threshold is SEK 197bn for 2026 (SEB's and Swedbank's Q3 2026 reports), indexed annually by the price base amount plus two percentage points (DNB Carnegie, 7 October 2026), at a rate of 0.07%; Avanza Bank's consolidated liabilities are about SEK 114bn (deposits SEK 111.9bn), SEK 82bn below; and DNB Carnegie (1 September 2026) puts a 70% probability on the tax disappearing or being reduced by 2028, with the EU Advocate General having challenged it on state-aid grounds. At 7% to 8% a year liability growth against 4% to 5% a year threshold indexation, the gap closes by about 3 points of the threshold a year from 58% today, so the base case does not cross before the mid-2030s, and even a SEK 40bn dash to cash in a crash leaves the bank SEK 40bn below. I remove the risk tax from the four-year cone; what remains is a political tail (a redesigned bank tax that lowers the threshold or taxes profit), which the first set's C-04 and C-81 already carry. (b) Operating margin bases. Deutsche Bank's initiation (30 September 2026) quotes Avanza's FY2025 operating margin at 62% against Nordnet's 76%; the company's own figure is 68.5% and the first set's 67% to 72% by quarter is right. Deutsche Bank's figure is on its own basis (it appears to treat part of commission expense or AT1 cost differently). The Consumer should use the company's margin and should read any broker's margin comparison with Nordnet as an accounting-basis question first (Nordnet capitalises SEK 178m of personnel cost a year; Avanza expenses). (c) Deutsche Bank's 16% savings-capital compound rate and 14% EPS compound rate to 2028 (quoted as "broker consensus" in the D-02 report) are the top of the range, not the middle: the FactSet median EPS path is 11.4% a year from FY2025 to FY2028, DNB Carnegie's is about 8%, ours 9.4% to FY2028 (8.8% to FY2030). The Consumer should not read the AlphaSense reports' "consensus" wording as the FactSet median. (d) The D-02 report's headline conclusion that the expected transition "depends on capabilities the company has yet to demonstrate" contradicts the first set's competitive-advantage judgement only if the base case needs Danish revenue, Sigma economics or pension acceleration; it needs none of them (D-02 below).

The incongruence inside Viewforth's own materials: the fair multiple. The Financial Analyst's model computes the return at a guideline fair multiple of 27.70x, which is the dashboard's ten-year 90th percentile ("mania") band, and reports a total shareholder return of +125% to December 2027 and +157% to June 2029. The first set's judgement (C-50, C-83, closing note) is that the fair range is 17x to 20x, the trough 15x to 16x, and the stock is at the 12th percentile of its decade because the market pays this sector for growth, which Avanza's narrative lacks until Denmark. The model's return is overstated twice: by the 28% EPS basis error above and by a multiple drawn from the 2020 to 2021 boom (the five-year peak was 37x) that no answer in either set supports for a company whose EPS growth is decelerating from 15% to 6% with estimates at a peak. On the corrected EPS and the first set's range, the value per share at end-2027 is about SEK 395 to 465 before dividends (17x to 20x on FY2029 EPS of SEK 23.3, the two-year-forward earnings at that date), 10% to 30% above SEK 360 plus a dividend yield of about 4%; at the dashboard's "conservative" band of 19.3x it is about SEK 450 (+25%, plus dividends). That is a defensible mid-teens annualised return, not a mania return. The guideline multiple is set by Viewforth's rule from the stock's phase and its range, and the memo Analyst's brief gives them the task of judging it (topics #11 and #12); I flag it here because it is the largest incongruence in the files, and because a reader of the one-page summary alone would be misled on both EPS and the multiple. My own view of the phase, for that judgement: stalling and turning (growth decelerating, estimates at a twelve-month high, technical momentum D, a discount to the one peer the market pays for growth), which Viewforth's rule maps to the lower half of the range, not the top decile.

Smaller incongruences, and why they are not a problem. The dashboard's sell-side earnings momentum rating of A against both sets' judgement that estimates are at a peak and will be cut (first set C-74 scored 35; second set put a 60% probability on a 3% cut to FY2027 before the Q4 print): the A is a twelve-month lookback (FY2027 EPS +13% over twelve months, +1% over one), it lags, and the valuation A (12th percentile) and technical D are exactly what a stock looks like when the price has absorbed the bear case and the estimates still carry the bull case. Inside the first set, pricing power scored 28 to 30 against competitive advantage 70 to 78: congruent by design, since the moat is built on sharing scale with customers (C-46, scored 85), which is also why the yield drift is structural. Management scored 63 while the cost line is given "very high weight" in the second set: congruent, because the weight is on what management controls and the discount is for what it does not (yield management, capital allocation). C-19 and C-64 (no buyback) against P-02's buyback option: the option is dated 2028 and probability-weighted, not a base line.

What the Consumer should carry from the congruence test. The structure is agreed and right; the model needs the basis correction and then reproduces the second set; the one pivot that moves the 18-month EPS by about plus or minus 5% is the cyclical-structural split of the Q3 compression, and it is decidable from disclosed data on two dates; the risk-tax scenario is removed; the fair-multiple incongruence is a Viewforth-internal matter the memo must settle explicitly rather than inherit from the model.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and how far does the thesis rely on demonstrated rather than acquired strengths?

C60

Question asked To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

Judgement. The expected change is a deceleration, not a transformation, and in its base-case form it rests almost entirely on strengths Avanza has demonstrated for years: gathering assets faster than the market, keeping customers, holding costs to a published ceiling, and pricing deposits. The one thing the base case asks for that the company has never shown is the ability to grow earnings at 6% to 9% a year with no help from a rate cycle or an activity cycle: every period of EPS growth in Avanza's history has had one or the other behind it, and the only period without either (2016 to 2019, zero rates and low activity) was a period of flat to falling earnings. The precedents say that asset compounding can carry revenue through yield erosion (Hargreaves Lansdown 2012 to 2017; Avanza itself 2023 to 2025), so the base case is plausible on demonstrated strengths; the upside case (Sigma at scale, Denmark re-rating the stock, yield recovering to 11 basis points) relies on strengths that would have to be acquired, and should be carried as option value, exactly as the second set carries it.

The change to test, restated as the requirement it places on the business. From FY2025 to FY2030 the base case needs: savings capital +10% to 11% a year (SEK 1,079bn to 1,910bn) from 5% market return and 6% net inflow; a revenue margin falling about 1.2 basis points a year (44.2 to 38.2); revenue therefore +7.5% to 8.5% a year; Swedish cost growth stepping from 9% to about 5% with a Danish line of SEK 130m to 175m a year on top; an operating margin held at 68% to 69.5%; EPS compounding at 8.8% a year. Against the last four years (revenue +15% a year, EPS +15.7%, margin up 12 points) it is a lower bar on every growth line and a higher bar on one thing: doing it without a tailwind.

What the record demonstrates, line by line.

  • Asset gathering (needed: 6% net inflow a year; demonstrated: yes, with a cyclical range). Net inflows SEK 90bn (2021), 24bn (2022), 72bn (2023), 86bn (2024), 54bn (2025), 58.5bn in nine months of 2026; as a share of opening capital that is roughly 4% to 14%, with 2022 the floor; share of Swedish savings capital 7.8% to 8.7% in 21 months; 11.9% of market inflows against 8.7% of stock; 32% of Swedish fund flows in Q3 2026; two-thirds of inflows from existing customers who still keep two-thirds of their wealth elsewhere. The 6% assumption is below the eight-year average and above the 2022 trough. Demonstrated.
  • Retention (needed: churn under 1%; demonstrated: yes). 0.7% rolling, under 2% through every cycle since 2019; 16 consecutive Swedish Quality Index wins; NPS 41 against an industry 3. Demonstrated, and the strongest line in the case.
  • Cost control (needed: 9% then about 7.5%, 6.7%, 6.3%, 4.8% in Sweden; demonstrated: yes, with one exception). Guides met or beaten in 2022 (minus 2.7% against the floor), 2023 (minus 0.7%), 2024 (9.2% against 9.5%), 2025 (10.4% against 11%); 2026 running at 6.6% in H1 and 3% below consensus in Q3. The exception is 2021 to 2022, when the long-term framework was withdrawn and costs rose about 20% because a volume surge forced support hiring; the lesson is that the ceiling holds in normal and weak years and can break in a boom, which is the reverse of the risk the Consumer usually worries about.
  • Deposit pricing (needed: 60% to 70% pass-through on a hike; demonstrated: better than needed). Zero pass-through through 2022; a cumulative beta of about 36% by Q3 2023 at a 4% policy rate; 75% to 80% pass-through of cuts in 2024 and 2025. The company has shown it can hold deposit cost down on the way up and push it down on the way down. Demonstrated; the base is conservative here.
  • Capital management (needed: a leverage ratio above 3.5% with a 75% payout; demonstrated: yes). Two AT1 issues at STIBOR plus 3.25% and plus 2.85% (Nordnet pays plus 5.0%); the Sparkonto+ wind-down with 60% of deposits retained against a 55% target; ratio back to 4.8% from 4.2% in six months. Demonstrated.
  • Product cadence and scale (needed: hold the home franchise against Nordnet, SAVR and Montrose; demonstrated: yes on customers, not yet on yield). 20% to 21% of Nasdaq Stockholm transactions; 52,800 net new customers in Q3; Sigma, extended US hours, the Pension Portal, the analysis hub all shipped in 2026. Demonstrated on volume.

What the base case needs that is not demonstrated. (1) Holding the yield drift to about 1.2 basis points a year. The record is a drift of about 2 basis points a year from 2023 (48) to 2025 (44.2), and about 3 annualised from FY2025 to Q3 2026 (41.3) in a quarter of adverse mix; the base therefore assumes a slower drift than the recent record, which is plausible only because part of the recent fall is cyclical (deposits and the retail share of trading both near lows). This is the one assumption in the base that leans on judgement rather than record, and it is why I kept the second set's two-thirds structural split rather than something kinder. (2) Earnings growth without a cycle. NII went from SEK 321m (2021) to 1.6bn (2023) on the rate cycle; the 2025 to 2026 step came from internalising SEK 25bn of Sparkonto+ deposits at ten times the spread; H1 2026 was an activity peak. The base's 6% to 9% EPS growth from 2027 is the asset engine alone (capital +10% to 11%, revenue +8%, costs +7% to 13%, below-the-line drag 2.5% to 3%). Avanza has not produced a year like that; the sector has (AJ Bell: double-digit customer and asset growth through fee cuts at a 41% pre-tax margin; Hargreaves Lansdown: revenue margin 75 to 47 basis points in five years while assets grew 23% a year and revenue 16%). The ratio the base asks of Avanza (margin minus 2.7% a year against capital plus 10% to 11%) is far less demanding than Hargreaves Lansdown's (margin minus 7.5% a year against assets plus 23%), and Avanza's 2023 to 2025 (margin minus 4% a year against capital plus 18%) was harder than what is now asked. Plausible on precedent; unproven in Avanza's own steady-state history.

What the upside case needs that would have to be acquired, and why it is not in the base. (1) Yield management across fee classes and currency accounts: management has said its brokerage pricing may not align with its simplicity goals and has not explained the Q1 2026 compression; there is no evidence of active yield management, and the moat's engine (sharing scale) argues against it ever appearing. (2) Building and running a second platform in a second country: unprecedented at Avanza; Nordnet's Denmark took five years to SEK 82m of revenue and needed the Stocknet acquisition; Nordnet exited Germany in 2010; Fineco exited the UK in 2023 after 23,000 clients in six years; Avanza's own method (AI-first, cloud-native, separate stack) is untested at scale and is dual-stack for ever. The base carries Denmark as cost only, so the thesis does not depend on this strength; the downside is bounded at 2% to 3% of EPS a year plus an overrun (Jefferies' SEK 340m to 380m over three years against management's implied SEK 300m) worth another 1% to 2%. (3) Monetising discretionary management (Sigma): SEK 710m in a month at 0.55% promotional pricing stepping to 0.85% in 2027; retention through the step is unproven; DNB Carnegie expects gradual adoption because affluent clients prefer an adviser; the base carries SEK 20m to 35m of revenue by 2028. (4) Converting pension and Private Banking wallet share fast enough to lift income per customer: occupational pension capital +27% year on year, premium share 10.7% (down from 11.0%); the transfer-signature petition is unresolved; Private Banking capital +7% in the quarter; income per customer fell 6%. Slow, recurring, and in the base only through the 6% inflow assumption.

Peer precedents, used as a check on the margin and the expansion year. FinecoBank held a 27% cost-to-income ratio (73% operating margin) while adding a record 193,804 customers in 2025; Swissquote delivered a 64% pre-tax margin in H2 2025 while stepping up technology investment and consolidating international operations; both run one platform. Nordnet's 71% to 76% margin (on its basis) through a German pre-launch year with SEK 22m a quarter of launch cost shows an expansion year inside a 70%-plus margin is ordinary for this model. flatexDEGIRO shows the other side: a transaction-geared platform whose profit growth is slowing to 9% after 40% and which Deutsche Bank downgraded in October 2026 for slowing growth. Avanza is between the two groups: 44% transactional revenue (flatexDEGIRO-like exposure) on an asset base and cost ratio (Fineco-like) that the precedents say can hold a 68% margin through 2027 if revenue grows at all.

Net. The base case is plausible on demonstrated strengths with medium-high confidence; its weakest link is the assumed slowing of yield erosion, which is a judgement, and the absence of any Avanza precedent for cycle-free earnings growth, which is a gap in the record rather than evidence against. The thesis as the second set built it does not rely on acquired strengths; the sell-side's bull cases (Deutsche Bank's 14% EPS compound rate; DNB Carnegie's 17% activity recovery) do.

D-03

How diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences between the forecasts and the last four to six years' financials?

❌ D47

Question asked In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

Judgement. Concentrated, in two senses that pull in opposite directions for predictability. The difference between the next four years and the last four is explained by a handful of drivers, and the variance around the forecast is explained by two inputs that the company does not control and discloses monthly. Concentration of the first kind makes the forecast easy to structure and to monitor; concentration of the second kind, in two exogenous and volatile variables, makes any single year hard to pin down. The net for the Consumer is a case whose drivers are few, external, visible and fast-moving, which is the profile of a trackable but not precisely forecastable business.

The difference to be explained. Last four years (FY2022 to FY2025): revenue +15% a year, costs +11% a year, operating margin 56% to 68.5%, EPS +15.7% a year (SEK 10.69 to 16.57); over six years (FY2019 to FY2025) revenue +25% a year, EPS +33% a year from a depressed 2019 base. Next four to five years (FY2025 to FY2030, base): revenue +8.8% a year including the FY2026 catch-up (then +7.5% to 8.5%), costs +8.6% a year (13% in 2027, 4% in 2030), margin flat at 68% to 69.5%, EPS +8.8% a year. The difference is about 7 points of annual revenue growth and 7 points of annual EPS growth, with the margin's 12-point climb replaced by a plateau.

The drivers of the difference, in order of size (my decomposition of the second set's P-09).

  1. The removal of three non-repeating revenue tailwinds: the 2022 to 2023 rate cycle (NII SEK 321m in 2021 to SEK 1.6bn in 2023; worth roughly 10 points of revenue growth a year for two years); the 2025 to 2026 deposit internalisation (SEK 25bn moved from a 5 basis point distribution fee to a roughly 50 basis point spread, about SEK 110m of annual NII, 2.5 points of revenue growth once); the H1 2026 activity peak (brokerage plus currency SEK 2,140m in 2026 against 1,978m in 2025 and a 2027 base of 2,160m). Together about half of the 7-point deceleration. These are not forecast assumptions about the company; they are the statement that the base carries a normal rate and a normal activity year.
  2. The revenue-margin drift of about 1.2 basis points a year (0.8 price and mix, 0.4 denominator inflation from market appreciation): about 3 points of revenue growth a year. This is the one structural company-level assumption, and the one the pivot at D-01 turns on.
  3. The cost escalator and the Danish bill: Swedish costs step down from 9% to 5%, which would add margin on its own, but Denmark adds SEK 130m to 175m a year from 2027, so the two together hold the margin flat rather than lifting it; Denmark alone takes about 1 point of margin through 2029 and makes 2027 the squeeze year (costs +13%).
  4. Below the line: the AT1 coupon fixed in kronor while profit grows, 0.4% a year of dilution and no buyback together subtract about 2.5 to 3 points a year from EPS growth relative to operating profit growth; in the last four years this layer was roughly neutral (tax fell from 16% to 14.5%, no AT1 until 2025).
  5. Offsets: one hike (about SEK 160m to 200m of NII, 4% to 5% of 2027 pre-tax profit), lending growth of 12% to 15% a year (about 2 points of NII growth), and the scale loop on the cost ratio (13.9 to 11.7 basis points of capital). Five drivers explain the whole difference; two of them (the tailwinds' absence and the yield drift) explain about 80% of it.

The drivers of the variance around the forecast. The AlphaSense D-03/D-04 report's own calculation is that the policy rate and turnover per customer explain about 75% of the variance in quarterly operating profit since 2019 (medium confidence; the report's regression, not the company's). The second set put it at "more than 80%" for three inputs (turnover per customer and its foreign share; the deposit base's response to risk appetite and rates; the fund and brokerage yields). I accept both: in a quarter, two external variables (realised volatility, which sets velocity, and the Riksbank path, which sets NII) move operating profit by plus or minus 10% to 15%; over a year the third (yield mix) adds or removes another 3% to 5%. The company's own sensitivities compose the same picture: 2 points of trading volume about SEK 0.25 of EPS; 5 points of cross-border mix about SEK 0.35; a point of policy rate SEK 2.3 down or up to SEK 5.9 before pass-through. Nothing else in the model (customers, inflows, costs, tax, share count) moves a year's EPS by more than 1% to 2% against the base.

Why this concentration matters, and how it compares. A business whose forecast error sits in two disclosed, exogenous variables is at once the easiest in the sector to monitor (monthly statistics in the first week of each month; a Riksbank calendar) and among the harder to forecast a year ahead, because neither variable is forecastable with confidence: the Riksbank's November decision is near-consensus (hike to 2.0%) but the 2027 path is contested (Nordea a second hike in February; Morgan Stanley a hold through 2027; Deutsche Bank 2.50% by mid-2027), and trading velocity has fallen 10% year on year in September with no base rate for how long a post-peak lull lasts (the 2022 to 2023 precedent was two years; August 2024 and April 2025 were weeks). Against peers: Integrafin (fee-only, no cash margin, costs capped at 3%) is the low-dispersion end; flatexDEGIRO and Swissquote the high end; Avanza sits in the middle because 56% of its revenue is asset-scaled and compounding, and because at a 2% policy rate the deposit loop is a genuine stabiliser (a dash to cash adds NII while brokerage falls), which it was not at 0% in 2022.

What the Consumer should carry. Model the difference between past and future on five lines and the variance on two; expect the two to move the quarter and the five to move the trend; and treat any broker's forecast that leans on one of the two variance drivers (a rate path or an activity recovery) as a scenario, not a base. Concentration is favourable for monitoring and for the simplicity of the case (memo topic #9) and unfavourable for the precision of any single year's number; on balance slightly below neutral for predictability over 18 months.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

❌ D44

Judgement. The cone is moderately wide for a company of this quality and skewed to the downside in probability-weighted terms, though not in the magnitude of its tails. For FY2027 EPS the plausible range is about SEK 16 to 23 against a base of SEK 20.2 and a consensus of SEK 20.87: a one-standard-deviation band of roughly plus or minus 10%, a bull tail of plus 11% and a bear tail of minus 20%, with roughly equal probability (about 25% each) on the two tails, so the probability-weighted expectation (about SEK 19.9) sits below the mode and about 5% below consensus. The skew comes from one asymmetry that both earlier sets identified and that the new evidence confirms: the cost line does not respond to revenue within a year and management does not cut, so a revenue shortfall drops through at 90% while a revenue beat does the same, but the revenue line itself is more likely to disappoint than to surprise over the next two quarters because the activity cycle turned down in Q3, the regime is risk-off, and consensus has not yet been cut. Predictability over 18 months is therefore medium-low on the number and medium-high on the direction: the Consumer can be confident that FY2027 EPS growth will be low single digits to high single digits and that the first two prints will test the downside, and cannot be confident within plus or minus SEK 2 where the year lands.

The cone, built from the drivers. Around the 2027 base (revenue SEK 5,600m; costs SEK 1,780m; operating profit SEK 3,820m):

  • Velocity. The base has turnover per customer at about 90% of the 2026 average (which included the H1 peak). A return to H1 2026 velocity adds about SEK 290m to brokerage plus currency (plus 7.5% to EPS); a 2022-type year (brokerage plus currency down 25% to about SEK 1,600m) removes about SEK 560m (minus 15%). The September data (trades minus 10% year on year; daily trades per customer minus 18%) and DNB Carnegie's expectation of lower velocity after October's turbulence put the near-term weight on the lower side; DNB Carnegie's own 2027 brokerage (SEK 1,256m) and currency (SEK 733m) sit SEK 170m below our base, consensus (SEK 1,394m and 839m) SEK 70m above.
  • The policy rate and deposits. One hike is in the base. A second to 2.25% by spring 2027 (Nordea's call) adds about SEK 100m of 2027 NII (plus 2.5% to EPS); no hike in November (a minority view; the forward curve, Barclays, Deutsche Bank, Nordea and Scotiabank all expect one) removes about SEK 160m to 200m (minus 4% to 5%). Deposits rebuilding to 9.5% to 10% of capital in a risk-off year add SEK 50m to 100m; falling to 8% removes about the same. The deposit-history report's lower historical betas (36% to 50% against the base's 60% to 70%) add a small positive skew of SEK 30m to 50m.
  • Yields. The base has brokerage yield 10.5, fund margin 21.5, currency margin 17.5 basis points. Reversion toward 11.0, 22.5 and 18.5 (the cyclical case) adds SEK 100m to 150m; a further step (a 25 basis point standard currency fee cut in response to SAVR, Montrose or Nordnet's affluent venture; RIS-driven rebate compression) removes SEK 100m to 150m. Symmetric in size; the step down has a trigger (a competitor's move) and the step up needs a regime change (volatility returning), so slightly negative in probability.
  • Costs. The habitual 1% beat adds SEK 15m to 20m; a Danish overrun toward Jefferies' three-year total removes SEK 30m to 50m a year; a Q4 2026 cost spike of the kind DNB Carnegie flagged is a timing effect. Narrow and well understood.
  • Market level. Savings capital's 5% market return assumption: a 15% market fall removes about SEK 150m of fund and NII revenue through lower average capital and deposits rising (partly offset), and raises the leverage-ratio question; a 15% rise adds about the same and loosens the ratio. Combining: bull case (velocity back, second hike, deposits held) operating profit about SEK 4,250m, EPS about SEK 22.5 (plus 11% on base, plus 8% on consensus), probability about 25%; bear case (a 2022-type activity year, markets down 15%, costs still rising 13%) operating profit about SEK 3,050m, EPS about SEK 16.2 (minus 20% on base, minus 22% on consensus), probability about 25%; base about 50%. I accept the second set's probabilities and add that the bear case does not need a recession: it needs eighteen months of low volatility after a peak, which is what 2022 to 2023 delivered without a Swedish recession.

The historical width, as a check. Realised annual EPS against the consensus of twelve months earlier: 2022 a miss of roughly 20% to 25% (consensus had extrapolated 2021 activity; EPS fell 19%); 2023 a beat (the rate cycle; NII SEK 1.6bn against earlier expectations well below); 2024 and 2025 modest beats of a few percent; Q1 2023 a 4% quarterly miss when broad deposit remuneration cut the realised rate sensitivity to a fraction of the published one (a 75 basis point hike worth SEK 50m instead of SEK 400m, per DNB Carnegie at the time). Within the last ten quarters: beats of 4% to 5% in rising-volatility quarters, misses of 2% to 5% in quiet ones. The record says a plus or minus 10% error on a year is ordinary and a 20% error happens when a boom ends; the current setting (a quarter after a peak, consensus unrevised, a rate turn due) is the setting in which the record's largest errors occurred, and they were misses. Peers: Integrafin's realised EPS tracks consensus closely (no rate or trading gearing); flatexDEGIRO's 2022 to 2023 normalisation and its October 2026 downgrade show the transaction-geared end; Nordnet (NII 42% of income) has a wider rate-driven dispersion than Avanza (33% to 35%). Avanza sits in the middle of the cohort.

The 18-month cone on the forward year. By the Q1 2028 report the market will price FY2028: base SEK 21.7, plausible range about SEK 17 to 25. The cone widens from plus or minus 10% to plus or minus 15% to 20% because the Danish running cost (SEK 150m in the base; SEK 180m to 220m if Jefferies is right), the 2028 policy rate and the second year of yield drift all enter.

Why the skew is down in probability but not in the franchise. Three asymmetries: (1) consensus starts above the base (SEK 20.87 against 20.2) and above DNB Carnegie's post-print SEK 19.29, so "in line with our base" is already a cut; (2) the operating leverage multiplier is about 1.45x in both directions, but management's demonstrated response to a weak year is to hold cost growth, not reverse it (2022 costs +19%), so the downside realises fully and the upside does too; (3) the activity cycle has just turned and post-peak lulls have lasted longer than recoveries. Against them, two positive asymmetries: the rate sensitivity is SEK 1.1bn up against SEK 420m down per point (a zero floor on transactional deposits; pass-through lags on the way up), and the deposit loop at a 2% policy rate cushions a risk-off quarter in a way it could not at 0%. None of the asymmetries touches the franchise: the bear case leaves a 63% margin, a 36% to 38% return on equity and a 70% payout.

The share price cone, for the memo's risk section. On a 15x to 20x multiple applied to the FY2027 EPS range, the twelve-month price cone is roughly SEK 240 (15x on 16.2, the bear case with a de-rating to the five-year low band) to SEK 450 (20x on 22.5); the base at 17x to 19x on SEK 20.2 is SEK 345 to 385, around the current SEK 360. The first set's trough analysis (SEK 310 to 345 on a fair trough multiple) and its exit trigger (a sequential margin below 50%; Q3 was 72%) stand.

Net for predictability. Width moderate (plus or minus 10% on EPS at one standard deviation, 35 points between the tails), skew modestly negative in probability-weighted terms for the next two prints and roughly neutral beyond April 2027, and both tails fully disclosed in advance by monthly data: a case that is more predictable in direction than in level, and less predictable than its cost line and volume lines suggest because 44% of revenue and 90% of the cost base do not move together.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and the key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C70

Question asked How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

Judgement. Among the most helpful in European financials for the volume drivers and the cost line, adequate for the recurring revenue lines, and inadequate on exactly the two things the 18-month forecast turns on: the mix within trading (who is trading, and in which fee class and currency account) and the realised deposit pass-through. The Consumer can track whether the volume engine is intact every month and whether the yield compression is cyclical or structural only every quarter, with a lag, and only by inference from three ratios the company publishes without comment. The practical consequence is that the thesis is monitorable to a standard few companies allow, but the specific question the market will ask over the next six months (is income per customer stabilising?) will be answered on two dates, 22 January and 16 April 2027, and not in between.

What the company discloses, and what each item tells the Consumer.

  • Monthly statistics (first week of each month: 4 to 6 November, December, January, February, March, April). Net inflow, customers, savings capital, brokerage-generating notes and turnover, lending, deposits. These resolve the volume lines (customers +7% to 8%, inflows about SEK 6bn to 7bn a month, savings capital against the SEK 1,270bn year-end base) and give a direct read on the two variance drivers in volume terms (notes and turnover per customer against the Q3 run rate of about 14% below Q2; deposits against 8.9% of capital). DNB Carnegie's web-traffic tracker correlates 70% to 80% with trades and its regression explains about half of monthly changes, so the monthly data are also anticipated by third-party trackers. What they do not show: the Private Banking and Pro share of turnover, the foreign share, average trade size, or the fee-class mix, which is why Q3 brokerage missed consensus by 5% on volumes that the monthly data had shown; nor the intra-month treasury volumes that made Q2 NII beat by 7% to 9%.
  • Quarterly report (22 January 2027 for Q4 and the full year; 16 April for Q1). Brokerage yield (10.3 basis points), fund margin (22.2), currency margin (18), income per customer (SEK 2,170), deposit rate (0.81%) and interest-bearing share (61%), the Private Banking and Pro share of trading (30%), index share of fund capital (55.5%), own-fund share (41% to 42%), lending yield (2.71%), leverage ratio (4.8%) and deposit headroom (SEK 46bn), Danish spend to date (SEK 11m expensed, 2m capitalised in Q3), Sigma assets (SEK 710m). These are the thesis's monitoring variables, and the thresholds the second set set are usable as written: income per customer below SEK 2,050 says the balancing loops have won, above SEK 2,300 says the activity cycle is back; brokerage yield below 10 basis points in Q1 2027 is a structural signal; deposits below 8.5% of capital with no hike is the NII warning.
  • The company-compiled consensus (about a week before each report; 18 contributors for Q3). It fixes the bar without ambiguity and shows whether the lagging brokers have moved; the January compilation is the first full view of post-Q3 revisions.
  • The cost guide and the Danish budget (22 January). The 2027 Swedish growth figure and any change to the SEK 120m to 150m pre-launch total are the two numbers the sector digest names as the re-rating test for the whole cohort.
  • The rate sensitivity (each quarter; SEK 420m down, SEK 1.1bn up per point). Useful for direction; the 2023 experience (realised NII sensitivity a fraction of the published figure once deposits were remunerated) says the Consumer should model pass-through separately and treat the published figure as gross.
  • The AT1 prospectus (April 2026) for deposit tiering; the annual report (March 2027) for the risk-tax statement, liabilities and the pension share.

What is missing, in order of how much it matters over six months. (1) No monthly or quarterly disclosure of the realised deposit pass-through after a hike, so the November hike's value (SEK 160m to 210m a year) will be inferred from the Q4 deposit rate and NII, with two months of effect. (2) No intra-quarter segment or fee-class mix, so brokerage and currency income remain the lines where monthly volume data mislead (Q3's 5% and 8% misses). (3) No Sigma customer count, retention through the 2027 price step from 0.55% to 0.85%, or acquisition cost. (4) No Danish product, pricing or wrapper disclosure before H1 2027; management has been explicitly reluctant for competitive reasons; brokers carry zero Danish revenue. (5) No uptime or incident statistics, despite outages under load being the expert-flagged operational risk and the Google Cloud migration (SEK 300m to 2030) being in progress. (6) No revenue, NII or EPS guidance of any kind, which is the company's long-standing policy for a market-driven business and is why forecast dispersion (FY2027 EPS SEK 19.3 to 22.9) is as wide as it is.

External monitoring that fills part of the gap. The Riksbank's 4 November and February decisions and the STIBOR fixings (the treasury portfolio has under three months' duration, so NII follows STIBOR with one quarter's lag); Nordnet's monthly statistics and its Q4 report (late January) for the relative-growth and German read-across; the Deutsche Bank and DNB Carnegie activity trackers (trades, web traffic) published monthly; the Swedish fund association's monthly flows (Avanza's share, 32% in Q3); SBAB's and the challenger banks' savings rates for the deposit-competition read; and the Ministry of Finance for the pension-transfer petition and the bank-tax debate.

Net. For the six months to April 2027 the Consumer will see the volume engine monthly and the monetisation question twice. That is enough to know by 22 January whether Q4 brokerage held near SEK 310m and NII reached about SEK 480m, and by 16 April whether Q1 2027 is the beat the second set's quarterly shape expects (a full hike quarter against an easy cost comparison) or the first evidence that the drift is faster than 1.2 basis points a year. Disclosure is a strong positive for the predictability of the case; its gaps sit on the lines where the forecast error concentrates, which caps the score.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace to operational drivers, revenue, cost and margin lines, and through to EPS; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to EPS?

C60

Question asked How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

Judgement. The internal transmission is fully specified on the cost and capital side and absent on the revenue side, by the company's design; the external transmission is clear in direction on every link and unreliable in magnitude on the two links that matter most (the rate pass-through and the trading mix). The result is a model in which the Consumer can trace every stated commitment to a line and every line to EPS, but in which the company's commitments cover about 30% of the income statement (costs) and none of the 70% that moves.

What the company says it will achieve, traced to the lines.

  1. Swedish cost growth of 9% in 2026, 8% average 2025 to 2030, about 5% in 2030. Traces directly: SEK 1,413m to about SEK 1,540m (2026) plus Denmark SEK 40m; then about SEK 1,650m, 1,760m, 1,870m, 1,960m. This is the only line the company guides and the one with the cleanest record; at 90% fixed, each 1 point of cost growth is about SEK 16m, 0.4% of operating profit. To EPS: one for one after 14.5% tax.
  2. Denmark: SEK 120m to 150m pre-launch (20% capitalised), SEK 80m running "and rising", up to SEK 60m marketing for three years, break-even about five years after an H2 2027 launch. Traces to the cost line (SEK 40m, about 130m, 150m, 175m, 170m) and to "other income" for the first small revenues (perhaps SEK 10m in 2028). To EPS: 2% to 3% a year of drag.
  3. SEK 2,000bn of savings capital by 2030 (15% a year). Traces to revenue only through a revenue margin the company does not guide: at 38 to 44 basis points, SEK 2,000bn is SEK 7.6bn to 8.8bn of operating income, so the target is worth SEK 0.6bn to 0.9bn more revenue in 2030 than the base's SEK 1,910bn implies at 38 basis points; the required growth from here is about 12% a year against the base's 10% to 11%, and it was missed in 2025 (13%) and is being met in 2026 on market return (21%). To EPS: each SEK 100bn of capital at the 2030 margin is about SEK 380m of revenue and SEK 2 of EPS, so the target gap is worth about SEK 2 of 2030 EPS. The weakest link in the internal chain is here: the company commits to a volume and not to a price, and the price is where the forecast error sits.
  4. Return on equity of at least 35%; payout at least 70%. Trace to net income over equity (base 38% to 42%) and to DPS (base 75%: SEK 14.3, 15.2, 16.3); both are met with room and tell the Consumer nothing new.
  5. The published rate sensitivity (SEK 420m down, SEK 1.1bn up per point). Traces to NII, but as a gross figure before pass-through; the 2023 record (a 75 basis point hike worth SEK 50m of NII once retail deposits were remunerated, against SEK 400m on the then-published guideline) says the realised figure can be a fraction of the published one on the way up, and the 2024 to 2025 record (75% to 80% pass-through of cuts) says it is close to the published one on the way down. The Consumer should model NII as deposits times a spread with an explicit beta, not from the sensitivity.
  6. Market leadership in Private Banking and in non-collectively-agreed occupational pensions by 2030; a leading European platform. No quantified line; these are the strategy's inputs and are visible only through the mix statistics (Private Banking and Pro share of trading; pension capital +27%; premium share 10.7%).

The external transmission, link by link.

  • Policy rate to NII. Direction certain; magnitude depends on the deposit beta (36% cumulative in 2023; 75% to 80% on cuts), the interest-bearing share (61%), the treasury portfolio's three-month repricing lag and the lending book's repricing (mortgages immediate; margin lending competitive). Clear to within about SEK 50m a year per 25 basis points.
  • Equity markets to savings capital to fund commissions and NII. Clear and nearly mechanical: fund capital is about 38% of savings capital, fund commissions are fund capital times a margin that drifts 0.6 to 0.8 basis points a year; deposits as a share of capital fall in rising markets (8.9% now from 9.6%) and rise in falling ones. The only ambiguity is the sign of the deposit effect, which cuts NII in risk-on and adds it in risk-off.
  • Volatility to trading velocity to brokerage and currency income. Clear in volume (sell-offs raise velocity for weeks; long rallies after a peak lower it for quarters; the five episodes in the velocity report all fit), unclear in price: the same number of trades yields less when the affluent share rises (Q3) and more when retail returns, and the monthly data do not show which is happening. This is the link where consensus was wrong in Q3 and will be tested again in Q4.
  • Risk appetite to deposits to the leverage ratio to lending and payout. Clear and two-way: every SEK 8bn to 10bn of customer buying frees 30 to 50 basis points of ratio; a dash to cash consumes it; the mortgage pause and the AT1 issues are the visible outputs.
  • Krona to foreign trading. A stronger krona lowers the SEK value of US holdings and turnover (foreign share 31%, 70% US); Morgan Stanley's EUR/SEK 10.40 by late 2026 is a mild headwind; the risks report's 30% appreciation shock cost SEK 179m. Direction clear, magnitude small in the base.
  • Competition to yield. Nordnet's commission-free weeks, SAVR's and Montrose's affluent pricing, Interactive Brokers' Swedish ISK and Nordnet's 2027 private-banking venture with ABG Sundal Collier act on the brokerage and currency margins by a few tenths of a basis point a year; a list-price response (the 25 basis point standard currency fee) would be a step of SEK 100m to 150m. Direction clear; timing unknowable.
  • Regulation to the fee pool. The RIS (final text January 2027, no inducement ban, value-for-money tests to 2029) and the Swedish FSA's declared intention to study national rules act on the 17% of income from fund rebates slowly; the risk tax is now a non-issue inside the horizon (D-01); a bank tax on profit is a political tail with a 2028-plus timetable (DNB Carnegie: no new tax before 2028 given the inquiry process).

Net. Everything the company commits to can be traced to a line and to EPS, and the company commits only to lines it controls; the lines it does not control transmit clearly in direction and are quantified by the company only gross. For the Consumer this means a model whose cost side can be carried with high confidence and whose revenue side must be built from external variables with explicit betas, which is what the second set did. Clarity is good; completeness is partial; the market's forecast dispersion is the honest measure of what the company leaves to the analyst.

D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (rolling six months), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

Judgement. The level of Avanza's multiple is industry-driven and its position inside the industry is company-driven; the share price has become markedly more idiosyncratic since mid-2026, and the stock has stopped amplifying the sector. Over the latest six-month window the dashboard's nine-stock platform basket explains 31% of Avanza's daily variance (correlation 0.56, beta 0.66), down from 56% (beta 1.00 to 1.06) in the windows ending April to June 2026 and from a 24-month average of about 40%; Nordnet alone explains 55% (correlation 0.74, beta 0.73), Fineco 27%, flatexDEGIRO 25%, and the CFD brokers (IG, Plus500, XTB) 1% to 4%. Avanza returned +4.7% over the window against the basket's minus 5.4%, while over twelve months it is flat (minus 0.2%) against Nordnet's +22%. For the Consumer: the sector sets the band the multiple trades in (asset-gathering platforms at the 12th to 24th percentile of their ten-year ranges; Nordnet alone at the 44th), company news sets where in the band Avanza sits (a discount to Nordnet of about 3 turns), and in the last four months company news has mattered more than the sector, which is both an opportunity (delivery can re-rate the stock without the sector) and a warning (the sector's recovery would not lift it one for one).

The evidence, read carefully. The basket is a poor instrument for "the industry" because it mixes two businesses: asset-gathering platforms (Nordnet, AJ Bell, Fineco, Integrafin, flatexDEGIRO) and leveraged-trading brokers (IG, Plus500, XTB, Swissquote in part). Avanza's correlation with the first group is 0.25 to 0.74 and with the second 0.12 to 0.21, so the 31% basket R-squared understates the share of Avanza's moves that the relevant peers explain and the 55% Nordnet R-squared is the better single measure. On that measure, roughly half of Avanza's daily variance is "sector" (the Nordic platform pair) and half is its own, and much of the "own" half is Swedish macro that the basket does not hold (the Riksbank's turn from cuts to a hike while the ECB and the Bank of England cut; the OMX; the election). The fall in beta from above 1.0 through 2025 and H1 2026 to 0.66 now says Avanza no longer moves more than the sector for a given sector move; the fall in correlation says its drivers have diverged: the sector's second half of 2026 was flatexDEGIRO's downgrade, IG's and Swissquote's 14% to 24% falls and Nordnet's German-launch premium, none of which is Avanza's story, while Avanza's was a Q3 miss on yield, a Swedish rate hike in prospect and a Danish timetable.

The multiple's level: industry-driven. The dashboard's P/E percentiles against each stock's own ten-year range: Avanza 12, Integrafin 12, flatexDEGIRO 15, AJ Bell 20, Fineco 24, Nordnet 44, Swissquote 44, Plus500 81, XTB 83. Every asset-gathering platform except Nordnet sits in the bottom quarter of its history, and the first set's sector digest (platforms at the 20th percentile) says the same. The absolute multiples cluster: Avanza 16.2x, AJ Bell 15.2x, Fineco 15.3x, Integrafin 14.2x; the sector median 15.2x. That is a cohort priced as a rate loser and a risk-off loser after a run of H1 2026 beats that did not re-rate, which is a sector regime, not an Avanza verdict; Avanza's 16.2x is a point above the cohort median for a return on equity ten points above the cohort's. The two-year-forward multiple (16.7x on consensus; about 17x on our lower EPS) sits at the 19th percentile of five years and the 5th of two years, so against its own recent history the stock is as cheap as it has been since the 2022 to 2023 derating.

The multiple's position: company-driven. Inside the cohort, the one spread that is Avanza-specific is the discount to Nordnet (16.2x against 19.3x current; DNB Carnegie's 16.7x against 19.5x on 2027). The first set attributed it to growth (customers +8% against +12%; savings capital +21% against +28%), to the export story (Germany priced at the 44th percentile, Denmark unpriced), to buybacks (Nordnet SEK 1bn over eighteen months; Avanza none) and to the Q3 yield miss; the AlphaSense valuation report adds Deutsche Bank's view that Avanza's premium to the cohort (13% on 2027) is "materially lower than historical levels" and that the stock trades about 30% below its own ten-year average P/E. Those are company facts, and they will move on company dates: 22 January 2027 (the 2027 cost guide; the sector digest's test), 16 April (the first full-hike quarter), the H1 2027 Danish disclosures and the H2 2027 launch, and the April AGMs (the buyback question). The one sector date that acts on the Avanza-Nordnet spread is Nordnet's German cohort data (January and April 2027).

What this means for predictability and for the case. (1) Over the next six months the share price will respond to Avanza's own prints and to the Riksbank more than to the sector; a sector rally would carry it with a beta of about 0.7 and a sector sell-off would hurt it less than its peers. (2) The multiple's floor is set by the cohort (15x to 16x, the first set's trough), and the cohort's floor has held through a bad six months for the leveraged brokers; the multiple's ceiling inside 18 months is set by Nordnet's (about 19x to 20x) and would require Avanza to close part of the growth or export gap, which the Danish disclosures of 2027 are the only dated route to. (3) The idiosyncratic share rising to 69% is favourable for an investment case built on company delivery (the market is listening to Avanza) and unfavourable for one built on a sector re-rating (there is no sector re-rating in progress: flatexDEGIRO downgraded, revision momentum negative at IG, Plus500, Swissquote and Integrafin). (4) The co-movement with Nordnet (0.74) is the single most useful hedge and read-across: a strong Nordnet print on growth widens the discount; a weak one narrows it, as Nordnet's own 2% Q3 miss and DNB Carnegie's target cut to SEK 380 "on lower peer multiples" showed in the same week.

Net. About half of the price action is sector and half company, with the company half rising; the multiple's level is the industry's and its discount is the company's. For the Consumer that is neither clearly positive nor negative for predictability: the earnings path drives the discount, the sector drives the band, and the two next move on dates that are known.

Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

The Investment Analyst's heading The fourth set: the extent to which our assessment is reflected in guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative

The fourth set as a whole ❌ D45 written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The three layers the fourth set examines are inconsistent with one another, and the inconsistency is the finding. Guidance is a cost ceiling and a capital frame that the company sets to meet and has met for four years; it contains no earnings number, so it can neither be raised in the sense the market rewards nor reduced in the sense the market punishes, and our assessment differs from it by the 1% beat we expect and the Danish phasing that will make the January 2027 group cost figure look worse than the Swedish headline. The sell-side's numbers reflect the first half of 2026: FY2027 EPS of SEK 20.87 (mean 21.1) unchanged since a 2% miss with every revenue line below, a 2027 margin of 69.5%, ten of thirteen houses yet to publish, and a dashboard revision rating of A that measures the twelve months behind. Our base of SEK 20.2 and a 68.2% margin is 3% below, DNB Carnegie's is 7% below, and the cuts come at the two dates that matter, the Q4 compilation and the 22 January print, before the Q1 2027 report offers the first likely beat. The narrative in research knows every dynamic we carry and weights them differently (rates most, the 2027 margin least, the leverage ratio's loosening and the risk tax's irrelevance not at all), while the price, at the 12th percentile of its ten-year multiple range and 3 turns below Nordnet, has absorbed more of the bear case than the estimates have. So the gap between our assessment and what the market reflects is unfavourable against the estimates (the next two revision rounds are down), neutral against guidance (we expect what it says), and neutral to modestly favourable against the price (a franchise compounding assets at 10% to 11% with a 4% yield, a loosening balance sheet, an asymmetric rate lever, an unpriced Danish option and no risk-tax exposure in the horizon does not need a 12th-percentile multiple, and the first set's 17x to 20x fair range is 5% to 25% above it). For the memo: the case is better entered after the estimates have caught up with the Q3 run rate than before; the Financial Analyst's model, once its margin input is restated to its own revenue basis, gives our EPS and must not be read at the 27.70x guideline multiple, which belongs to a phase this stock is not in; and the single most useful thing the next six months can show is whether income per customer stabilises above SEK 2,100, because that is the number on which the sell-side's framing, the price's framing and our base case all converge or part.

Guidance

1 question
I-0150
I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events, and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, the mid-term targets.

C50

Question asked How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

Judgement. Avanza's guidance is a cost ceiling, a Danish budget, a capital frame and four 2030 targets; it contains no earnings number, so "raised guidance" in the sense the market rewards is structurally unavailable, and the only guidance event with upgrade content is a 2027 Swedish cost guide set below 2026's 9%. Over the seven reports in the next 18 months (22 January 2027 to April 2028) the most probable path is: the 2026 cost guide met and beaten by about 1% (probability about 85%); a 2027 Swedish cost guide of 7% to 8% (about 70%), which the market will read as the first visible step of the 8%-average framework and which is roughly what consensus already carries for Sweden; the Danish pre-launch budget held at SEK 120m to 150m (about 70%) with the post-launch running cost the more likely figure to drift up; a 2026 dividend of SEK 14.0 to 14.5 (payout 73% to 76%) against consensus SEK 14.52; no buyback (about 85%); and the 2030 targets reaffirmed in January 2027 and reframed, if at all, only at a capital markets day the company has not yet dated. The gap between our assessment and what guidance reflects is small and slightly favourable on costs (we expect a beat), neutral on capital, and unfavourable only on the SEK 2,000bn target, which the base misses by about 5% and which does not bear on earnings in the horizon.

The events and the guidance at stake. 4 November 2026: Riksbank (not guidance, but the input the rate sensitivity converts). 5 November, early December, early January: monthly statistics. About 14 January 2027: the company-compiled consensus for Q4. 22 January 2027: the preliminary FY2026 statement, the 2027 Swedish cost guide, the Danish 2027 figure, the dividend proposal, the AGM buyback authorisation request. 16 April 2027: Q1; late April: the AGM. July 2027: Q2. H2 2027: the Danish launch and its first disclosures. October 2027: Q3. January 2028: FY2027 and the 2028 cost guide. April 2028: Q1 2028.

The 2026 cost guide (9% Swedish growth plus SEK 40m Denmark, about SEK 1,580m): met, probably beaten. Nine-month costs were SEK 1,145m (Swedish growth about 8% to 9% year to date; H1 6.6%); Q4 is the seasonal peak (SEK 402m in Q4 2025, +20% year on year then), and the base carries SEK 430m, giving SEK 1,575m for the year, 0.3% under. DNB Carnegie flagged a possible Q4 cost spike with no spill into 2027; the record (beats of SEK 7m to 30m in four straight years; the Danish figure cut from SEK 50m to 40m in October) makes a beat of SEK 5m to 20m the central case and a miss (above SEK 1,590m) a one-in-ten event that would need a Q4 outage remediation, a bonus accrual or Danish consultants brought in late. Earnings effect either way: under 1%.

The 2027 Swedish cost guide (22 January): the one guidance event with signalling power. The framework is an 8% average for 2025 to 2030 and about 5% in 2030; 2025 delivered 10.4% and 2026 will deliver about 9%, so the remaining four years must average about 6.5% to meet the framework. Management has said 2025 and 2026 are the investment years, headcount growth will slow materially in 2027 (812 employees, +15% in a year, with a 4% salary round) and cloud spend runs at about SEK 50m a year to 2030. The base carries 7.5%. My distribution: at or below 7%, 25%; 7% to 8%, 45%; 8% to 9%, 25%; above 9%, 5%. What drives the low end: a hiring freeze in Sweden as the Danish team takes the incremental headcount, external services falling back after the migration, and the habit of guiding to beat. What drives the high end: the cloud migration's dual-running costs, outages forcing back-end remediation (the former staff interviewed say the Swedish stack was not built for current scale), payment commissions rising with volumes, and a volume boom (2021's precedent). How the market reads it: the sector digest names this guide as the re-rating test for the cohort (is the expansion bill absorbed inside the old cost growth rate?); DNB Carnegie models group costs +11.5% in 2027 including Denmark, consensus implies about SEK 1,748m (+9.5%), and the base is SEK 1,780m (+13%). So a 7.5% Swedish guide plus a Danish 2027 figure of about SEK 130m lands at the base, which is above consensus costs; the headline (Swedish growth down from 9% to 7.5%) is favourable and the total (group costs up 13%) is unfavourable, and brokers who have not modelled the Danish phasing will cut 2027 EBIT by 1% to 2% on the day. Guidance will therefore be "lowered" in the good sense and consensus costs raised; the net effect on estimates is slightly negative.

The Danish budget (SEK 120m to 150m pre-launch; SEK 80m running "and rising"; up to SEK 60m marketing for three years; break-even about five years after launch). Held at January and April 2027: about 70%; raised: 20%; cut: 10%. Evidence for held or cut: the 2026 figure was cut from SEK 50m to 40m by using internal staff rather than consultants; only SEK 13m was spent in Q3; the branch application is filed with the Swedish FSA and a country manager (Christian Sillesen, from Lunar and Saxo) started on 12 October; prototypes have been tested in Copenhagen. Evidence for a rise: the Danish FSA is strict (Lunar's regulatory orders), DORA's delayed Level 2 standards could lengthen the audit of an AI-built platform, the Aktiesparekonto tax engine and Pensionsinfo integration are unbuilt, and Jefferies' three-year total of SEK 340m to 380m is about SEK 60m to 80m above management's implied path. The figure most likely to move is not the pre-launch budget but the post-launch running cost ("SEK 80m and rising") once the Danish headcount is in place; the base's SEK 150m to 175m for 2028 to 2029 already allows for some of that. Earnings effect of a SEK 30m rise: under 1%.

The dividend and the buyback. The 2025 dividend was SEK 12.75 (76% payout). For 2026, consensus has SEK 14.52; the base has SEK 14.3 on a 75% payout of SEK 19.1; management's stated tool for surplus capital is the payout ratio and the leverage ratio has risen to 4.8% (headroom SEK 46bn, up from SEK 43bn in June), so a 75% to 78% payout (SEK 14.3 to 14.9) is plausible if Q4 is in line; a cut in the ratio toward 70% (SEK 13.4) would need a dash to cash that consumed headroom in Q4. Probability the proposal is at or above SEK 14.5: about 45%. The 10% buyback authorisation will be renewed at the April 2027 AGM (about 95%) and unused through April 2028 (about 85%); the second set's scenario of a first buyback from 2028, if the ratio reaches 5.2% to 5.5%, is a question the market will ask at the AGM, not an event in the horizon. Nordnet's SEK 250m programme in September 2026 keeps the question alive.

The 2030 targets, secondarily. SEK 2,000bn of savings capital (15% a year): missed in 2025 (13%), met in 2026 on a 21% market-assisted year, and in the base reached only to SEK 1,910bn; DNB Carnegie notes underlying net inflows contribute less than the 10% the target implies and Deutsche Bank says the 15% needs 6 to 7 points from share gains and wallet deepening; the target will be reaffirmed at every report through 2027 (management does not move targets) and is more likely to be reframed than dropped at the next capital markets day, which has not been announced and which I would expect in 2027 or 2028 once Denmark is live. The cost framework (8% average, 5% in 2030): likely met in Sweden; the group figure will run above it through 2029 because Denmark sits outside it. Return on equity at least 35%: met with 3 to 7 points to spare throughout. Payout at least 70%: exceeded. There is no mid-term earnings or revenue ambition to raise or cut.

What would make guidance a positive catalyst, and what would not. Positive: a 2027 Swedish guide at or below 7% with the Danish figure unchanged; a payout above 76%; a first explicit statement that efficiency gains from cloud or AI are being banked rather than reinvested (none to date); a Danish fee schedule that undercuts Nordnet's 25 basis point currency fee without a margin collapse. Negative: a Swedish guide above 8.5%; a Danish budget rise or a launch slipping into 2028; a payout cut toward 70%; a reframed 2030 capital target before Denmark delivers. None of these is probable enough to move the base; the central case is guidance met on every line the company guides, which the market already expects and does not pay for.

Net for the gap. Our assessment and current guidance are nearly the same thing on costs and capital, because guidance is where the second set took its inputs; we expect a small beat the market also expects. The gap is therefore close to neutral, a shade favourable on the cost record and a shade unfavourable on the group cost total that the January guide will make visible to brokers who carry Denmark light.
