Sell-side estimates

1 question
I-0236
I-02

How likely are consensus forecasts and the net breadth of revisions to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events, and why? Secondarily, 18 months to three years out.

❌ D36

Question asked How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

Judgement. Consensus for FY2026 and FY2027 is more likely to be cut than held or raised over the next two reports, by 2% to 4%, with the net breadth of revisions turning negative in the window from now to the Q4 report and staying mildly negative through the Q1 2027 report; thereafter the odds of a cut and a hold are roughly even and a raise needs a second rate hike or a return of H1 2026 trading. Over the full 18 months my probabilities for FY2027 EPS are: cut by more than 2%, 60%; held within plus or minus 2%, 30%; raised by more than 2%, 10%, with the central outcome a consensus of about SEK 20.0 to 20.5 by April 2028 against SEK 20.87 (median) and 21.1 (mean) today. For FY2028 and the one broker on FY2029 the cuts are larger in prospect (3% to 6%) because the Danish running cost and the second year of yield drift are under-modelled, and the bull end (Deutsche Bank's 14% EPS compound rate to 2028, Nordea's SEK 22.70 for 2027) has further to fall than the bear end has to rise. The dashboard's sell-side earnings momentum rating of A and its revision-momentum score of 10 are the last readings of a cycle that has peaked: the gap between our assessment and what the estimates reflect is unfavourable for the case over the next six months, and it is the price's job, not the estimates', to have discounted it.

Where consensus stands and what has moved since the Q3 print. FactSet median (13 October, before the print): EPS SEK 19.40, 20.87, 22.88; revenue SEK 5,225m, 5,726m, 6,216m; EBIT SEK 3,629m, 3,978m, 4,274m (69.5%, 69.5%, 68.8%); DPS SEK 14.52, 15.41, 16.93; 11 to 13 brokers. FactSet mean FY2027 EPS 21.14 on 9 October and 21.12 on 22 October: unchanged after a 2% operating profit miss with every revenue line below. Only three houses have published since 16 October: DNB Carnegie cut FY2026 to SEK 18.90 from 19.04 and FY2028 to 20.79 from 20.93, held FY2027 at 19.29, trimmed the target to SEK 425 from 430 and trimmed 2027 to 2028 brokerage by 3%; Deutsche Bank held Buy and SEK 440 with FY2027 EPS SEK 22.85 (the top of the range); Jefferies held Underperform and SEK 335. Ten houses (Pareto SEK 450, Barclays 420, Nordea 465, UBS 415, Goldman Sachs 430, Keefe Bruyette & Woods 370, two restricted Buys at 450 and 460, a restricted Hold at 408 and a restricted Sell at 360) are on pre-print numbers; Jefferies' and KBW's estimates date from July. The buy share is 50% (up 3 points in three months), the mean target SEK 416 (down 1% in a month), FY2027 EPS up 13% over twelve months and 1% over one. The sell-side revision counts therefore describe the twelve months to the Q3 print, not the quarter after it.

Our base against consensus, line by line, and what the lines imply for the direction of revisions. Revenue SEK 5,165m, 5,600m, 6,025m against 5,225m, 5,726m, 6,216m (minus 1%, minus 2%, minus 3%): the gap is in brokerage (SEK 1,350m against 1,394m for 2027) and currency (810 against 839), partly offset by fund commissions in line and NII (2,130 against 2,197) slightly below because the base takes one hike and consensus appears to take the forward curve. Costs SEK 1,575m, 1,780m, 1,910m against about 1,596m, 1,748m, 1,942m implied: consensus is right for 2026 and about SEK 30m to 80m light for 2027 on the Danish phasing. Operating margin 69.5%, 68.2%, 68.3% against 69.5%, 69.5%, 68.8%: the 2027 margin is the line most likely to be cut (1.3 points, about 2% of EBIT). EPS SEK 19.1, 20.2, 21.7 against 19.40, 20.87, 22.88 (minus 1.5%, minus 3%, minus 5%). DNB Carnegie (18.90, 19.29, 20.79) is below us on every line; Deutsche Bank (20.98, 22.85, 24.47) above on every line; the base sits between, nearer DNB Carnegie.

The revision path by event.

  • Now to the Q4 compilation (about 14 January 2027). The ten lagging houses incorporate Q3's run rate (operating income SEK 1,283m, brokerage yield 10.3 basis points) and the October to December statistics (September trades minus 10% year on year; DNB Carnegie expects lower velocity after October's turbulence); the Riksbank's 4 November hike is already in most 2027 NII numbers (consensus NII +17% for 2027 requires it), so it confirms rather than lifts; a hold would cut 2027 EPS by 3% to 4% and would be the one event that turns the whole cohort's revisions negative at once. Expected: FY2026 to about SEK 19.1 to 19.2 (minus 1% to 1.5%), FY2027 to about 20.3 to 20.5 (minus 2% to 3%); breadth negative (eight to ten cuts, one or two holds, no raises).
  • 22 January 2027 (Q4 print). Consensus implies Q4 EPS of about SEK 4.80 (19.40 less 14.58 for nine months); the base has about SEK 4.55 (income SEK 1,290m; costs 430m; margin 66.7%). If the base is right the print is a 5% miss on the quarter that lands after the pre-compilation trims as a 2% to 3% miss; the 2027 Swedish cost guide of 7% to 8% is in line with what brokers carry for Sweden, and the Danish 2027 figure (about SEK 130m) is above what most carry, so 2027 EBIT estimates fall another 1% to 2%; the dividend (SEK 14.3 against 14.52) is a small negative. Breadth negative. The offset: NII with two months of the hike at about SEK 480m against a Q3 of 464m is the one line that can beat, and a payout at 76% would please.
  • 16 April 2027 (Q1 print). The second set's quarterly shape makes this the likeliest beat of the horizon: a full quarter of the hike, an easy cost comparison (Q1 2026 costs SEK 378m), and a Q1 that is seasonally strong for activity; base EPS about SEK 5.1 against a consensus that will by then be about SEK 5.0 to 5.1. Breadth turns neutral to slightly positive; if a second hike has arrived in February (Nordea's call) 2027 NII estimates rise 3% to 4% and FY2027 is held or nudged up.
  • July 2027 (Q2). The comparison is the Q2 2026 peak (operating income SEK 1,334m); the base has growth of 4.6%; the likeliest miss after January; breadth negative again unless volatility has returned.
  • October 2027 and January 2028. Danish launch costs in H2 2027 (the Q4 2027 margin of 66.3% is the horizon's lowest); the 2028 cost guide; FY2028 estimates (SEK 22.88) cut toward SEK 21.5 to 22.0 as the Danish running cost of SEK 150m and the 2028 yield drift are modelled. Net over the 18 months: FY2027 consensus down 3% to 5% from today, concentrated in the first two events; FY2028 down 4% to 6%; the buy share drifting from 50% toward 40% to 45% as targets (median SEK 430) are trimmed toward SEK 400 to 410; the dashboard's pinned A falling to B or C by the spring.

What would make consensus rise instead, and the odds. (1) A return of H1 2026 trading velocity: DNB Carnegie values it at 17% of 2027 EPS; the 2022 to 2023 precedent (two years of lower activity after the 2021 peak) and the September data make it a 20% to 25% event inside twelve months. (2) A second hike by February 2027 and STIBOR at 2.4% to 2.5% (Deutsche Bank's Riksbank path): worth 3% to 4% of 2027 EPS; perhaps 40%. (3) Deposits rebuilding to 9.5% to 10% of capital in a risk-off winter: 1% to 2%; perhaps 35%. (4) A 2027 Swedish cost guide at or below 7%: about 1%; 25%. For consensus to be raised on net by more than 2%, (1) or two of (2) to (4) must happen together before the Danish costs are modelled: about 10%.

18 months to three years (FY2028 to FY2029). Consensus FY2028 EPS SEK 22.88 (13 brokers) against the base's 21.7; FY2029 SEK 24.60 (one broker) against 23.3. The structural gap is the revenue margin (consensus implies about 42 to 43 basis points in 2028 against our 40.6) and the Danish running cost; the offsets are the loosening leverage ratio (a first buyback in 2028 would add about 1.5% once) and the possibility that the 2030 cost cap pulls forward. Cuts of 3% to 6% are the central case; the compound rate the median carries (11.4% a year FY2025 to FY2028) will settle toward 9% to 10%. Deutsche Bank's 14% and 16% savings-capital compound rates are the numbers with the furthest to fall; DNB Carnegie's are already below ours.

Read-across from peers. The sector's Q3 was softer than its H1 everywhere: Nordnet missed by 2% on adjusted operating profit and DNB Carnegie cut its target to SEK 380 on lower peer multiples; flatexDEGIRO missed on the top line with guidance unchanged and was downgraded by Deutsche Bank on 12 October; Fineco's and AJ Bell's FY2027 consensus rose 3% and 7% over three and six months (diversified fee models); the dashboard's revision-momentum scores are 12 at Nordnet and Fineco, 11 at AJ Bell, 0 at flatexDEGIRO and negative at IG, Plus500, Swissquote and Integrafin. Avanza's 10 puts it with the asset gatherers today; the Q3 miss and the lagging brokers put it on the way to the middle.

Net for the gap. The estimates reflect the first-half 2026 Avanza; our assessment reflects the third-quarter one; the distance is 3% on 2027 EPS and 1.3 points on the 2027 margin, and it closes through cuts over the next two prints. For an investment case this is the unfavourable layer, and it argues for timing (after 22 January 2027) rather than against the case.

Stock market narrative

1 question
I-0346
I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of sell-side and investor commentary; how well known is each?

❌ D46

Question asked How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

Judgement. Every dynamic in our assessment is known to the sell-side houses whose research we can see, and none of them is reflected in those houses' aggregate numbers in the way our assessment reflects them: the narrative has absorbed the words (yield compression, a fixed cost base, a Danish bill, the Nordnet growth gap, the rate lever) and not the arithmetic (2027 EPS growth of about 6%, an operating margin falling to 68%, consensus 3% too high). The split is the story: the bears (Jefferies, and the restricted Sell at SEK 360) articulate the decoupling of asset growth from earnings as a structural fact and are a quarter of the covering houses; the bulls (Deutsche Bank, Nordea, Pareto, the two restricted Buys at SEK 450 to 460) frame the stock as a compounder whose next move depends on the Riksbank and on retail re-engaging; DNB Carnegie, the house closest to our view, calls the Q3 softness seasonal and says yields will not return to peak, so growth must come from assets, which is our frame with a kinder number. The share price, at the 12th percentile of its ten-year multiple range with a technical momentum rating of D, has absorbed more of the bear framing than the sell-side's numbers have, which is why the gap between our assessment and the narrative is unfavourable against the estimates and roughly neutral against the price. On investor commentary (X, the Placera and Avanza forums, Redeye, Börsvärlden, Dagens Industri, Affärsvärlden) the research found nothing, so how well the dynamics are known to retail and domestic institutional investors is an evidence gap, not a finding; the AlphaSense coverage covers four houses (DNB Carnegie, Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, Morgan Stanley) of the thirteen that publish, so "well known to the sell-side" is a judgement on about a third of it.

The ten dynamics, who articulates each, and how far the articulation matches our assessment.

  1. The fall in revenue per unit (brokerage 10.3, fund 22.2, currency 18 basis points; income per customer SEK 2,170). Universally discussed since 16 October. Management: "temporary market sentiment", with record fund inflows as the counter-evidence. DNB Carnegie (19 October): mostly seasonal, but Private Banking competition "a concern" and yields "unlikely to return to historical peaks". Deutsche Bank (30 September, 16 October): brokerage yield stable around 11 basis points since 2018, so the fall is transient mix; volume will overwhelm yield. Jefferies (16 October): permanently lower income per customer; revenue decouples from assets. Morgan Stanley (2023 to 2024, no longer actively publishing on the name in the files): a 45 basis point platform yield unsustainable. Our two-thirds-structural split is articulated by nobody in those words; DNB Carnegie's flat 10.6 basis point yield and Jefferies' 0.5 basis point cut are its nearest neighbours. Well known as a debate; not settled; the market's price leans to Jefferies, the estimates to Deutsche Bank.
  2. The fixed-cost structure and the 2027 cost and margin outlook. Discussed by all four as "9% in 2026, 8% average, slowing hiring"; DNB Carnegie models 2027 group costs +11.5%; Deutsche Bank and Jefferies carry the Danish budget but not the phasing; none writes the 2027 margin as the line that disappoints. Known in parts; the consequence (margin minus 1.3 points in 2027) is not in the narrative.
  3. The asymmetric rate sensitivity and the November hike. Universally discussed and the most prominent single theme: DNB Carnegie's "4% EPS upside on the forward curve", Deutsche Bank's "most powerful near-term catalyst", Nordea's two-hike call, Morgan Stanley's hold-through-2027. Fully known; arguably over-weighted relative to the deposit-beta history, which only the own-question report surfaced (36% cumulative beta in 2023; realised sensitivity a fraction of published).
  4. The deposit and risk-appetite loop and the leverage ratio (8.9% of capital; 4.8% against 3.5%; the mortgage pause). Deutsche Bank: the leverage ratio is "the governing constraint" on buybacks; Jefferies: the pause protects the ratio, no buybacks expected; DNB Carnegie (2025): an extraordinary dividend of SEK 5 would be possible. The loop is known as a constraint; the second set's reading that it loosens from 2027 and opens a buyback option in 2028 is articulated by no one.
  5. The passive-mix shift (index funds 55.5%; own funds 41% to 42%) and the RIS value-for-money tests. Deutsche Bank ("an asset-growth story where volume compensates for yield"), DNB Carnegie (32% of Swedish fund flows; index funds squeezing margins), Morgan Stanley (2023, the inducement-ban risk). Well known; the own-fund buffer and Nordnet's 50% own-fund inflow share are noted by DNB Carnegie.
  6. The Danish entry (budget, separate AI-built platform, Nordnet as price-setter at 49 to 50 basis points, five years to break-even). Discussed by all four since April 2026, as a cost and an option, never as revenue: Jefferies ("a bigger chance on a smaller prize"; SEK 340m to 380m over three years; downside margin 60%, target SEK 260 in that case), DNB Carnegie ("dual IT platforms seem suboptimal"; five years realistic), Deutsche Bank ("long-term geographic optionality"; a repeatable template). No house models Danish revenue or customers; management discloses no pricing. Fully known as a debate; unpriced in both directions; the H1 2027 disclosures are the first information.
  7. The relative growth gap to Nordnet (customers +8% against +12%; capital +21% against +28%; buybacks). Deutsche Bank, Jefferies and DNB Carnegie all carry it, with the multiple discount (16.7x against 19.5x on 2027) attributed to it. Well known and priced.
  8. Sigma (SEK 710m) and occupational pensions (premium share 10.7%; the transfer petition). DNB Carnegie ("margin accretive as it scales; adoption gradual"), Jefferies ("a major revenue catalyst"; conservative near term), Deutsche Bank (Private Banking the clearest route to wallet share). Known as options; the 2027 price step from 0.55% to 0.85% and its retention risk are noted only in the own-question research. Not in any model.
  9. The risk tax on credit institutions. Discussed by no covering house as an Avanza matter; DNB Carnegie's bank research gives the threshold (SEK 197bn) and Avanza's liabilities (SEK 114bn); the narrative report's claim that liabilities "approach the SEK 150bn threshold" is wrong on the current threshold. Correctly ignored, as D-01 concluded.
  10. Platform reliability and the Google Cloud migration. Discussed only in expert calls (former Avanza staff on outages under load; a Nordea technologist on incumbents closing the gap) and in DNB Carnegie's note that cloud cost is "a key risk"; no uptime data; no house carries it as a thesis variable. A dynamic our assessment carries and the narrative does not: the quarterly shape of 2027 (Q1 the likeliest beat; Q2 against the peak comparison; Q4 carrying the Danish launch), which determines the sequence of revisions and is articulated nowhere.

The question that most needed answering: has the narrative absorbed the decoupling of asset growth from earnings growth? In two of three layers, partly. The sell-side's words have (Jefferies entirely; DNB Carnegie as "growth must come from assets"; Deutsche Bank as "an asset-growth story"), its numbers have not (FY2027 EPS unchanged after the print; the 2027 margin held at 69.5%). The price has: a current P/E of 16.2x at the 12th percentile, a 24-month-forward 16.7x at the 5th percentile of two years, a six-month technical reading of D and a 12% fall from the 52-week high are what a stock looks like when investors have stopped paying for the growth the estimates still carry. The third layer, investor commentary, is unobserved in the files. On balance the stock is still framed in sell-side research as a rate and volume play ("17% upside if H1 activity returns"; "4% on STIBOR"), and that framing is what makes the next two prints risky for the estimates and not for the price.

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out. The narrative for 2028 to 2029 is Denmark (as cost and option), the 2030 target's feasibility (Deutsche Bank: 6 to 7 points of the 15% must come from share gains; DNB Carnegie: underlying inflows below 10%), the cost cap stepping to 5%, and the leverage ratio as a buyback gate; the structural yield drift to about 38 basis points by 2030 is Morgan Stanley's 2023 to 2024 view and no current house's base; the loosening of the leverage ratio as a positive is nobody's. Our assessment is more cautious than the sell-side on the 2028 to 2029 margin and more constructive than the price on the franchise's durability; the Danish milestones of 2027 to 2028 are the only dated events that can move either.

Net for the gap. The dynamics are well known qualitatively to the houses we can see, unevenly weighted (rates over-weighted, the 2027 margin under-weighted, the leverage-ratio loosening and the risk-tax non-issue unknown), and not yet in the aggregate numbers; the price has run ahead of the estimates in absorbing them. Against the estimates the gap is unfavourable; against the price it is neutral to slightly favourable; against investor commentary it is unknown, and that is the memo's research gap.

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Avanza Bank Holding AB (AZA-SE), Sweden's largest digital savings and investment platform: 2.39 million retail, Private Banking and Pro customers and SEK 1,277bn of savings capital (30 September 2026) in one reported segment (Savings and Investments) across three regulated entities (Avanza Bank, Avanza Pension, Avanza Fonder); FY2025 operating income of SEK 4,495m split net interest income 33%, net brokerage 28%, fund commissions 17%, currency-related income 16%, other 6%; organic entry into Denmark planned for the second half of 2027.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Avanza Bank Holding AB (AZA-SE), what have investors and the Swedish financial press said about the company since its third-quarter 2026 report of 16 October 2026 (operating income SEK 1,283m against the company-compiled consensus of SEK 1,310m; brokerage yield 10.3 basis points; income per customer SEK 2,170 annualised; record fund inflows of SEK 18bn), and what have the covering brokers whose research is not held in the earlier reports (Pareto Securities, Nordea Equity Research, Barclays, UBS, Goldman Sachs, Keefe Bruyette & Woods, SEB, Handelsbanken, ABG Sundal Collier, Carnegie before its merger with DNB) published on the company since that date: on X (Twitter), the Placera and Avanza forums, Redeye, Börsvärlden, Dagens Industri and Affärsvärlden, and in those brokers' notes, which themes dominate (the fall in revenue per unit and whether it is cyclical or structural; the 2027 cost outlook and the Danish budget; the Riksbank decision of 4 November 2026; the growth gap to Nordnet; the dividend and the unused buyback authorisation; Avanza Sigma), with what view, how prominently and since when; how the share price reacted on 16 and 17 October 2026 and around Nordnet's third-quarter report of 22 October 2026; what the brokers' post-report estimate and target-price changes were; and how retail and institutional positioning in the share (holdings of AMF, Creades, the Hagströmer family, Vanguard and SEB; any short interest data) has changed over 2026?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For Avanza Bank Holding AB (AZA-SE), what is the evidence that competition for affluent and active Swedish customers is compressing platform pricing: what do SAVR, Montrose, Nordnet (including its 15 basis point currency-exchange campaign, its commission-free anniversary weeks and its private-banking joint venture with ABG Sundal Collier announced on 15 September 2026 for a 2027 launch), Interactive Brokers (which now offers Swedish ISK accounts) and Levler charge affluent and high-frequency clients for Swedish and foreign equity trading, currency conversion, fund distribution and discretionary management, against Avanza's Private Banking and Pro terms (manual currency account at 12.5 basis points against a 25 basis point standard; flexible fee classes introduced in late 2025; Avanza Sigma at 0.55% to 0.85% launched 31 August 2026 with SEK 710m after one month); what evidence exists of Private Banking or Pro customers or assets moving between these providers in 2025 and 2026; how Avanza Sigma's early adopters have responded to the scheduled move from the promotional 0.55% fee to the 0.85% base fee in 2027; what former Avanza, SAVR, Montrose and Nordnet staff say about the drivers of the brokerage yield's fall to 10.3 basis points in the third quarter of 2026 from 11.0 in the second; and how Hargreaves Lansdown, AJ Bell and Interactive Investor priced and defended their affluent tiers when challengers targeted them?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For Avanza Bank Holding AB (AZA-SE), how has the twelve-month-forward and twenty-four-month-forward price-to-earnings multiple, and its premium or discount to Nordnet (SAVE-SE), to the European online broker and platform cohort (FinecoBank, AJ Bell, Integrafin, flatexDEGIRO, Swissquote) and to the OMX Stockholm 30, moved through each earnings phase since 2015: the 2016 to 2019 period of flat earnings at zero policy rates, the 2020 to 2021 retail trading boom, the 2022 earnings decline (EPS SEK 10.69 from SEK 13.19), the 2023 rate-driven recovery, the 2024 to 2025 Riksbank cutting cycle, and 2026 to date; at what multiple the stock traded at the earnings troughs of 2019 and 2022 to 2023 and at the earnings peaks of 2021 and the first half of 2026; what the multiple did in the six and twelve months after each quarter in which operating profit missed the company-compiled consensus (including the first quarter of 2023 and the third quarter of 2026); what re-rated the stock in 2019 to 2020 and in 2023; and what the sell-side and the financial press identified as the catalysts and the phase of the stock at each turn?
